Monomial bialgebras Thanks: This work was partially supported by the Simons Foundation Collaboration Grant no. 636972 (A. Berenstein), the Simons foundation collaboration grant no. 245735 (J. Greenstein), and Austrian Science Fund (FWF): P 34602, Grant DOI: 10.55776/P34602, and PAT 9039323, Grant-DOI 10.55776/PAT9039323 (J.-R. Li).
Arkady Berenstein, Jacob Greenstein, and Jian-Rong Li

Abstract
. Starting from a single solution of QYBE (or CYBE) we produce an infinite family of solutions of QYBE (or CYBE) parametrized by transitive arrays and, in particular, by signed permutations. We are especially interested in cases when such solutions yield quasi-triangular structures on direct powers of Lie bialgebras and tensor powers of Hopf algebras. We obtain infinite families of such structures as well and study the corresponding Poisson-Lie structures and co-quasi-triangular algebras.



1.
Introduction

Classical and quantum Yang Baxter equations (CYBE and QYBE, respectively) play a fundamental role in representation theory, low-dimensional topology and mathematical physics,
especially in the theory of integrable
systems and statistical mechanics. This makes the construction of new solutions of CYBE and QYBE quite important.

In the present work, starting from
a single solution of CYBE (QYBE), we produce an infinite family of solutions of CYBE (QYBE) parametrized by
transitive arrays
and, in particular, by signed permutations. More precisely, we start with a quasi-triangular Lie
bialgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
with a classical r-matrix
r\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
.
As customary, let
U({\mathfrak{l}})
be the universal enveloping algebra of a Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{l}}
, and it is convenient to identify
{\mathfrak{l}}
as the subspace (of all primitive elements)
inside the Hopf algebra
U({\mathfrak{l}})
.
Then
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes n}
,
n\geq 1
, is naturally isomorphic to
U({\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n})
. Given
x=\sum_{t}a_{t}\otimes b_{t}\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
and
1\leq i<j\leq m
, denote

(1.1)

x_{i,j}=\sum_{t}1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes a_{t}\otimes 1^{\otimes(j-i-1)}\otimes b_{t}\otimes 1^{\otimes(m-j)}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes m}

and set
x_{j,i}=(\tau(x))_{i,j}
where
\tau
is the permutation of factors.
For
any
n\times n
-matrix
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
with entries in
\{1,-1\}

define

\mathbf{r}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}\subset U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 2n}

by

(1.2)

\mathbf{r}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}r_{j,i+n}^{(\epsilon_{i,j})}

where
r^{(1)}=r
,
r^{(-1)}=-r_{2,1}
.
A remarkable result
[
27
]
*Theorem 6.2 can
be restated as follows to justify the notation.

Theorem 1.1

(
[
27
]
*Theorem 6.2 and Remark 6.4)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}

be a quasi-triangular Lie bialgebra with a classical r-matrix
r
and let
n\geq 2
. Then,
for any
w\in S_{n}
and
\boldsymbol{d}\in\{1,-1\}^{n}
,

{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
is a quasi-triangular Lie bialgebra with the classical r-matrix
\mathbf{r}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w,\boldsymbol{d}))}
where

(1.3)

\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w,\boldsymbol{d})_{i,j}=\delta_{i,j}d_{i}+\sign(w(j)-w(i)),\qquad 1\leq i,j\leq n.

By
[
33
]
, under the additional assumption that
r+r_{2,1}\in S^{2}({\mathfrak{g}})
is non-degenerate,

{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}
with
the r-matrix corresponding to
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(\id,(1,1))

is isomorphic to the Drinfeld double of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The Lie bracket dual to the Lie cobracket
corresponding to
\mathbf{r}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(\id,(1,\dots,1)))}

for arbitrary
n
was constructed in
[
16
]
*Proposition 1.9.

It turns out that matrices

\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w,\boldsymbol{d})
,
w\in S_{n}

and
\boldsymbol{d}\in\{1,-1\}^{n}
have a special combinatorial property. In the spirit of
[
4
,
3
]
, we say that an
n\times n
-matrix
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{i,j})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}

with entries in some set
C
is
transitive
if

a_{i,k}\in\{a_{i,j},a_{j,k}\}
for all
1\leq i,j,k\leq n
. By Lemma
3.2
, every transitive
n\times n
matrix
\boldsymbol{a}

with entries
a_{i,j}\in\{1,-1\}
,
1\leq i,j\leq n
which is
almost skew-symmetric
,
that is,
a_{j,i}=-a_{i,j}
for all
1\leq i<j\leq n
,
is equal to
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w,\boldsymbol{d})

for some unique
w\in S_{n}
and
\boldsymbol{d}\in\{1,-1\}^{n}
.
In particular, there are
2^{n}n!
of such matrices.
More generally, transitive
n\times n
matrices
with entries in
\{1,-1\}
naturally identify with

bitransitive
relations (
[
18
]
) on the set
\{1,\dots,n\}
, and their numbers

\{B_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
are given by the sequence
A004123
in
[
34
]
, the first few terms being
2
,
10
,
74
,
730
,
9002
,
133210,\dots
(see
[
18
,
24
,
39
]
and §
3.3
). By
[
18
]
*Proposition 4

(1.4)

\frac{B_{n}}{2^{n}n!}=O(\lambda^{n}),\qquad n\to\infty,

where
\lambda=(2\log(\frac{3}{2}))^{-1}>\frac{6}{5}
.
The following is an ultimate justification of our notion of transitivity.

Conjecture 1.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
be a
transitive
n\times n
matrix with entries in
\{1,-1\}

and let
r\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}

be a solution of CYBE.
Then
\mathbf{r}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}
solves CYBE.

We verified this conjecture for
n\leq 4
and, by the above, it holds for all transitive matrices with entries in
\{1,-1\}
which are almost skew-symmetric.
We expect that, for a generic
r
, this exhausts all solutions of CYBE of the form (
1.2
). It should be noted that the symmetric group
S_{n}
acts
naturally on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
, and each r-matrix
provided by Theorem
1.1
is equivalent
to
\mathbf{r}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(\id,\boldsymbol{d}))}
for some
\boldsymbol{d}\in\{1,-1\}^{n}
, while
bialgebra structures on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
given by these r-matrices are all isomorphic.
This is no longer the case for a general transitive
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
. While the class of r-matrices provided by Conjecture
1.2
is closed under the action of
S_{n}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
, by (
1.4
) the number of
S_{n}
-orbits grows exponentially faster than
2^{n}
.

Theorem
1.1
can be recovered as a special case of a much more general Theorem
1.3
, there the role of transitivity, albeit of a slightly different kind, becomes even more prominent.
Namely, generalizing the basic family
\{r^{(1)},r^{(-1)}\}=\{r,-r_{2,1}\}
, let

\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
be a
family of r-matrices
corresponding to the
same
Lie cobracket on
{\mathfrak{g}}
. By Proposition
2.11
, such a family solves
the
transitive CYBE
in
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 3}
, which appears to be new, namely

(1.5)

[r_{1,2}^{(c)},r_{1,3}^{(c^{\prime})}]+[r_{1,2}^{(c)},r_{2,3}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}]+[r_{1,3}^{(c^{\prime})},r_{2,3}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}]=0

whenever
c^{\prime}\in\{c,c^{\prime\prime}\}\subset C
. The ordinary CYBE corresponds to the
case when
c=c^{\prime}=c^{\prime\prime}
.

Given an upper triangular
n
-array
\mathbf{c}=(c_{i,j})_{1\leq i<j\leq n}
with entries in a set
C
,

\boldsymbol{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\in C^{n}
and
\mathbf{r}=\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
, define

(1.6)

\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{d}):=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}(r^{(d_{i})})_{i,i+n}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(r^{(c_{i,j})})_{j,i+n}-(r^{(c_{i,j})})_{j+n,i}.

We say that an upper triangular
n
-array
\mathbf{c}=(c_{i,j})_{1\leq i<j\leq n}
is
transitive
(cf.
[
4
,
3
]
)
if
c_{i,k}\in\{c_{i,j},c_{j,k}\}
for all
1\leq i<j<k\leq n
. Our main result in the classical case is

Main Theorem 1.3
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a quasi-triangular Lie bialgebra with a family
\mathbf{r}=\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}
of r-matrices corresponding to the same Lie cobracket.
Then for
any
\boldsymbol{d}\in C^{n}
and
any transitive
n
-array
\mathbf{c}
with entries in
C
,

{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
is a quasi-triangular Lie bialgebra with the
r
-matrix
\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{d})
, that is,

\tilde{\delta}_{\mathbf{c}}:{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}\to{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
defined by

\tilde{\delta}_{\mathbf{c}}(x)=[\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{d}),1\otimes x+x\otimes 1],\qquad x\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}

is a Lie cobracket and
\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{d})
solves the CYBE.

It should be noted that
\tilde{\delta}_{\mathbf{c}}
,
unlike
\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{d})
, does not
depend on
\boldsymbol{d}
, hence the notation.
We prove this Theorem in §
4.3
by constructing, for each transitive

n
-array
\mathbf{c}
, a
classical Drinfeld twist

j_{\mathbf{c}}(\mathbf{r})
which deforms the natural Lie bialgebra structure
on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
to
\tilde{\delta}_{\mathbf{c}}
. Classical twists were defined by
Drinfeld in the foundational work
[
13
]
and studied in
[
1
,
14
,
23
,
25
,
27
]

to name but a few. We proceed by induction on
n
,
and the main ingredient in the inductive step is
a generalization of classical Drinfeld twist which
we refer to as a
relative classical twist
for a pair
of Lie bialgebras (see §
2.7
).
Another important tool is
a “transitive version”
{\mathfrak{qtr}}_{n}(C)
, which we introduce in §
4.1
, of the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{qtr}}_{n}
defined in
[
5
]
.

As shown in
[
4
]
(cf. Lemma
3.1
for the precise statement), transitive
n
-arrays
with entries in
\{1,-1\}

are
in a natural bijection with
S_{n}

via

(1.7)

w\mapsto\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w):=(\sign(w(j)-w(i)))_{1\leq i<j\leq n},\quad w\in S_{n}.

Theorem
1.1
is a special case of
Theorem
1.3
for
\mathbf{r}=\{r^{(1)},r^{(-1)}\}=\{r,-r_{2,1}\}
and
\mathbf{c}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)
(see Remark
4.7
).

If
C
is finite, the number of transitive
n
-arrays with entries in
C

equals
p_{n}(|C|)
where

(1.8)

p_{n}(x)=\sum_{1\leq k\leq n-1}p_{n,k}\,x(x-1)\cdots(x-k+1)

with
p_{n,k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,
1\leq k\leq n-1
(
[
3
]
*Proposition 2.13, see also Corollary
3.12
). In particular,

p_{n,1}=1
,
p_{n,2}=\frac{1}{2}n!-1
, whence
p_{n}(2)=n!
, and, by
[
4
]
*Theorem 2.17,
p_{n,n-1}
is the
(n-1)
th Catalan
number. The coefficient
p_{n,n-2}
is given in Conjecture
3.13
.
We discuss this polynomiality phenomenon in much greater generality in §
3.2
.

To take the full advantage of Theorem
1.3
, we pose the following

Question 1.4
.

Which quasi-triangular Lie bialgebras admit non-trivial families of classical r-matrices
\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}
with
|C|>2
?

We answer this question when the underlying Lie algebra is of the form
{\mathfrak{t}}={\mathfrak{t}}(V,{\mathfrak{g}}):=V\rtimes{\mathfrak{g}}
, where
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a Lie algebra and
V
is
(a quotient of) the adjoint
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ad}
. Lie algebras of this type appeared in various contexts (see e.g.
[
9
,
2
,
8
,
10
,
22
,
32
,
36
]
). Namely, suppose that
{\mathfrak{g}}
is quasi-triangular with an r-matrix
r
and fix a surjective homomorphism of
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules
f:{\mathfrak{g}}_{\ad}\to V
.
We prove (Proposition
6.5
) that

{\mathfrak{t}}
is quasi-triangular with the r-matrix

\widehat{r}:=(f\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes f)(r)
, which seems to be new. Finally, let
\Omega
be a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-invariant in
V\otimes V
(for example, if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple then
\Omega
is the image of the Casimir element in
{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
) and
suppose that
r
is skew-symmetric. Then
\{\widehat{r}^{(c)}\}_{c\in\mathbb{k}}
where
\widehat{r}^{(c)}=\widehat{r}+c\,\Omega

provides an answer to Question
1.4

(Proposition
6.5
).

One consequence of our construction is the following observation. The diagonal embedding of Lie algebras
{\mathfrak{g}}\hookrightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
is not, generally speaking, a homomorphism of Lie bialgebras for the direct sum bialgebra structure. However, this Nature’s mistake
is rectified if
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
is regarded as
a Lie bialgebra with the cobracket
\tilde{\delta}_{\mathbf{c}}
(Theorem
4.10
).

The following is a natural generalization of Conjecture
1.2
, also verified for
n\leq 4
.

Conjecture 1.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{a}
be a
transitive
n\times n
matrix with entries in
C

and let
\mathbf{r}=\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}

be any solution of the transitive CYBE (
1.5
). Then

\mathbf{r}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}
solves CYBE.

Once
\mathbf{r}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}

satisfies CYBE, the necessary and sufficient
condition for it to be an r-matrix for
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
is provided by Lemma
4.11

and is reminiscent the almost skew-symmetry discussed above.

It is curious that the number of transitive
n\times n
matrices with entries
in a finite
C
is again a polynomial
q_{n}
in
|C|
of degree
n
which is dramatically easier to compute than
p_{n}
from (
1.8
). Namely,

q_{n}(x)=2x^{n}-x+(\frac{1}{2}B_{n}-2^{n}+1)x(x-1)

(see Proposition
3.14
and
Corollary
3.20
; in particular,
q_{n}(2)=B_{n}
as expected).
In general, under a very mild assumption on
C
and the family
\mathbf{r}
,

\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{d})
can be
written as
\mathbf{r}^{(\widehat{\mathbf{c}})}

where the diagonal of
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
is
\boldsymbol{d}
and the off-diagonal part is
obtained by a certain “skew-symmetrization” of
\mathbf{c}
.
Yet
\widehat{\mathbf{c}}
so obtained is not
a transitive
n\times n
-matrix and satisfies a rather different
combinatorial condition.

We conclude the discussion of the “classical story” with applications to Poisson geometry. Let
G

be an algebraic Poisson-Lie group whose Lie algebra is
the Lie bialgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Then its coordinate algebra
\mathbb{k}[G]
is Poisson
with the Poisson bracket induced by
the cobracket on
{\mathfrak{g}}
(see §
2.8
for details). In particular, any Lie bialgebra structure on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
yields a Poisson algebra structure on
\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}\cong\mathbb{k}[G^{\times n}]
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
\mathbf{r}=\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}
be
a family of r-matrices for a Lie bialgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, let
\mathbf{c}
be a transitive
n
-array with entries in
C
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>1}
, and let
G
be an algebraic Poisson-Lie group with the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Then
the diagonal embedding
G\hookrightarrow G^{\times n}
is Poisson, that is the multiplication
\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{k}[G]

is a homomorphism of Poisson algebras, where
the Poisson structure on
\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}
is induced by the cobracket
\tilde{\delta}_{\mathbf{c}}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
corresponding to
\mathbf{r}
and
\mathbf{c}
in the notation of Theorem
1.3
.

We provide a proof of Theorem
1.6
,
along with an explicit formula for the corresponding Poisson bracket

\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathbf{c}}
on
\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}
,
in §
4.5
.
As a first example (which we did not find in the literature), taking
G
to be the group
GL_{m}
or even the monoid
\Mat_{m}
and starting from the standard Poisson structure (
6.1
) on
\mathbb{k}[G]
, yields the Poisson structure (
6.2
) on

\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}
.

For
\mathbf{r}=\{r^{(1)},r^{(-1)}\}=\{r,-r_{21}\}
where
r
is an r-matrix for
{\mathfrak{g}}
, Poisson structures on
\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}

corresponding to
different transitive
n
-arrays are naturally isomorphic
(see Remark
6.2
). However, this is no longer the case if
|C|>2

(moreover, in the quantum case even when
C=\{1,-1\}
the corresponding quantum algebras need not be isomorphic, see Proposition
5.13

and §
6.1
). An example is provided by
Poisson algebra structures on tensor powers of
the coordinate algebra
\mathbb{k}[V\rtimes G]
of the
algebraic group
V\rtimes G
, where
V
is (a quotient of) the adjoint
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module.
As an algebra,
\mathbb{k}[V\rtimes G]
identifies with
\mathbb{k}[V]\otimes\mathbb{k}[G]
where
\mathbb{k}[G]
is
a Poisson-commutative Poisson ideal, while
\mathbb{k}[V]\cong S(V^{*})
is a Poisson subalgebra whose Poisson bracket extends the (non-abelian) Lie bracket
on
V^{*}
induced by the Lie cobracket
on
{\mathfrak{t}}=V\rtimes{\mathfrak{g}}
(see §
6.3
). It is quite involved even for
G=GL_{m}
and
r\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
belonging to the skew-symmetric family (
6.4
) which extends the
well-known skew-symmetric solution of CYBE for
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{2}

(cf. (
6.5
)). Since
{\mathfrak{t}}
,
as discussed above, admits an infinite family of non-equivalent r-matrices,
our construction yields a family (
6.6
) of
Poisson brackets on
\mathbb{k}[V\rtimes G]^{\otimes n}\cong\mathbb{k}[(V\rtimes G)^{\times n}]
parametrized
by transitive
n
-arrays with entries in
\mathbb{k}
which appear to be non-isomorphic
for
|C|>2
.
Quite remarkably, for
n>1
the natural image of
\mathbb{k}[V]^{\otimes n}
in
\mathbb{k}[V\rtimes G]^{\otimes n}

is no longer a Poisson subalgebra, while
\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}

remains a Poisson-commutative Poisson ideal.

We will now discuss the quantum case, which turns out
to be even more spectacular.
Let
H
be an associative algebra.
Given
w\in S_{n}

and an invertible
R
in (a suitable completion
H\widehat{\otimes}H
, see §
2.2
, of)
H\otimes H
, define

J_{w}=J_{w}(R)\in H^{\widehat{\otimes}2n}
by

J_{w}=(R_{2,n+1}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{1,2})})(R_{3,n+2}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{2,3})}R_{3,n+1}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{1,3})})\cdots(R_{n,2n-1}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{n-1,n})}\cdots R_{n,n+1}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{1,n})})

where
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)
is defined by (
1.7
) and

R_{i,j}^{(\epsilon)}=\begin{cases}R_{i,j},&\epsilon=1,\\
R_{j,i}^{-1},&\epsilon=-1.\end{cases}

The notation
R_{i,j}
has the same meaning as in (
1.1
) with
U({\mathfrak{g}})
now replaced by
H
.

Henceforth, let
H
be a bialgebra with the comultiplication
\Delta:H\to H\otimes H
. Then
H^{\otimes n}
is naturally a
bialgebra with the comultiplication

\Delta_{H^{\otimes n}}:H^{\otimes n}\to H^{\otimes n}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
determined by

\Delta_{H^{\otimes n}}(1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes h\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-i)})=\Delta(h)_{i,n+i},\qquad h\in H,\quad 1\leq i\leq n.

In the sequel, we consider quasi-triangular bialgebras, rather than Hopf algebras, as we do not need the antipode for our constructions.
The following is our first main result in the quantum case.

Main Theorem 1.7
.

Let
H
be a quasi-triangular bialgebra
with a (universal) R-matrix
R
and
let
w\in S_{n}
. Then

(a)

J_{w}=J_{w}(R)
is a Drinfeld twist for
\Delta_{H^{\otimes n}}
, hence

\Delta_{w}:H^{\otimes n}\to H^{\widehat{\otimes}2n}
defined by

\Delta_{w}(h)=J_{w}^{-1}\Delta_{H^{\otimes n}}(h)J_{w}
,
h\in H^{\otimes n}

equips

H^{\otimes n}
with a structure
of a (topological) bialgebra
H^{\otimes n,w}
;

(b)

For every
w\in S_{n}
,
\boldsymbol{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\in\{1,-1\}^{n}
,

\mathbf{R}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w),\boldsymbol{d}):=(J_{w}(R)^{op})^{-1}R_{1,n+1}^{(d_{1})}\cdots R_{n,2n}^{(d_{n})}\,J_{w}(R)

is an R-matrix for
H^{\otimes n,w}
, where for

X=X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{2n}\in H^{\otimes 2n}

in Sweedler-like notation we abbreviate
X^{op}:=X_{n+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{2n}\otimes X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{n}
.

This result was inspired by
[
33
]
*Theorem 2.9
for
n=2
and its generalization
[
30
]
*§6.2
for
w=\id\in S_{n}
.
Similarly to the classical setup, we prove Theorem
1.7
in §
5.2
as a special case of a more general result (Theorem
1.9
).
This result is highly non-trivial, since, unlike
the classical situation (Theorem
1.1
),
bialgebras corresponding to different
w\in S_{n}

do not have to be isomorphic (see for example §
6.4
). In particular, unlike
in the classical case,
there is no obvious connection between new classes of solutions of QYBE
provided by Theorem
1.7
(b)
for various
w\in S_{n}
.
Furthermore, similarly to Conjecture
1.2
, we expect a new large class of solution of QYBE as a non-commutative manifestation of transitivity.

Conjecture 1.8
.

Let
R
be any solution of QYBE. Then
for any transitive
n\times n
-matrix
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=(\epsilon_{i,j})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
with
\epsilon_{i,j}\in\{1,-1\}
,
1\leq i,j\leq n
,

(1.9)

\mathbf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}:=(R_{1,2n}^{(\epsilon_{n,1})}\cdots R_{n,2n}^{(\epsilon_{n,n})})(R_{1,2n-1}^{(\epsilon_{n-1,1})}\cdots R_{n,2n-1}^{(\epsilon_{n-1,n})})\cdots(R_{1,n+1}^{(\epsilon_{1,1})}\cdots R_{n,n+1}^{(\epsilon_{1,n})})

solves QYBE.

One can show (Proposition
5.6
)
that if
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
is transitive almost skew-symmetric
then
\mathbf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon})}=\mathbf{R}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w),\boldsymbol{d})
for some
w\in S_{n}
and

\boldsymbol{d}\in\{1,-1\}^{n}
, which in particular
verifies Conjecture
1.8
in that case. We also verified it for
n\leq 4
.

Another difference between the classical and the quantum situations is that while in the former
a twist only affects the cobracket but not the comultiplication on
U({\mathfrak{g}})
, in the latter new comultiplications heavily depend on a choice of a
Drinfeld twist
. These were first introduced by Drinfeld in
[
13
]
and have been extensively
studied, in particular in connection with lattice models in statistical physics and as a tool
for turning cocommutative bialgebras into non-commutative ones (see
[
11
,
6
,
37
,
7
,
28
,
38
,
29
,
31
]
to name but a few).

As in the classical world, suppose that our bialgebra
H

admits a family of R-matrices
\mathbf{R}=\{R^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}

for the
same
comultiplication.
By Proposition
2.23
, such a family solves the
transitive QYBE

(1.10)

R_{1,2}^{(c)}R_{1,3}^{(c^{\prime})}R_{2,3}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}=R_{2,3}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}R_{1,3}^{(c^{\prime})}R_{1,2}^{(c)}

whenever
c^{\prime}\in\{c,c^{\prime\prime}\}\subset C
, which
is the multiplicative analogue
of our transitive CYBE (
1.5
).
Our second quantum main result generalizes Theorems
1.3

and
1.7
.

Main Theorem 1.9
.

Let
H
be a bialgebra with a family

\mathbf{R}=\{R^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}
of (universal) R-matrices.
Then for any transitive
n
-array
\mathbf{c}=(c_{i,j})_{1\leq i<j\leq n}

J_{\mathbf{c}}=J_{\mathbf{c}}(\mathbf{R}):=R_{2,n+1}^{(c_{1,2})}(R_{3,n+2}^{(c_{2,3})}R_{3,n+1}^{(c_{1,3})})\cdots(R_{n,2n-1}^{(c_{n-1,n})}\cdots R_{n,n+1}^{(c_{1,n})})

is a Drinfeld twist for
H^{\otimes n}
with its
standard comultiplication
\Delta_{H^{\otimes n}}
.
In particular,

\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{d}):=(J_{\mathbf{c}}^{op})^{-1}R_{1,n+1}^{(d_{1})}\cdots R_{n,2n}^{(d_{n})}\,J_{\mathbf{c}}

is an R-matrix for
H^{\otimes n}
with
the comultiplication twisted by
J_{\mathbf{c}}

and thus solves QYBE.

We prove Theorem
1.9
in §
5.2

using the notion of a
relative Drinfeld twist

for a pair of bialgebras (see §
2.11
)
and a transitive generalization
\mathsf{QTr}_{n}(C)

of the group
\mathsf{QTr}_{n}
defined in
[
5
]

(see §
5.1
for the details).

Furthermore, starting with any family
\mathbf{R}=\{R^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}\subset H\widehat{\otimes}H
and an
n\times n
matrix
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{i,j})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}

define

\mathbf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}=(R_{1,2n}^{(a_{n,1})}\cdots R_{n,2n}^{(a_{n,n})})(R_{1,2n-1}^{(a_{n-1,1})}\cdots R_{n,2n-1}^{(a_{n-1,n})})\cdots(R_{1,n+1}^{(a_{1,1})}\cdots R_{n,n+1}^{(a_{1,n})})

which is the quantum counterpart of (
1.2
).
Mirroring Conjecture
1.5
, we formulate

Conjecture 1.10
.

Given a solution
\mathbf{R}=\{R^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}\in H\widehat{\otimes}H
of the transitive QYBE (
1.10
),

\mathbf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}
solves QYBE for any transitive
n\times n
-matrix
\boldsymbol{a}
with
entries in
C
.

This conjecture was verified for
n\leq 4
. It should
be noted that for
|C|>2
solutions
\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{d})
of QYBE provided by Theorem
1.9
are not of the form
\mathbf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}
for a transitive
matrix
\boldsymbol{a}
, and it would be interesting to
find a class of solutions of QYBE encompassing these two.

Similarly to Question
1.4
, it is only natural to raise the following

Question 1.11
.

Which bialgebras
H
admit families
of R-matrices
\{R^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}
?

Any quantization (see, for example,
[
14
,
15
,
17
,
16
,
12
]
) of an answer to Question
1.4
answers Question
1.11
. However, there are also finite-dimensional examples (see §
6.4
)
which are not directly obtainable via quantization.
Yet even in finite-dimensional cases twisting the
comultiplication can have very non-trivial
consequences, as e.g. in
[
6
]
.

We finish the discussion of the quantum story with the dual picture which is even more attractive since it does not require completions. Namely,
for a family of co-quasi-triangular structures

\{\mathcal{R}^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}\subset\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(A^{\otimes 2},\mathbb{k})
on a bialgebra
A
and
a transitive
n
-array
\mathbf{c}
, we construct
(Theorem
5.10
)
a
dual Drinfeld twist

\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(A^{\otimes 2n},\mathbb{k})
(see §
2.12

and §
5.4

for the details).
This yields a family of associative multiplications on
A^{\otimes n}
with the same coalgebra structure.
In addition to avoiding completions,
the twisted multiplication can often be expressed in
a more compact form (Proposition
5.12
).
In particular, for
n=2
this recovers the multiplication
introduced by Takeuchi (
[
35
]
*Section 8)
and studied by Majid for bialgebras (see e.g.
[
29
]
). It turns out that the (iterated) multiplication

A^{\otimes n}\to A
becomes a homomorphism of
bialgebras for the twisted algebra structure on
A^{\otimes n}
(Theorem
5.14
). Once again, in the inductive argument
we utilize a
relative dual Drinfeld twist
(see §
2.12
). As an example,
we obtain a family of twisted algebra structures
parametrized
by permutations on tensor powers of “quantum matrices” (§
6.1
), which, unlike their
q=1
limits, are
no longer isomorphic under permutations of factors.
We expect that they are pairwise non-isomorphic.
Of course, they become isomorphic after passing to the completion, but to construct such an isomorphism is a serious challenge. Perhaps, an even stronger
challenge is to find all
rational
quantizations of
a given classical object.
Moreover, the above “homomorphism assertion” generalizes to the situation when the natural map
A\otimes B\to C
for any sub-bialgebras
A
,
B
of a bialgebra
C

becomes an algebra homomorphism when the algebra
structure on
A\otimes B
is twisted in an appropriate way
(Proposition
2.44
).
Based on the above and other observations, we expect these algebras to be of interest and, in particular, to admit quantum cluster-like structures,
which we plan to explore.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
General notation

All vector spaces, algebras and coalgebras are over some fixed base field
\mathbb{k}
of characteristic zero. The symbol
\otimes
, when unadorned, stands for the tensor product over
\mathbb{k}
.

Given
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, denote
[a,b]:=\{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,:\,a\leq x\leq b\}
. We abbreviate

[n]:=[1,n]
for
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
.
Define
\Upsilon:\mathbb{R}\to\{0,1\}
by
\Upsilon(x)=1

if
x>0
and
\Upsilon(x)=0
if
x\leq 0
, i.e.

(2.1)

\Upsilon(x)=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+\sign(x)-\delta_{x,0}),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

Let
\mathsf{M}
be a multiplicative monoid.
Given any finite subset
I\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
and
a family
X_{i}
,
i\in I
of elements of
\mathsf{M}
we set

\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{i\in I}X_{i}=X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}},\qquad\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{i\in I}X_{i}=X_{i_{r}}\cdots X_{i_{1}}.

where
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}
.

Given any collection
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}
,
n\geq 2
of vector spaces
and a permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
, define
\widehat{\sigma}:\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(V_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{n},V_{\sigma(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{\sigma(n)})

by
\widehat{\sigma}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})=v_{\sigma(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\sigma(n)}
,
v_{i}\in V_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
.
For
\sigma=(i,j)
,
1\leq i<j\leq n
, we denote this map by
\tau_{i,j}
and often abbreviate
\tau_{1,2}
as
\tau
.
It is easy to see that
\widehat{\sigma\rho}=\widehat{\rho}\circ\widehat{\sigma}

in
\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(V_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{n},V_{\sigma\rho(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{\sigma\rho(n)})
,
\sigma,\rho\in S_{n}
.

Let
B
be a unital algebra. Given
N\geq n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
and
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
with

1\leq i_{r}\leq N
,
1\leq r\leq n
,
and
i_{r}\not=i_{s}
,
1\leq r<s\leq n
, we denote by
\phi_{\mathbf{i}}:B^{\otimes n}\to B^{\otimes N}
the unique homomorphism of algebras satisfying

\phi_{\mathbf{i}}(1_{B}^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes b\otimes 1_{B}^{\otimes(n-r)})=1_{B}^{\otimes(i_{r}-1)}\otimes b\otimes 1_{B}^{\otimes(N-i_{r})},\qquad b\in B,\,1\leq r\leq n.

We will often abbreviate
X_{\mathbf{i}}:=\phi_{\mathbf{i}}(X)
,
X\in B^{\otimes n}
.
For example, for
X=x_{1}\otimes x_{2}\otimes x_{3}\in B^{\otimes 3}
in Sweedler-like notation,

X_{2,6,4}=1_{B}\otimes x_{1}\otimes 1_{B}\otimes x_{3}\otimes 1_{B}\otimes x_{2}\in B^{\otimes 6}.

When we write a sequence of indices as a set,
we assume that it is ordered increasingly; for example,
[1,2n]\setminus\{n\}
stands for the sequence
(1,\dots,n-1,n+1,\dots,2n)
.
Note that if
N\geq\max(k,l)
,
X\in B^{\otimes k}
,
Y\in B^{\otimes l}
then, for any disjoint sequences
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})

and
\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{l})

with
1\leq i_{r},j_{s}\leq N
,

X_{\mathbf{i}}
and
Y_{\mathbf{j}}

commute in
B^{\otimes N}
.

We will also use similar notation for tensor products of different (bi)algebras.

For any associative algebra
A
, we denote its center by
Z(A)
. If
B
is an associative algebra and

\varphi:A\to B
is a homomorphism of algebras,
we say that
\partial\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(A,B)
is a
\varphi
-
derivation
if
\partial(aa^{\prime})=\partial(a)\varphi(a^{\prime})+\varphi(a)\partial(a^{\prime})

for all
a,a^{\prime}\in A
.

2.2.
Completions

Let
A
be an algebra and let
K_{A}
be an ideal in
A
. We say
that
(A,K_{A})
is a
locally finite pair

if

(2.2)

\dim(A/K_{A}^{r})<\infty,\qquad r\geq 1.

The following well-known fact provides
a large class of locally finite pairs.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
A
be finitely generated and let
K_{A}\subset A

be an ideal of codimension
1
. Then
(A,K_{A})
is
a locally finite pair.
Moreover, the dimension of

A/K_{A}^{r}
,
r\geq 0
is bounded above by
rN^{r-1}
where
N

is the number of generators of
A
.

Proof.

Let
V\subset K_{A}
be a finite-dimensional subspace generating
A
. Let
K=\bigoplus_{r\geq 1}V^{\otimes r}
be the augmentation ideal of the tensor algebra
T(V)
of
V

and let
I
be the kernel of the
canonical projection
T(V)\to A
. Then
K_{A}
identifies
with
(K+I)/I
, hence
K_{A}^{r}
identifies with
(K^{r}+I)/I
.
Therefore,
A/K_{A}^{r}
is isomorphic
to
T(V)/(K^{r}+I)
, which is a homomorphic
image of
T(V)/K^{r}\cong\mathbb{k}\oplus V\oplus\cdots\oplus V^{\otimes(r-1)}
as a vector space.
∎

Let
\widehat{A}=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\lim}A/K_{A}^{r}

be the completion of
A
with respect to
K_{A}
.
We say that
f\in A^{*}
is
locally finite
with respect to
K_{A}
if
f(K_{A}^{r})=0
for some
r>0
and
let
A^{\smallsquare}\subset A^{*}
be the subspace of all locally finite linear functionals with respect to
K_{A}
.
Clearly,
Sweedler finite dual
A^{o}
of
A
is
contained in
A^{\smallsquare}
, which justifies the notation.
The following is immediate.

Lemma 2.2
.

The evaluation pairing
A^{\smallsquare}\otimes A\to\mathbb{k}
naturally lifts to a well-defined pairing

A^{\smallsquare}\otimes\widehat{A}\to\mathbb{k}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose that
(A,K_{A})
and
(B,K_{B})
are locally finite pairs and let
K_{A\otimes B}=K_{A}\otimes B+A\otimes K_{B}
,
which is an ideal in
A\otimes B
.

(a)

(A\otimes B,K_{A\otimes B})
is a locally finite pair;

(b)

A^{\smallsquare}\otimes B^{\smallsquare}\subset(A\otimes B)^{\smallsquare}
;

(c)

Let
A\widehat{\otimes}B
be the completion
of
A\otimes B
with respect to
K_{A\otimes B}
.
Then the evaluation pairing
(A^{\smallsquare}\otimes B^{\smallsquare})\otimes(A\otimes B)\to\mathbb{k}
naturally lifts to a
well-defined pairing
(A^{\smallsquare}\otimes B^{\smallsquare})\otimes(A\widehat{\otimes}B)\to\mathbb{k}
.

Proof.

Clearly,
K_{A\otimes B}^{r}\supset K_{A}^{r}\otimes B+A\otimes K_{B}^{r}
hence
(A\otimes B)/K_{A\otimes B}^{r}

is a homomorphic image of
(A\otimes B)/(K_{A}^{r}\otimes B+A\otimes K_{B}^{r})\cong A/K_{A}^{r}\otimes B/K_{B}^{r}
. This proves
(a)
. Part
(b)
is obvious while
(c)

follows from
(a)
,
(b)
and Lemma
2.2

∎

Corollary 2.4
.

Let
H
be a finitely generated algebra with counit and
let
H^{\widehat{\otimes}n}
be the natural completion of
H^{\otimes n}
in the spirit of Lemma
2.3
. Then for any
R\in H^{\widehat{\otimes}n}

the assignments
f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{1}\mapsto(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{n})(R)
,

f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in H^{\smallsquare}
define a
linear map
\mathcal{R}:H^{\smallsquare}{}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{k}
.

2.3.
Coalgebras

Let
C
be a coalgebra with comultiplication
\Delta:C\to C\otimes C
and counit
\varepsilon:C\to\mathbb{k}
.
Recall that
\sigma\in\End_{\mathbb{k}}C
is
a coalgebra endomorphism if

(\sigma\otimes\sigma)\circ\Delta=\Delta\circ\sigma
and
\varepsilon\circ\tau=\varepsilon
.
Clearly, this notion is dual to that of
an endomorphism of unital algebras
and that coalgebra endomorphisms form
a monoid with respect to composition.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\sigma\in\End_{\mathbb{k}}C
be a coalgebra endomorphism. Then

\sigma
is also an endomorphism of

C^{cop}=(C,\Delta^{op})
where
\Delta^{op}=\tau_{1,2}\circ\Delta
is the opposite
comultiplication.

Proof.

Let
c\in C
. Since
\sigma
is a coalgebra endomorphism, we have
\Delta(\sigma(c))=(\sigma(c))_{(1)}\otimes(\sigma(c))_{(2)}=\sigma(c_{(1)})\otimes\sigma(c_{(2)})
.
Then
\Delta^{op}(\sigma(c))=(\sigma(c))_{(2)}\otimes(\sigma(c))_{(1)}=\sigma(c_{(2)})\otimes\sigma(c_{(1)})=(\sigma\otimes\sigma)\Delta^{op}(c)
.
∎

Let
C
,
D
be coalgebras with respective comultiplications

\Delta_{C}:C\to C\otimes C
,
\Delta_{D}:D\to D\otimes D
and respective
counits
\varepsilon_{C}:C\to\mathbb{k}
,
\varepsilon_{D}:D\to\mathbb{k}
.
Then
C\otimes D
is naturally a coalgebra with the
counit
\varepsilon_{C}\otimes\varepsilon_{D}
and with the
comultiplication
\Delta_{C\otimes D}:C\otimes D\to(C\otimes D)\otimes(C\otimes D)
defined by

\Delta_{C\otimes D}(c\otimes d)=c_{(1)}\otimes d_{(1)}\otimes c_{(2)}\otimes d_{(2)}

where
\Delta_{C}(c)=c_{(1)}\otimes c_{(2)}
in Sweedler notation.
In particular, for any coalgebra
C
and
n>0
,
C^{\otimes n}

is naturally a coalgebra with

\Delta_{C^{\otimes n}}(c^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{n})=c^{1}_{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{n}_{(1)}\otimes c^{1}_{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{n}_{(2)}

for all
c^{1},\dots,c^{n}\in C
.

Lemma 2.6
.

The symmetric group
S_{n}
acts
on
C^{\otimes n}
by coalgebra automorphisms via

\sigma\triangleright(c^{1}\otimes\cdots c^{n})=c^{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{\sigma^{-1}(n)},\quad c^{1},\dots,c^{n}\in C,\sigma\in S_{n}.

Proof.

Given
\sigma\in S_{n}
, denote
\tilde{\sigma}
the
\mathbb{k}
-linear automorphism of
C^{\otimes n}
defined by

\tilde{\sigma}(c^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{n})=c^{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{\sigma^{-1}(n)},c^{1},\dots,c^{n}\in C.

It is a standard fact that
\widetilde{\sigma\circ\tau}=\tilde{\sigma}\circ\tilde{\tau}
for all
\sigma,\tau\in S_{n}
.
We have, for any
c^{1},\dots,c^{n}\in C

\displaystyle\Delta

{}_{C^{\otimes n}}(\tilde{\sigma}(c^{1}\otimes\cdots c^{n}))=\Delta_{C^{\otimes n}}(c^{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{\sigma^{-1}(n)})

\displaystyle=(c^{\sigma^{-1}(1)})_{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes(c^{\sigma^{-1}(n)})_{(1)}\otimes(c^{\sigma^{-1}(1)})_{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes(c^{\sigma^{-1}(n)})_{(2)}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{\sigma}\otimes\tilde{\sigma})(c^{1}_{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{n}_{(1)})\otimes(c^{1}_{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{n}_{(2)})=(\tilde{\sigma}\otimes\tilde{\sigma})\Delta_{C^{\otimes n}}(c^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{n}).\qed

Let
B
be a bialgebra. We say that
\sigma\in\End_{\mathbb{k}}B
is a bialgebra endomorphism if it is an algebra and a coalgebra endomorphism.

Let
C
be a coalgebra.
Given
f\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(C^{\otimes r},\mathbb{k})
,
r\geq 1
,
and a sequence
(i_{1},\dots,i_{r})\in[N]^{r}
,
N\geq r

with
i_{s}\not=i_{t}
,
1\leq s<t\leq r

define
f_{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(C^{\otimes N},\mathbb{k})

by

f_{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}}(c_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{N})=f(c_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{i_{r}})\prod_{j\in[N]\setminus\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}}\varepsilon(c_{j}),\qquad c_{1},\dots,c_{N}\in C.

The convolution product
\ast
is defined on
\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(C,\mathbb{k})
via

(f\ast g)(c)=f(c_{(1)})g(c_{(2)}),\qquad f,g\in\Hom(C,\mathbb{k}),\,c\in C,

and is easily seen to be associative, while
\varepsilon
is the unity. It is immediate from the definition that for any homomorphism of coalgebras
\varphi:C\to C^{\prime}

(2.3)

(f\circ\varphi)\ast(g\circ\varphi)=(f\ast g)\circ\varphi,\qquad f,g\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(C^{\prime},\mathbb{k}),

whence if
f\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(C^{\prime},\mathbb{k})
is
\ast
-invertible
then so is
f\circ\varphi

and

(2.4)

(f\circ\varphi)^{\ast-1}=f^{\ast-1}\circ\varphi,

where
f^{\ast-1}
is the
\ast
-inverse of
f
.
Note the following
elementary yet very useful

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
C
be a coalgebra and let
\mathcal{S}\in\Hom(C,\mathbb{k})

be
\ast
-invertible.
Then the following are equivalent for
a
\mathbb{k}
-vector space
V
and
f,g\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(C,V)

(i)

(\mathcal{S}\otimes f)\circ\Delta=(g\otimes\mathcal{S})\circ\Delta
;

(ii)

f=(\mathcal{S}^{\ast-1}\otimes g\otimes\mathcal{S})\circ(\Delta\otimes\id_{C})\circ\Delta
;

(iii)

(f\otimes\mathcal{S}^{\ast-1})\circ\Delta=(\mathcal{S}^{\ast-1}\otimes g)\circ\Delta
.

Proof.

To prove implications
(i)
\implies
(ii)

and
(iii)
\implies

(ii)

it suffices to observe that
(\mathcal{S}^{\ast-1}\otimes\mathcal{S}\otimes h)\circ(\Delta\otimes\id_{C})\circ\Delta=h=(h\otimes\mathcal{S}^{\ast-1}\otimes\mathcal{S})\circ(\Delta\otimes\id_{C})\circ\Delta

for all
h\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(C,V)
.
Suppose that
(ii)
holds,
that is
f(c)=\mathcal{S}^{\ast-1}(c_{(1)})\mathcal{S}(c_{(3)})g(c_{(2)})

for all
c\in C
. Then

\mathcal{S}(c_{(1)})f(c_{(2)})=\mathcal{S}(c_{(1)})\mathcal{S}^{\ast-1}(c_{(2)})\mathcal{S}(c_{(4)})g(c_{(3)})=\mathcal{S}(c_{(3)})g(c_{(2)}),

which is
(i)
, and
similarly

\mathcal{S}^{\ast-1}(c_{(2)})f(c_{(1)})=\mathcal{S}^{\ast-1}(c_{(1)})\mathcal{S}(c_{(3)})\mathcal{S}^{\ast-1}(c_{(4)})g(c_{(2)})=\mathcal{S}^{\ast-1}(c_{(1)})g(c_{(2)}),

which is
(iii)
.
∎

If
A
and
B
are unital bialgebras, we can write

\Delta_{A\otimes B}(a\otimes b)
as
(\Delta_{A}(a))_{(1,3)}\cdot(\Delta_{B}(b))_{(2,4)}
, and we will use the shorthand

\Delta_{A\otimes B}=(\Delta_{A})_{1,3}\circ(\Delta_{B})_{2,4}
in this
situation. Likewise, the standard comultiplication on
B^{\otimes n}
can be presented as
\Delta_{B^{\otimes n}}=\Delta_{1,n+1}\circ\cdots\circ\Delta_{n,2n}
.

2.4.
Lie bialgebras

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a Lie algebra and let
U({\mathfrak{g}})
be its universal enveloping algebra. Let
\Delta:U({\mathfrak{g}})\to U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})
be the standard
comultiplication which is uniquely defined by
\Delta(x)=x\otimes 1+1\otimes x
,
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
. We identify
{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
with its natural image in
U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})
.

Recall that
\delta\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})
is a Lie
cobracket

if it satisfies

(
LB_{1}
)

\delta([x,y]_{{\mathfrak{g}}})=[\delta(x),\Delta(y)]-[\delta(y),\Delta(x)]
in
U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})

for all
x,y\in{\mathfrak{g}}
;

(
LB_{2}
)

\tau_{1,2}\circ\delta=-\delta
;

(
LB_{3}
)

(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 3}}+\tau_{1,2}\tau_{2,3}+\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2})\circ(\delta\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})\circ\delta=0

as a linear map
{\mathfrak{g}}\to{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 3}
.

The first condition is equivalent to the requirement that
\delta
extends to a
\Delta
-derivation

U({\mathfrak{g}})\to U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})
,
that is

(2.5)

\delta(xy)=\delta(x)\Delta(y)+\Delta(x)\delta(y),\quad x,y\in U({\mathfrak{g}}).

Note that if
\delta
satisfies
(

⁢
L

B
2

)

then
(

⁢
L

B
3

)
can be rewritten as

(2.6)

(\delta\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})\circ\delta=(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\delta)\circ\delta+\tau_{2,3}(\delta\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})\circ\delta.

The following is well-known (see e.g.
[
11
]
).

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
be a Lie bialgebra. Then

(\Delta\otimes\id)\circ\delta=(\id\otimes\delta)\circ\Delta+\tau_{2,3}(\delta\otimes\id)\circ\Delta
on
U({\mathfrak{g}})
.

Proof.

The argument is by induction on the canonical filtration
\{U_{n}({\mathfrak{g}})\}_{n\geq 0}
on
U({\mathfrak{g}})
. The assertion for
n=0
is trivial while for
n=1
it is easily checked. Suppose that the Lemma is proved for all

u\in U_{n-1}({\mathfrak{g}})
. Then for any
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
,
u\in U_{n-1}({\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes\id)\delta(xu)

\displaystyle=(\Delta\otimes\id)\delta(x)\cdot(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(u)+(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)\cdot(\Delta\otimes\id)\delta(u)

\displaystyle=(\id\otimes\delta)\Delta(x)\cdot(\id\otimes\Delta)\Delta(u)+\tau_{2,3}(\delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)\cdot(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(u)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+(\id\otimes\Delta)\Delta(x)\cdot(\id\otimes\delta)\Delta(u)+(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)\cdot\tau_{2,3}(\delta\otimes\id)\Delta(u)

\displaystyle=(\id\otimes\delta)(\Delta(xu))+\tau_{2,3}(\delta\otimes\id)(\Delta(xu)),

where we used (
2.5
) and the cocommutativity and the co-associativity of
\Delta
. Since
U_{n}({\mathfrak{g}})=U_{n-1}({\mathfrak{g}})+{\mathfrak{g}}U_{n-1}({\mathfrak{g}})
, this proves the inductive step.
∎

Define
\mathbb{\Lbrack}\cdot,\cdot\mathbb{\Rbrack}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}({\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 2}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 2},U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 3})
via

\mathbb{\Lbrack}s,s^{\prime}\mathbb{\Rbrack}=[(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\Delta)(s),(\Delta\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(s^{\prime})]=[s_{1,2}+s_{1,3},s^{\prime}_{1,3}+s^{\prime}_{2,3}],\qquad s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}.

The following is well-known
(see e.g.
[
29
]
*§8.1 and
[
13
,
15
,
17
,
16
]
).
We provide a proof here for the reader’s convenience, since some of the intermediate identities will be
needed later.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
r\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
and define
\delta_{r}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})
by
\delta_{r}(x)=[r,\Delta(x)]
,
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
.

(a)

\delta_{r}
satisfies
(

⁢
L

B
1

)

and so extends to a
\Delta
-derivation

\delta_{r}:U({\mathfrak{g}})\to U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})
. In particular,

\delta_{r}(u)=[r,\Delta(u)]
for all
u\in U({\mathfrak{g}})
.

(b)

\delta_{r}
satisfies

(

⁢
L

B
2

)
if and only if
r+\tau_{1,2}(r)
centralizes

\Delta(U({\mathfrak{g}}))\subset U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})
or, equivalently, if and only
if
r+\tau_{1,2}(r)
is

{\mathfrak{g}}
-invariant with respect to the natural
diagonal action of
{\mathfrak{g}}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
;

(c)

Suppose that
\delta_{r}
satisfies
(

⁢
L

B
2

)
. Then
for all
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}

((\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 3}}+\tau_{1,2}\tau_{2,3}+\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2})\circ(\delta_{r}\otimes\id)\circ\delta_{r})(x)=[\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,r\mathbb{\Rbrack},(\Delta\otimes\id)\circ\Delta(x)].

In particular, under this assumption
\delta_{r}
satisfies
(

⁢
L

B
3

)
if and only if

\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,r\mathbb{\Rbrack}
centralizes

(\Delta\otimes\id)\circ\Delta(U({\mathfrak{g}}))\subset U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 3}

or, equivalently, is
{\mathfrak{g}}
-invariant
with respect to the natural diagonal

{\mathfrak{g}}
-action on
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 3}
.

(d)

\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,r\mathbb{\Rbrack}=0
if and only if
(\delta_{r}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(r)=[r_{2,3},r_{1,3}]
if and only if

(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\delta_{r})(r)=[r_{1,2},r_{1,3}]
.

Proof.

We need the following

Lemma 2.10
.

Suppose that
\delta:U({\mathfrak{g}})\to U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})
is a
\Delta
-derivation.
Then for all
u\in U({\mathfrak{g}})
,
r\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
we have in
U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})

(2.7)

(\delta\otimes\id)([r,\Delta(u)])=[(\delta\otimes\id)(r),(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(u)]+[r_{1,3}+r_{2,3},(\delta\otimes\id)\Delta(u)].

Proof.

Write
r=r_{1}\otimes r_{2}
in Sweedler-like notation. Then

\displaystyle(\delta\otimes\id)(r\cdot\Delta(u))

\displaystyle=(\delta\otimes\id)(r_{1}u_{(1)}\otimes r_{2}u_{(2)})

\displaystyle=\delta(r_{1})\cdot\Delta(u_{(1)})\otimes r_{2}u_{(2)}+\Delta(r_{1})\cdot\delta(u_{(1)})\otimes r_{2}u_{(2)}

\displaystyle=(\delta\otimes\id)(r)\cdot(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(u)+(\Delta\otimes\id)(r)\cdot(\delta\otimes\id)\Delta(u)

\displaystyle=(\delta\otimes\id)(r)\cdot(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(u)+(r_{1,3}+r_{2,3})\cdot(\delta\otimes\id)\Delta(u).

The identity for
(\delta\otimes\id)(\Delta(u)\cdot r)
is obtained similarly, and the assertion follows.
∎

To prove part
(a)
, note that for all
x,y\in{\mathfrak{g}}
,

\displaystyle\delta_{r}([x,y]_{{\mathfrak{g}}})

\displaystyle=[r,\Delta([x,y]_{{\mathfrak{g}}})]=[r,[\Delta(x),\Delta(y)]]

\displaystyle=[[r,\Delta(x)],\Delta(y)]+[\Delta(x),[r,\Delta(y)]]=[\delta_{r}(x),\Delta(y)]-[\delta_{r}(y),\Delta(x)].

Thus,
\delta_{r}
satisfies
(

⁢
L

B
1

)
and hence extends to a
\Delta
-derivation
U({\mathfrak{g}})\to U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})
. The second assertion of
(a)
follows since
{\mathfrak{g}}

generates
U({\mathfrak{g}})
as an associative algebra.

Since
\Delta
is cocommutative,

\tau_{1,2}\circ\delta_{r}(x)+\delta_{r}(x)=[\tau_{1,2}(r)+r,\Delta(x)]=0
for all
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}

if and only if
\tau_{1,2}(r)+r
commutes with
\Delta(x)
for all
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
. Since
{\mathfrak{g}}
generates
U({\mathfrak{g}})
as an associative algebra, part
(b)
follows.

By Lemma
2.10
we have
for all
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle(\delta_{r}

\displaystyle\otimes\id)\delta_{r}(x)=[(\delta_{r}\otimes\id)(r),(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)]+[r_{1,3}+r_{2,3},(\delta_{r}\otimes\id)\Delta(x)]

\displaystyle=[(\delta_{r}\otimes\id)(r),(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)]+[r_{1,3}+r_{2,3},\delta_{r}(x)_{1,2}].

Furthermore,

(2.8)

\displaystyle(\delta_{r}\otimes\id)(r)=[r_{1,2},r_{1,3}+r_{2,3}]=-[r_{2,1},r_{1,3}+r_{2,3}]

and

\delta_{r}(x)_{1,2}=[r_{1,2},x\otimes 1\otimes 1+1\otimes x\otimes 1]=[r_{1,2},(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)]=-[r_{2,1},(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)].

Thus,

\displaystyle(\delta_{r}\otimes\id)\delta_{r}(x)

\displaystyle=[[r_{1,2},r_{1,3}+r_{2,3}],(\Delta\otimes\id)(x)]+[r_{1,3}+r_{2,3},[r_{1,2},(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)]]

\displaystyle=[r_{1,2},[r_{1,3}+r_{2,3},(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)]]

\displaystyle=-[[r_{2,1},r_{1,3}+r_{2,3}],(\Delta\otimes\id)(x)]-[r_{1,3}+r_{2,3},[r_{2,1},(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)]]

\displaystyle=-[r_{2,1},[r_{1,3}+r_{2,3},(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)]]

Note that, since
r+\tau_{1,2}(r)
commutes with
\Delta({\mathfrak{g}})
,

r_{i,j}+r_{j,i}
commutes with
(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta({\mathfrak{g}})
for all
1\leq i<j\leq 3
.
Applying
\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 3}}+\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}+\tau_{1,2}\tau_{2,3}

and taking into account that
\Delta
is cocommutative and coassociative we obtain

\displaystyle(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 3}}

\displaystyle+\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}+\tau_{1,2}\tau_{2,3})((\delta_{r}\otimes\id)\delta_{r}(x))

\displaystyle=[r_{1,2},[r_{1,3}+r_{2,3},z]]-[r_{1,3},[r_{3,2}+r_{1,2},z]]+[r_{2,3},[r_{2,1}+r_{3,1},z]]

\displaystyle=[r_{1,2},[r_{1,3}+r_{2,3},z]]-[r_{1,3},[r_{1,2}-r_{2,3},z]]-[r_{2,3},[r_{1,2}+r_{1,3},z]]

\displaystyle=([r_{12},[r_{2,3},z]]+[r_{2,3},[z,r_{12}]])-([r_{1,3},[r_{1,2},z]]+[r_{1,2},[z,r_{1,3}]])

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-([r_{2,3},[r_{1,3},z]]+[r_{1,3},[z,r_{2,3}]])

\displaystyle=[[r_{1,2},r_{2,3}]-[r_{1,3},r_{1,2}]-[r_{2,3},r_{1,3}],z]=[\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,r\mathbb{\Rbrack},z],

where we abbreviated
z=(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)
. Part
(c)
is now immediate.

Finally, note that by (
2.8
)

\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,r\mathbb{\Rbrack}=[r_{1,2}+r_{1,3},r_{1,3}+r_{2,3}]=(\delta_{r}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(r)+[r_{1,3},r_{2,3}].

Similarly, since
(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\delta_{r})(r)=[r_{2,3},r_{1,2}+r_{1,3}]
,

\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,r\mathbb{\Rbrack}=-(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\delta_{r})(r)+[r_{1,2},r_{1,3}].

These identities prove part
(d)
.
∎

2.5.
Quasi-triangular Lie bialgebras

Following
[
13
]
, Lie bialgebra
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
is
called
quasi-triangular
if there
is
r\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
, called a
classical r-matrix, such that

\delta=\delta_{r}

and
\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,r\mathbb{\Rbrack}=0
.

The equation
\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,r\mathbb{\Rbrack}=0
is called the
Classical Yang-Baxter equation
(CYBE).
More generally, we have the following

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
be a Lie bialgebra and let

\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
be a family
of classical r-matrices for
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
.
Then in
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 3}

(2.9)

[r_{i,j}^{(c)},r_{i,k}^{(c^{\prime})}]+[r_{i,j}^{(c)},r_{j,k}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}]+[r_{i,k}^{(c^{\prime})},r_{j,k}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}]=0

for all
c^{\prime}\in\{c,c^{\prime\prime}\}\subset C
,
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
.

Proof.

Since
S_{3}
acts by algebra automorphisms on
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 3}
, it suffices
to prove (
2.9
) for
(i,j,k)=(1,2,3)
.
Let
c,c^{\prime\prime}\in C
.
Suppose first that
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime\prime}
. Since
\delta=\delta_{r^{(c)}}=\delta_{r^{(c^{\prime\prime})}}
, we have by Proposition
2.9
(d)

0=(\delta\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(r^{(c^{\prime\prime})})+[r^{(c^{\prime\prime})}_{1,3},r^{(c^{\prime\prime})}_{2,3}]=[r^{(c)}_{1,2},r^{(c^{\prime\prime})}_{1,3}+r^{(c^{\prime\prime})}_{2,3}]+[r^{(c^{\prime\prime})}_{1,3},r^{(c^{\prime\prime})}_{2,3}],

which is (
2.9
) with
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime\prime}
. Similarly, if
c^{\prime}=c
then
by Proposition
2.9
(d)

0=[r^{(c)}_{1,2},r^{(c)}_{1,3}]-(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\delta)(r^{(c)})=[r^{(c)}_{1,2},r^{(c)}_{1,3}]-[r^{(c^{\prime\prime})}_{2,3},r^{(c)}_{1,2}+r^{(c)}_{1,3}],

which yields (
2.9
) with
c^{\prime}=c
.
∎

The basic example of such a family is provided by the following

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
be a quasi-triangular Lie bialgebra
with a classical r-matrix
r
.
Then
-\tau_{1,2}(r)
is also a classical r-matrix
for
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
. In particular, if
r^{(1)}=r

and
r^{(-1)}=-\tau_{1,2}(r)
then

[r^{(\epsilon)}_{i,j},r^{(\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i,k}]+[r^{(\epsilon)}_{i,j},r^{(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})}_{j,k}]+[r^{(\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i,k},r^{(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})}_{j,k}]=0

provided that
\epsilon^{\prime}\in\{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\}\subset\{1,-1\}
and
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\delta=\delta_{r}
for some
r\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}

satisfying
\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,r\mathbb{\Rbrack}=0
.
Then, since
\Delta
is cocommutative and
\tau_{1,2}\circ\delta=-\delta
, we have

\delta=\delta_{-\tau_{1,2}(r)}
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{\Lbrack}-\tau_{1,2}(r),-\tau_{1,2}(r)\mathbb{\Rbrack}

\displaystyle=[(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\Delta)(\tau_{1,2}(r)),(\Delta\otimes\id)(\tau_{1,2}(r)]

\displaystyle=\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}([(\Delta\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(r),(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\Delta)(r)])=-\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}(\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,r\mathbb{\Rbrack})=0.

Thus,
-\tau_{1,2}(r)
is also a classical r-matrix for
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
.
The remaining assertion is then immediate from Proposition
2.11
.
∎

2.6.
Classical Drinfeld twists

Given
r\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
, denote
r^{-}:=r-\tau_{1,2}(r)
.

A
weak classical Drinfeld twist

j
for
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
is an element
of
{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
such that
\tilde{\delta}_{j}:=\delta+\delta_{j^{-}}

is a Lie cobracket.

Remark 2.13
.

This definition is slightly different from that of the classical twist given in
[
13
]
to emphasize the parallel with the quantum situation.

The following is well-known (see
[
13
]
and
[
29
]
*§8.1).

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
be a Lie bialgebra and
j\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
.

(a)

j
is a weak classical Drinfeld twist if and only if

(2.10)

\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta}:=(\delta\otimes\id)(j^{-})-(\id\otimes\delta)(j^{-})-\tau_{2,3}(\delta\otimes\id)(j^{-})+\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}

centralizes
(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(U({\mathfrak{g}}))\subset U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 3}
or, equivalently, is
{\mathfrak{g}}
-invariant
with respect to the natural diagonal
{\mathfrak{g}}
-action on
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 3}
.

(b)

Suppose that
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
is quasi-triangular with a classical r-matrix

r
. Then

(2.11)

\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta}=\mathbb{\Lbrack}r+j^{-},r+j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}.

In particular,
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta+\delta_{j^{-}})

is quasi-triangular with the classical r-matrix
r+j^{-}

if and only if
\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta}=0
.

Proof.

Note that both
(

⁢
L

B
1

)
and
(

⁢
L

B
2

)

are linear in
\delta
.
Since
\delta
satisfies
(

⁢
L

B
1

)
and
(

⁢
L

B
2

)
by assumption while
\delta_{j^{-}}
satisfies
(

⁢
L

B
1

)
by Proposition
2.9
(a)
and
(

⁢
L

B
2

)

by Proposition
2.9
(b)
as
\tau_{1,2}(j^{-})+j^{-}=0
,
it follows
that
\tilde{\delta}_{j}=\delta+\delta_{j^{-}}
satisfies
(

⁢
L

B
1

)
and
(

⁢
L

B
2

)
.

We have

\displaystyle(\tilde{\delta}_{j}\otimes\id)\circ\tilde{\delta}_{j}=(\delta\otimes\id)\circ\delta+(\delta\otimes\id)\circ\delta_{j^{-}}+(\delta_{j^{-}}\otimes\id)\circ\delta+(\delta_{j^{-}}\otimes\id)\circ\delta_{j^{-}}.

Note that
\delta
satisfies
(

⁢
L

B
3

)
by assumption and

(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 3}}+\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}+\tau_{1,2}\tau_{2,3})\circ(\delta_{j^{-}}\otimes\id)\circ\delta_{j^{-}}(x)=[\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack},(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)]

by Proposition
2.9
(c)
. Since

(\delta_{j^{-}}\otimes\id)\delta(x)=[j^{-}_{1,2},(\Delta\otimes\id)\delta(x)]=[j^{-}_{1,2},\delta(x)_{1,3}+\delta(x)_{2,3}],

we have

\displaystyle(\id_{\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 3}}

\displaystyle+\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}+\tau_{1,2}\tau_{2,3})\circ(\delta_{j^{-}}\otimes\id)\circ\delta(x)

\displaystyle=[j^{-}_{1,2},\delta(x)_{1,3}+\delta(x)_{2,3}]+[j^{-}_{3,1},\delta(x)_{3,2}+\delta(x)_{1,2}]+[j^{-}_{2,3},\delta(x)_{2,1}+\delta(x)_{3,1}]

(2.12)

\displaystyle=-[j^{-}_{1,3}+j^{-}_{2,3},\delta(x)_{1,2}]+[j^{-}_{1,2}-j^{-}_{2,3},\delta(x)_{1,3}]+[j^{-}_{1,2}+j^{-}_{1,3},\delta(x)_{2,3}].

Since

(\delta\otimes\id)\delta_{j^{-}}(x)=[(\delta\otimes\id)(j^{-}),(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)]+[j^{-}_{1,3}+j^{-}_{2,3},\delta(x)_{1,2}]

by Lemma
2.10
,
\Delta
is coassociative and cocommutative and
\tau_{1,2}(j^{-})=-j^{-}
,
it follows that

\displaystyle(

\displaystyle\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 3}}+\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}+\tau_{1,2}\tau_{2,3})\circ(\delta\otimes\id)\circ\delta_{j^{-}}(x)

\displaystyle=[(\delta\otimes\id)(j^{-})-\tau_{2,3}(\delta\otimes\id)(j^{-})-(\id\otimes\delta)(j^{-}),(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+[j^{-}_{1,3}+j^{-}_{2,3},\delta(x)_{1,2}]+[j^{-}_{3,2}+j^{-}_{1,2},\delta(x)_{3,1}]+[j^{-}_{2,1}+j^{-}_{3,1},\delta(x)_{2,3}]

\displaystyle=[\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta}-\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack},(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)]-((\id_{\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 3}}+\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}+\tau_{1,2}\tau_{2,3})\circ(\delta_{j^{-}}\otimes\id)\circ\delta)(x),

where we used (
2.12
). Thus,

(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 3}}+\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}+\tau_{1,2}\tau_{2,3})\circ(\tilde{\delta}_{j}\otimes\id)\circ\tilde{\delta}_{j}(x)=[\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta},(\Delta\otimes\id)\Delta(x)],

and part
(a)
is now
immediate.

Suppose that
\delta=\delta_{r}
with
\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,r\mathbb{\Rbrack}=0
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{\Lbrack}r+j^{-},r+j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}=\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,r\mathbb{\Rbrack}+\mathbb{\Lbrack}r,j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}+\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},r\mathbb{\Rbrack}+\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}+[r_{1,2}+r_{1,3},j^{-}_{1,3}+j^{-}_{2,3}]-[r_{1,3}+r_{2,3},j^{-}_{1,2}+j^{-}_{1,3}]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}+[r_{1,2},j^{-}_{1,3}+j^{-}_{2,3}]+[r_{1,3},j^{-}_{2,3}-j^{-}_{1,2}]-[r_{2,3},j^{-}_{1,2}+j^{-}_{1,3}]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}+(\delta_{r}\otimes\id)(j^{-})-(\id\otimes\delta_{r})(j^{-})-[r_{1,3},j^{-}_{1,2}+j^{-}_{3,2}]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}+(\delta_{r}\otimes\id)(j^{-})-(\id\otimes\delta_{r})(j^{-})-\tau_{2,3}([r_{1,2},j^{-}_{1,3}+j^{-}_{2,3}])=\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta}.

This proves the first assertion in part
(b)
.
To prove the second, note that since
\delta=\delta_{r}
,

\tilde{\delta}_{j}=\delta_{r+j^{-}}
. The assertion
is then immediate from (
2.11
).
∎

From now on, we call
j\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfying

\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-},j^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta}=0
a
classical Drinfeld twist
for
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
.

2.7.
Relative classical twist

Recall that
U({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}})
is isomorphic
to
U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})
as a bialgebra,
with
(x,y)\mapsto x\otimes 1+1\otimes y
,
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
,
y\in{\mathfrak{h}}
. If
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})
and
({\mathfrak{h}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}})
are Lie bialgebras, then

{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}
is a Lie bialgebra
with
\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}((x,y))=\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}}(x)_{1,3}+\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}(y)_{2,4}
in
(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}}))^{\otimes 2}
for all
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
,
y\in{\mathfrak{h}}
.
Note that if
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})
and
({\mathfrak{h}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}})

are quasi-triangular with respective classical r-matrices

r_{{\mathfrak{g}}}
,
r_{{\mathfrak{h}}}
then
{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}

is quasi-triangular, its classical r-matrix
being
(r_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}+(r_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4}
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})
and
({\mathfrak{h}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}})
be Lie bialgebras.
We say that
\mathbf{f}\in{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}

is a
relative classical Drinfeld twist
for
({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})

if
\mathbf{f}_{2,3}=1_{U({\mathfrak{g}})}\otimes\mathbf{f}\otimes 1_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})

is a classical Drinfeld twist for
({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})
.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})
and
({\mathfrak{h}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}})
be Lie bialgebras and let

\mathbf{f}\in{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
. Then
\mathbf{f}
is a relative
Drinfeld twist if and only if

(\id_{(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}}))^{\otimes 3}}-\tau_{2,4}\tau_{1,3}+\tau_{2,6}\tau_{3,5}\tau_{1,3})((\id_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{2,3,5})\\
-(\id_{(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}}))^{\otimes 3}}-\tau_{4,6}\tau_{3,5}+\tau_{2,4}\tau_{4,6}\tau_{1,5})((\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{2,4,5})\\
=[\mathbf{f}_{2,3},\mathbf{f}_{2,5}]+[\mathbf{f}_{2,5},\mathbf{f}_{4,5}]+[\mathbf{f}_{4,5},\mathbf{f}_{4,1}]+[\mathbf{f}_{4,1},\mathbf{f}_{6,1}]+[\mathbf{f}_{6,1},\mathbf{f}_{6,3}]+[\mathbf{f}_{6,3},\mathbf{f}_{2,3}].

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{f}_{23}^{-}=\mathbf{f}_{2,3}-\mathbf{f}_{4,1}
, we have

\displaystyle(\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}

\displaystyle\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(\mathbf{f}_{2,3}-\mathbf{f}_{4,1})=(\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{2,4,5}-(\id_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{6,1,3},

whence

\displaystyle(\id_{(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}}))^{\otimes 3}}+\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}}\tau_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{2}}+\tau_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{2}}\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}})\circ(\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(\mathbf{f}_{2,3}-\mathbf{f}_{4,1})

\displaystyle\qquad=(\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{2,4,5}+(\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{6,2,3}+(\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{4,6,1}

\displaystyle\qquad\phantom{=}-(\id_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{6,1,3}-(\id_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{4,5,1}-(\id_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{2,3,5}

\displaystyle\qquad=(\id_{(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}}))^{\otimes 3}}-\tau_{4,6}\tau_{3,5}+\tau_{2,4}\tau_{4,6}\tau_{1,5})((\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{2,4,5})

\displaystyle\qquad\phantom{=}-(\id_{(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}}))^{\otimes 3}}-\tau_{2,4}\tau_{1,3}+\tau_{2,6}\tau_{3,5}\tau_{1,3})((\id_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{2,3,5})

where
\tau_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{2}}=\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}\tau_{3,4}\tau_{2,3}
,

\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}}=\tau_{4,5}\tau_{3,4}\tau_{5,6}\tau_{4,5}
,
while

\displaystyle\mathbb{\Lbrack}\mathbf{f}_{2,3}-\mathbf{f}_{4,1},\mathbf{f}_{2,3}-\mathbf{f}_{4,1}\mathbb{\Rbrack}=[\mathbf{f}_{2,3}-\mathbf{f}_{4,1}+\mathbf{f}_{2,5}-\mathbf{f}_{6,1},\mathbf{f}_{2,5}-\mathbf{f}_{6,1}+\mathbf{f}_{4,5}-\mathbf{f}_{6,3}]

\displaystyle\qquad=[\mathbf{f}_{2,3},\mathbf{f}_{2,5}-\mathbf{f}_{6,3}]+[\mathbf{f}_{4,1},\mathbf{f}_{6,1}-\mathbf{f}_{4,5}]+[\mathbf{f}_{2,5},\mathbf{f}_{4,5}]+[\mathbf{f}_{6,1},\mathbf{f}_{6,3}].

The assertion is now immediate.
∎

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
be a quasi-triangular bialgebra with a classical r-matrix
r
. Then
r
is a relative classical Drinfeld twist.
In particular, in the notation of Lemma
2.12
,

({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}})
is quasi-triangular
with the classical r-matrix
r_{2,3}^{(\epsilon)}+r_{1,4}^{(-\epsilon)}+r_{1,3}^{(\epsilon^{\prime})}+r_{2,4}^{(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})}

for any
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\in\{1,-1\}
.

Proof.

Using Proposition
2.9
(d)
we obtain

\displaystyle(\id_{({\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 3}}-\tau_{2,4}\tau_{1,3}+\tau_{2,6}\tau_{3,5}\tau_{1,3})((\id_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\delta)(r)_{2,3,5})

\displaystyle-(\id_{({\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 3}}-\tau_{4,6}\tau_{3,5}+\tau_{2,4}\tau_{4,6}\tau_{1,5})((\delta\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(r)_{2,4,5})

\displaystyle\quad=(\id_{({\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 3}}-\tau_{2,4}\tau_{1,3}+\tau_{2,6}\tau_{3,5}\tau_{1,3})([r_{2,3},r_{2,5}])

\displaystyle\quad\phantom{=}-(\id_{({\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 3}}-\tau_{4,6}\tau_{3,5}+\tau_{2,4}\tau_{4,6}\tau_{1,5})([r_{4,5},r_{2,5}])

\displaystyle\quad=[r_{2,3},r_{2,5}]+[r_{4,5},r_{4,1}]+[r_{6,1},r_{6,3}]+[r_{2,5},r_{4,5}]+[r_{6,3},r_{2,3}]+[r_{4,1},r_{6,1}].

The first assertion with
\epsilon=1
is now immediate by Proposition
2.15
. To prove it for
\epsilon=-1
it suffices to note that
-\tau_{1,2}(r)

is also a classical r-matrix for the same cobracket
\delta
by Lemma
2.12
.
To prove the second assertion, it remains to apply Proposition
2.14
(b)
together with Lemma
2.12
.
∎

Remark 2.17
.

Note that the identity in Proposition
2.15

can be rewritten as

\displaystyle(\id_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{2,3,5}-(\id_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{4,1,5}-(\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{2,4,5}+[\mathbf{f}_{4,1},\mathbf{f}_{4,5}]+[\mathbf{f}_{4,5},\mathbf{f}_{2,5}]+[\mathbf{f}_{2,5},\mathbf{f}_{2,3}]

\displaystyle=(\id_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{6,1,3}-(\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{6,2,3}-(\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(\mathbf{f})_{4,6,1}

\displaystyle\mskip 400.0mu+[\mathbf{f}_{4,1},\mathbf{f}_{6,1}]+[\mathbf{f}_{6,1},\mathbf{f}_{6,3}]+[\mathbf{f}_{6,3},\mathbf{f}_{2,3}].

The left hand side is contained in
(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}}))^{\otimes 2}\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes 1_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}
, while the right hand side is contained in

(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}}))^{\otimes 2}\otimes 1_{U({\mathfrak{g}})}\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})
.
It follows that this reduces to an identity in
(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}}))^{\otimes 2}
.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})
,
({\mathfrak{h}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}})
be
Lie bialgebras and let
j_{{\mathfrak{g}}}
,
j_{{\mathfrak{h}}}

be respective classical Drinfeld twists. Abbreviate
\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}=\widetilde{\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}}}_{j_{{\mathfrak{g}}}}
,

\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{h}}}=\widetilde{\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}}_{j_{{\mathfrak{h}}}}

and let
\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}
be the Lie cobracket on
{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}

obtained from Lie cobrackets
\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}
and
\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{h}}}
.
Let
j_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\in({\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{h}})^{\otimes 2}

and set
j^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}:=j_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}-(j_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}-(j_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4}
. Then

\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}}=\mathbb{\Lbrack}(j^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})^{-},(j^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{h}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}}
.
In particular,
j_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}
is a classical Drinfeld
twist for
({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})
if and only if

j_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}^{\prime}
is
a classical Drinfeld twist for
({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}},\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})
.

Proof.

Note that for all
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
,
y\in{\mathfrak{h}}

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}((x,y))

\displaystyle=\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}(x)_{1,3}+\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{h}}}(y)_{2,4}=\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}}(x)_{1,3}+\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}(y)_{2,4}+[j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}},\Delta(x)]_{1,3}+[j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}},\Delta(y)]_{2,4}

\displaystyle=\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}((x,y))+[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4},\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}((x,y))].

Thus,

\displaystyle(\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}-(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}-(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4})

\displaystyle=(\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})-(\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3,5}-(\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{h}}})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4,6}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4},(\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3},(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,5}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{3,5}]-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4},(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,6}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{4,6}]

and

\displaystyle(\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}

\displaystyle\otimes\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}-(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}-(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4})

\displaystyle=(\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})-(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3,5}-(\id_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4,6}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{3,5}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{4,6},(\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{3,5},(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,5}]-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{4,6},(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{2,6}].

Therefore

\displaystyle((\delta^{\prime}

{}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})-(\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})-\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}}(\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}))((j^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})^{-})

\displaystyle=((\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})-(\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})-\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}}(\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}))(j_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}^{-})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-(((\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})-(\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})-\tau_{2,3}(\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}))(j_{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{-}))_{1,3,5}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-(((\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{h}}})-(\id_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}})-\tau_{2,3}(\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{h}}}))(j_{{\mathfrak{h}}}^{-}))_{2,4,6}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4},(\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3},(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,5}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{3,5}]-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4},(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,6}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{4,6}]]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,5}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,6},\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}}(\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,5},(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}-(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{3,5}]+[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,6},(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4}-(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{4,6}]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{3,5}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{4,6},(\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{3,5},(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,5}]+[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{4,6},(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{2,6}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}}-\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}-(\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}}})_{1,3,5}-(\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}})_{2,4,6}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{1,3,5}-\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{1,3,5}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4},(\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,5}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,6},\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}}(\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{3,5}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{4,6},(\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}}-\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}-\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{1,3,5}-\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{1,3,5}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\mathbb{\Lbrack}(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,5}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,6},(\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,5}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,6},\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}}(\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}},(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4}\mathbb{\Rbrack}-[(\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}),(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,5}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,6}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}}-\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}-(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}-(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}-(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}-(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4}\mathbb{\Rbrack}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,5}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,6},((\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})+\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}}(\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})

\displaystyle\phantom{=-[(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,5}+(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,6},}-(\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}))(j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})].

We need the following

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
{\mathfrak{l}}
be a Lie algebra and let
s\in{\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}
.
Then
((\Delta\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{l}}})+\tau_{2,3}(\Delta\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{l}}})-(\id_{{\mathfrak{l}}}\otimes\Delta))(s^{-})=0
in
U({\mathfrak{l}})^{\otimes 3}
.

Proof.

Indeed,

\displaystyle((\Delta

\displaystyle\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{l}}})+\tau_{2,3}(\Delta\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{l}}})-(\id_{{\mathfrak{l}}}\otimes\Delta))(s^{-})=s^{-}_{1,3}+s^{-}_{2,3}+s^{-}_{1,2}+s^{-}_{3,2}-s^{-}_{1,2}-s^{-}_{1,3}

\displaystyle=s^{-}_{2,3}+s^{-}_{3,2}=0.\qed

It follows from the Lemma applied to
{\mathfrak{l}}={\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}
and
s=j_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}
that

((\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})})-(\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}\otimes\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})-\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}}(\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}})}))((j^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})^{-})\\
=\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}}-\mathbb{\Lbrack}(j^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})^{-},(j^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}

or

\mathbb{\Lbrack}j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}},j^{-}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}}=\mathbb{\Lbrack}(j^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})^{-},(j^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})^{-}\mathbb{\Rbrack}_{\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}}}
,
which is the first assertion of the Proposition. The second is immediate
from the first and the definition of a classical Drinfeld twist.
∎

Corollary 2.20
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}})
and
({\mathfrak{h}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}})
be Lie bialgebras.
Suppose
that
\mathbf{f}\in{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
is a relative classical Drinfeld twist for
({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})
and that
\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}}
(respectively,
\delta_{{\mathfrak{h}}}
)
is obtained by twisting another cobracket
\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}
(respectively,
\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{h}}})
by some classical Drinfeld twist
j_{{\mathfrak{g}}}
(respectively,
j_{{\mathfrak{h}}}
). Then
\mathbf{f}_{2,3}+(j_{{\mathfrak{g}}})_{1,3}+(j_{{\mathfrak{h}}})_{2,4}

a Drinfeld twist for
({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}},\delta^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}})
.

2.8.
The dual picture: Poisson algebras

Consider
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
with the algebra structure defined by the convolution product.
The natural left and right
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-actions on
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
are given by

(x\triangleright f)(u)=f(ux),\quad(f\triangleleft x)(u)=f(xu)

for all
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
,
f\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
and
u\in U({\mathfrak{g}})
.
For an algebraic group
G
with
\operatorname{Lie}(G)={\mathfrak{g}}
,

U({\mathfrak{g}})
and
\mathbb{k}[G]
admit a natural Hopf pairing via

\langle u,f\rangle=(u\triangleright f)(1_{G})
and so
\mathbb{k}[G]
identifies
with a subalgebra of
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
. The natural left
and right actions of
{\mathfrak{g}}
on
\mathbb{k}[G]
are given, respectively, by

(2.13)

(x\triangleright f)(g)=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}f(g\exp(tx)),\qquad(f\triangleleft x)(g)=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}f(\exp(tx)g)

for all
f\in\mathbb{k}[G]
,
g\in G
and
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
.

If
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
is a Lie bialgebra then
\delta
induces a Poisson structure on
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}

via

\{f,f^{\prime}\}:=(f\otimes f^{\prime})\circ\delta,\qquad f,f^{\prime}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}.

Indeed,
(

⁢
L

B
2

)
and
(

⁢
L

B
3

)
imply that
\{\cdot,\cdot\}

is skew-symmetric and satisfies the Jacobi identity. Furthermore, for all

f,f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}

we have by Lemma
2.8

\displaystyle\{f\cdot f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime}\}

\displaystyle=(f\cdot f^{\prime}\otimes f^{\prime\prime})\circ\delta=(f\otimes f^{\prime}\otimes f^{\prime\prime})\circ(\Delta\otimes\id_{U({\mathfrak{g}})})\circ\delta

\displaystyle=(f\otimes f^{\prime}\otimes f^{\prime\prime})\circ(\id\otimes\delta)\circ\Delta+(f\otimes f^{\prime\prime}\otimes f^{\prime})\circ(\delta\otimes\id)\circ\Delta

\displaystyle=f\cdot\{f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime}\}+\{f,f^{\prime\prime}\}\cdot f^{\prime}.

Proposition 2.21
.

Let
