({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
be a Lie bialgebra and let
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
be
the Poisson bracket on
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
induced by
\delta
.
Let
j\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}

be a weak classical Drinfeld twist. Then
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{j}:U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}\to U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
given by

(2.14)

\{f,f^{\prime}\}_{j}=\{f,f^{\prime}\}+\mu(j^{-}\bowtie(f\otimes f^{\prime})),\qquad f,f^{\prime}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*},

where
\mu:U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}\to U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
is the multiplication map and

(x\otimes y)\bowtie(f\otimes f^{\prime}):=(f\triangleleft x)\otimes(f^{\prime}\triangleleft y)-(x\triangleright f)\otimes(y\triangleright f^{\prime}),\quad f,f^{\prime}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*},\,x,y\in{\mathfrak{g}},

is a Poisson bracket on
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
. In particular, if
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)

is quasi-triangular with a classical r-matrix
r
then

\{f,f^{\prime}\}_{j}=\mu(((r+j^{-})\bowtie(f\otimes f^{\prime})),\qquad f,f^{\prime}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}

and restricts to a Poisson bracket on
\mathbb{k}[G]
.

Proof.

We need the following

Lemma 2.22
.

Let
r\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
. Then

(f\otimes f^{\prime})\circ\delta_{r}=\mu(r\bowtie(f\otimes f^{\prime})),\quad f,f^{\prime}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}.

Proof.

For any
u\in U
,

\displaystyle(f\otimes f^{\prime})

\displaystyle(\delta_{r}(u))=(f\otimes f^{\prime})(r\cdot\Delta(u)-\Delta(u)\cdot r)

\displaystyle=((f\otimes f^{\prime})\triangleleft r)(\Delta(u))-(r\triangleright(f\otimes f^{\prime}))(\Delta(u))=\mu(r\bowtie(f\otimes f^{\prime}))(u).\qed

Since
j
is a weak classical Drinfeld twist,
\tilde{\delta}_{j}=\delta+\delta_{j^{-}}
is a cobracket and hence induces a Poisson bracket on
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
. We claim that this bracket coincides with
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{j}
.
Indeed,
(f\otimes f^{\prime})\circ\tilde{\delta}_{j}=(f\otimes f^{\prime})\circ\delta+(f\otimes f^{\prime})\circ\delta_{j^{-}}=\{f,f^{\prime}\}+(f\otimes f^{\prime})\circ\delta_{j^{-}}
for all
f,f^{\prime}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
, and it remains to
apply the Lemma. The second assertion follows from the first and Proposition
2.14
(b)
.
∎

2.9.
Quasi-triangular bialgebras

Let
B
be a bialgebra. Recall (see e.g.
[
13
,
11
,
33
,
29
]
) that
B
is called quasi-triangular if there is an invertible element
R

in a (suitable completion
B\widehat{\otimes}B
of)
B\otimes B
satisfying

\Delta^{op}(b)=R\Delta(b)R^{-1},\qquad b\in B

where
\Delta^{op}=\tau_{1,2}\circ\Delta
,
and

(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(R)=R_{13}R_{23},\quad(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta)(R)=R_{13}R_{12}.

Note that
(\varepsilon_{B}\otimes\id_{B})(R),(\id_{B}\otimes\varepsilon_{B})(R)\in Z(B)
.
It is well-known (see e.g.
[
33
]
*Proposition 2.2) that if
B
is quasi triangular then

R
satisfies the quantum Yang-Baxter equation (QYBE)

(2.15)

R_{12}R_{13}R_{23}=R_{23}R_{13}R_{12}.

More generally, we have the following

Proposition 2.23
.

Let
C
be a set and let

\{R^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}
be a family of R-matrices for the
same bialgebra
(B,\Delta)
. Then for all
i\not=j\not=k\in[n]

(2.16)

R_{jk}^{(c)}R_{ik}^{(c^{\prime})}R_{ij}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}=R_{ij}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}R_{ik}^{(c^{\prime})}R_{jk}^{(c)},\qquad c^{\prime}\in\{c,c^{\prime\prime}\}\subset C.

Proof.

Let
c,c^{\prime\prime}\in C
. Since the symmetric group
S_{n}

acts on
B^{\otimes n}
by algebra automorphisms, it suffices to prove (
2.16
)
for
(i,j,k)=(1,2,3)
.
We have

\displaystyle R_{23}^{(c)}R_{13}^{(c)}

\displaystyle=\tau_{1,2}(R_{13}^{(c)}R_{23}^{(c)})=\tau_{1,2}((\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(R^{(c)}))

\displaystyle=R_{12}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(R^{(c)})(R_{12}^{(c^{\prime\prime})})^{-1}=R_{12}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}R_{13}^{(c)}R_{23}^{(c)}(R_{12}^{(c^{\prime\prime})})^{-1},

which implies (
2.16
) for
c^{\prime}=c
. The identity for
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime\prime}
is proved similarly by using
\id_{B}\otimes\Delta^{op}
.
∎

The most basic example of such a family is provided by the following

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
B
be a quasi-triangular bialgebra with an R-matrix
R
.
Then
\tau_{1,2}(R)^{-1}
is also an R-matrix for the same comultiplication. In particular, if
R^{(1)}=R
and
R^{(-1)}=\tau_{1,2}(R)^{-1}
then

R_{23}^{(\epsilon)}R_{13}^{(\epsilon^{\prime})}R_{12}^{(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})}=R_{12}^{(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})}R_{13}^{(\epsilon^{\prime})}R_{23}^{(\epsilon)}

provided that
\epsilon^{\prime}\in\{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\}\subset\{1,-1\}
.

Proof.

Let
b\in B
. Then

\tau_{1,2}(R)^{-1}\Delta(b)=\tau_{1,2}(R^{-1}\Delta^{op}(b))=\tau_{1,2}(\Delta(R^{-1}))=\Delta^{op}(b)\tau_{1,2}(R)^{-1}.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(\tau_{1,2}(R)^{-1})

\displaystyle=((\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(\tau_{1,2}(R))^{-1}=(\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta)(R))^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}(R_{12}^{-1}R_{13}^{-1})=(\tau_{1,2}(R)^{-1})_{12}(\tau_{1,2}(R)^{-1})_{23}.

The remaining identity is proved similarly.
∎

Lemma 2.25
.

Let
B
be a quasi-triangular bialgebra with an R-matrix
R
and let
\sigma:B\to B
be a bialgebra automorphism. Then
(\sigma\otimes\sigma)(R)

is also an R-matrix for
B
.

Proof.

Let
R^{\sigma}=(\sigma\otimes\sigma)(R)
.
Then for any
b\in B

\displaystyle R^{\sigma}

\displaystyle\Delta(b)(R^{\sigma})^{-1}=(\sigma\otimes\sigma)(R\Delta(\sigma^{-1}(b))R^{-1})=(\sigma\otimes\sigma)\Delta^{op}(\sigma^{-1}(b))=\Delta^{op}(b),

where we used Lemma
2.5
.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(R^{\sigma})=(\sigma\otimes\sigma\otimes\sigma)((\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(R))=(\sigma\otimes\sigma\otimes\sigma)(R_{13}R_{23})=R_{13}^{\sigma}R_{23}^{\sigma},

and similarly for the remaining identity.
∎

2.10.
Drinfeld twists

Let
B
be a bialgebra with the comultiplication
\Delta
. We say
that an invertible
J\in B\widehat{\otimes}B
is a (right)
weak Drinfeld twist
if
\Delta_{J}:B\to B\widehat{\otimes}B
, where
\Delta_{J}(b)=J^{-1}\Delta(b)J
,
b\in B
,
is a (topological)
comultiplication.

We will need the following standard facts (see for example
[
29
]
*Theorem 2.3.4). We provide proofs here for the reader’s convenience
and to introduce the notation that will be used later.

Proposition 2.26
.

Let
B
be a bialgebra with the comultiplication
\Delta:B\to B\otimes B
and the counit
\varepsilon:B\to\mathbb{k}
.
Let
J
be an invertible element of (a suitable completion

B\widehat{\otimes}B
of)
B\otimes B
. Then

(a)

J
is a weak Drinfeld twist if and only if

(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J)\cdot(J\otimes J^{-1})\cdot(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta)(J^{-1})

centralizes
(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})\Delta(B)

in
B^{\otimes 3}

and
(\varepsilon\otimes\id_{B})(J),(\id_{B}\otimes\varepsilon)(J)\in Z(B)
.
In particular, if
(\varepsilon\otimes\id_{B})(J),(\id_{B}\otimes\varepsilon)(J)\in Z(B)
and

(2.17)

(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J)\cdot(J\otimes 1)=(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta)(J)\cdot(1\otimes J)

then
J
is a weak Drinfeld twist.

(b)

Suppose that
B
is quasi-triangular with an R-matrix
R
.
If
J
satisfies (
2.17
)
then
B
is a (topological) quasi-triangular bialgebra with respect to
\Delta_{J}
with the R-matrix
R_{J}=J_{21}^{-1}RJ
.

(c)

Let
\sigma
be a bialgebra automorphism of
B
. If
J

satisfies (
2.17
) then so does
(\sigma\otimes\sigma)(J)
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\Delta_{J}
is a homomorphism of algebras.
Abbreviate
J_{12,3}=(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J)
and
J_{1,23}=(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta)(J)
. In this notation (
2.17
) becomes

(2.18)

J_{12,3}J_{12}=J_{1,23}J_{23}.

We have, for all
x\in B

\displaystyle(\Delta_{J}\otimes

\displaystyle\id_{B})\Delta_{J}(x)=(\Delta_{J}\otimes\id_{H})(J^{-1}\Delta(x)J)

\displaystyle=(J^{-1}\otimes 1)\cdot(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J^{-1}\Delta_{H}(h)J)\cdot(J\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=(J^{-1}\otimes 1)\cdot((\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J^{-1}))\cdot(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})\Delta(x)\cdot(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J)(J\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=J_{12}^{-1}J_{12,3}^{-1}(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})\Delta(x)J_{12,3}J_{12}

and similarly

\displaystyle(\id_{B}\otimes

\displaystyle\Delta_{J})\Delta_{J}(x)=(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta_{J})(J^{-1}\Delta(x)J)

\displaystyle=(1\otimes J^{-1})\cdot(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta)(J^{-1}\Delta(x)J)\cdot(1\otimes J)

\displaystyle=(1\otimes J^{-1})\cdot((\id_{B}\otimes\Delta)(J^{-1}))\cdot(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta)\Delta(x)\cdot(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta)(J)(1\otimes J)

\displaystyle=J_{23}^{-1}J_{1,23}^{-1}(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta)\Delta(x)J_{1,23}J_{23}.

Since
\Delta
is coassociative, it follows that
\Delta_{J}

is coassociative if and only
J_{12,3}J_{12}J_{23}^{-1}J_{1,23}^{-1}

commutes with
(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})\Delta(x)

for all
x\in B
.
We also have, for all
x\in B
,

(\varepsilon\otimes\id_{B})(J^{-1}\Delta(x)J)=(\varepsilon\otimes\id_{B})(J^{-1})x(\varepsilon\otimes\id_{B})(J)
, which is equivalent to
(\varepsilon\otimes\id_{B})(J)\in Z(B)
. The remaining
identity is proven similarly.

To prove
(b)
, note first that, for all
x\in B
,

\displaystyle R_{J}\Delta_{J}(x)

\displaystyle=J_{21}{}^{-1}R\Delta(x)J=J_{21}^{-1}\Delta^{op}(x)RJ=\Delta^{op}_{J}(x)R_{J}.

Furthermore, we need to prove that

(2.19)

(\Delta_{J}\otimes\id_{B})(R_{J})=(R_{J})_{13}(R_{J})_{23},\quad(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta_{J})(R_{J})=(R_{J})_{13}(R_{J})_{12}.

We have

\displaystyle(\Delta_{J}\otimes\id_{B})(R_{J})

\displaystyle=J_{12}^{-1}(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J_{21}^{-1}RJ)J_{12}=J_{12}^{-1}(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J_{21}^{-1})R_{13}R_{23}J_{12,3}J_{12}

\displaystyle=J_{12}^{-1}(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J_{21}^{-1})R_{13}R_{23}J_{1,23}J_{23}

while
(R_{J})_{13}(R_{J})_{23}=J_{31}^{-1}R_{13}J_{13}J_{32}^{-1}R_{23}J_{23}
.
Thus, the first identity in (
2.19
) is equivalent to

J_{12}^{-1}(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J_{21}^{-1})R_{13}R_{23}J_{1,23}=J_{31}^{-1}R_{13}J_{13}J_{32}^{-1}R_{23}

or

(2.20)

J_{12}^{-1}((\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J_{21}))^{-1}R_{13}J_{1,32}=J_{31}^{-1}R_{13}J_{13}J_{32}^{-1}

where
J_{1,32}:=(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta^{op})(J)
; here we used that
R\Delta(x)R^{-1}=\Delta^{op}(x)
for all
x\in B
.
We also have, by (
2.18
)

J_{1,32}J_{32}=\tau_{2,3}(J_{1,23}J_{23})=\tau_{2,3}(J_{12,3}J_{12})=\tau_{2,3}(J_{12,3})J_{13}
,
whence (
2.20
) is equivalent to

J_{12}^{-1}((\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J_{21}))^{-1}R_{13}\tau_{2,3}(J_{12,3})=J_{31}^{-1}R_{13}

Since
R_{13}=\tau_{2,3}(R_{12})
,
the last identity is equivalent to

J_{12}^{-1}((\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J_{21}))^{-1}\tau_{2,3}(R_{12}J_{12,3}R_{12}^{-1})=J_{31}^{-1}

or

J_{12}^{-1}((\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J_{21}))^{-1}\tau_{2,3}(J_{21,3})=J_{31}^{-1}

where
J_{21,3}=(\Delta^{op}\otimes\id_{B})(J)=\tau_{1,2}(J_{12,3})
.
This in turn is equivalent to

(2.21)

\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}(J_{12,3})J_{31}=(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J_{21})J_{12}.

Furthermore,

(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J_{21})=\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}((\id_{B}\otimes\Delta)(J))=\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}(J_{1,23})
,
and so (
2.21
) is equivalent to

\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}(J_{12,3})J_{31}=\tau_{2,3}\tau_{1,2}(J_{1,23})J_{12}.

Applying
\tau_{1,2}\tau_{2,3}
to both sides we conclude that (
2.21
) and hence
the first identity in (
2.19
) are equivalent to

J_{12,3}\tau_{1,2}\tau_{2,3}(J_{31})=J_{1,23}\tau_{1,2}\tau_{2,3}(J_{12})

which is manifestly equivalent
to (
2.18
). The argument for the second identity in (
2.19
) is similar and is omitted.

To prove part
(c)
, denote
J^{\sigma}=(\sigma\otimes\sigma)(J)
. Then

(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J^{\sigma})\cdot(J^{\sigma}\otimes 1)=(\sigma\otimes\sigma\otimes\sigma)((\Delta\otimes\id_{B})(J)\cdot(J\otimes 1)).

Similarly,

(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta)(J^{\sigma})\cdot(1\otimes J^{\sigma})=(\sigma\otimes\sigma\otimes\sigma)((\id_{H}\otimes\Delta)(J)\cdot(1\otimes J)).

Thus,
J^{\sigma}
satisfies (
2.17
).
Furthermore,
(\varepsilon\otimes\id_{B})(J^{\sigma})=(\varepsilon\circ\sigma\otimes\sigma)(J)=\sigma((\varepsilon\otimes\id_{B})(J))
which is central in
B
since
\sigma
is an automorphism of
B
and
(\varepsilon\otimes\id_{B})(J)
is central.
∎

Remark 2.27
.

In the literature, the term “Drinfeld twist” is also used for the
left
Drinfeld twist, that is, an invertible element in (a suitable completion of)
B\otimes B
such that
\Delta^{\prime}_{J}:B\to B\otimes B
defined by

\Delta^{\prime}_{J}(b)=J\Delta(b)J^{-1}
,
b\in B
, is a comultiplication.
Clearly,
J
is a (right) Drinfeld twist if and only if
J^{-1}

is a left Drinfeld twist, and if
B
is quasi-triangular
with an R-matrix
R
then
R
is a left Drinfeld twist.

From now on, we refer to a weak Drinfeld twist satisfying (
2.17
) as a
Drinfeld twist
.

2.11.
Relative Drinfeld twists

Let
A
and
B
be bialgebras with respective comultiplications
\Delta_{A}
and
\Delta_{B}
and
let
F
in (a suitable completion
B\widehat{\otimes}A
of)
B\otimes A
be invertible. We say that
F
is a
relative Drinfeld twist
if
1_{A}\otimes F\otimes 1_{B}
is a Drinfeld twist for
A\otimes B
with respect to its standard comultiplication
\Delta_{A\otimes B}
.
The following is a quantum analogue of Proposition
2.15
.

Proposition 2.28
.

Let
(A,\Delta_{A})
,
(B,\Delta_{B})
be
bialgebras and let
F\in B\otimes A
be invertible. Then
F

is a relative Drinfeld twist if and only if

(\varepsilon_{B}\otimes\id_{A})(F)\in Z(A)
,
(\id_{B}\otimes\varepsilon_{A})(F)\in Z(B)

and

(2.22)

F_{13,4}F_{12}=F_{1,24}F_{34}

in
B\otimes A\otimes B\otimes A
where
F_{13,4}=[(\Delta_{B}\otimes\id_{A})(F)]_{1,3,4}
and
F_{1,24}=[(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta_{A})(F)]_{1,2,4}
.

Proof.

The identity

(\Delta_{A\otimes B}\otimes\id_{A\otimes B})(F_{23})(F_{23}\otimes 1_{A\otimes B})=(\id_{A\otimes B}\otimes\Delta_{A\otimes B})(F_{23})(1_{A\otimes B}\otimes F_{23})

in
(A\otimes B)^{\otimes 3}
is equivalent to

[(\Delta_{B}\otimes\id_{A})(F)]_{2,4,5}F_{23}=[(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta_{A})(F)]_{2,3,5}F_{45}

where both sides are in
1_{A}\otimes B\otimes A\otimes B\otimes A\otimes 1_{B}\subset(A\otimes B)^{\otimes 3}
. This
is clearly equivalent to (
2.22
). The remaining assertions are straightforward.
∎

Note that
F_{12}\in B\otimes A\otimes 1_{B}\otimes 1_{A}
and
F_{34}\in 1_{B}\otimes 1_{A}\otimes B\otimes A
commute in
(B\otimes A)^{\otimes 2}
. Thus, (
2.22
) is equivalent to

(2.23)

F_{13,4}F_{34}^{-1}=F_{1,24}F_{12}^{-1}

It follows that if
F
is a relative Drinfeld twist then
F_{13,4}F_{34}^{-1}
and

F_{1,24}F_{12}^{-1}
are in
B\otimes 1_{A}\otimes 1_{B}\otimes A
and thus (
2.23
) is essentially an identity in
B\otimes A
. It should be noted that, by Remark
2.17
, the corresponding identity in the classical case encompasses the entire
(U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{h}}))^{\otimes 2}
and cannot be collapsed to just two of its tensor factors.

The following recovers
[
33
]
*Theorem 2.9 and (2.26).

Proposition 2.29
.

Let
B
be quasi-triangular with an R-matrix
R
. Then

J_{\epsilon}=1\otimes R^{(\epsilon)}\otimes 1
,
\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}
in
the notation from Lemma
2.24
, is a Drinfeld twist for
B^{\otimes 2}\otimes B^{\otimes 2}
. In particular, for any
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\in\{1,-1\}
,

B^{\otimes 2}

with the comultiplication twisted by
J_{\epsilon}
is quasi-triangular
with the R-matrix
R_{14}^{(-\epsilon)}R_{13}^{(\epsilon^{\prime})}R_{24}^{(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})}R_{23}^{(\epsilon)}
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon=1
.
We apply Proposition
2.28
with
A=B
and
F=R
. Since

F_{13,4}=(R_{13}R_{23})_{(1,3,4)}=R_{14}R_{34}
, while
F_{1,24}=(R_{13}R_{12})_{(1,2,4)}=R_{14}R_{12}
,
it follows that

F_{13,4}F_{34}^{-1}=R_{14}=F_{1,24}F_{12}^{-1}

and so (
2.23
) holds.
Since
(\varepsilon\otimes\id_{B})(R)=(\id_{B}\otimes\varepsilon)(R)
are central, the second condition in Proposition
2.28
follows. It remains to apply Proposition
2.26
(b)
and Lemma
2.24
,
taking into account that in this case
J_{\mathbf{21}}=J_{(3,4,1,2)}
is obtained from
J
by
applying
\tau_{1,3}\tau_{2,4}
. The case
\epsilon=-1
is similar with
R
replaced
by
R_{21}^{-1}
, which is also an R-matrix for
B
by Lemma
2.24
.
∎

Remark 2.30
.

Taking
\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime\prime}=1
,
\epsilon^{\prime}=-1
yields
[
33
]
*(2.6).

The next two results are quantum analogues of, respectively, Proposition
2.18
and
Corollary
2.20
.

Lemma 2.31
.

Let
A
(respectively,
B
) be unital bialgebras with
respective comultiplications
\Delta_{A}
,
\Delta_{B}
. Let
J_{A}
,
J_{B}
be Drinfeld twists on
(A,\Delta_{A})
,
(B,\Delta_{B})
, respectively. Let
\Delta^{\prime}_{A}
,
\Delta^{\prime}_{B}
be comultiplications
twisted by, respectively,
J_{A}
and
J_{B}
.
Then

J_{A\otimes B}
is a Drinfeld twist for
(A\otimes B,\Delta_{A\otimes B})
if and only if
J^{\prime}_{A\otimes B}:=(J_{A})_{1,3}^{-1}(J_{B})_{2,4}^{-1}J_{A\otimes B}
is a Drinfeld twist for
(A\otimes B,\Delta^{\prime}_{A\otimes B})
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle(\Delta^{\prime}

{}_{A\otimes B}\otimes\id_{A\otimes B})(J^{\prime}_{A\otimes B})(J^{\prime}_{A\otimes B}\otimes 1_{A}\otimes 1_{B})

\displaystyle=(J_{A}^{-1})_{1,3}(J_{B}^{-1})_{2,4}(\Delta_{A\otimes B}\otimes\id_{A\otimes B})((J_{A}^{-1})_{1,3}(J_{B}^{-1})_{2,4}J_{A\otimes B})(J_{A\otimes B}\otimes 1_{A}\otimes 1_{B})

\displaystyle=[(J_{A}^{-1}\otimes 1_{A})\cdot(\Delta_{A}\otimes\id_{A})(J_{A}^{-1})]_{(1,3,5)}[(J_{B}^{-1}\otimes 1_{B})\cdot(\Delta_{B}\otimes\id_{B})(J_{B}^{-1})]_{(2,4,6)}\cdot

\displaystyle\mskip 300.0mu(\Delta_{A\otimes B}\otimes\id_{A\otimes B})(J_{A\otimes B})(J_{A\otimes B}\otimes 1_{A}\otimes 1_{B})

\displaystyle=[(1_{A}\otimes J_{A}^{-1})\cdot(\id_{A}\otimes\Delta_{A})(J_{A}^{-1})]_{(1,3,5)}[(1_{B}\otimes J_{B}^{-1})\cdot(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta_{B})(J_{B}^{-1})]_{(2,4,6)}\cdot

\displaystyle\mskip 300.0mu(\id_{A\otimes B}\otimes\Delta_{A\otimes B})(J_{A\otimes B})(1_{A}\otimes 1_{B}\otimes J_{A\otimes B})

\displaystyle=[(1_{A}\otimes J_{A}^{-1})\cdot(\id_{A}\otimes\Delta_{A})(J_{A}^{-1})]_{(1,3,5)}[(1_{B}\otimes J_{B}^{-1})\cdot(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta_{B})(J_{B}^{-1})]_{(2,4,6)}\cdot

\displaystyle\mskip 300.0mu(\id_{A\otimes B}\otimes\Delta_{A\otimes B})(J_{A\otimes B})(1_{A}\otimes 1_{B}\otimes J_{A\otimes B})

\displaystyle=(J_{A}^{-1})_{3,5}(J_{B}^{-1})_{4,6}(\id_{A\otimes B}\otimes\Delta_{A\otimes B})(J^{\prime}_{A\otimes B})(1_{A}\otimes 1_{B}\otimes J_{A\otimes B})

\displaystyle=(J_{A}^{-1})_{3,5}(J_{B}^{-1})_{4,6}(\id_{A\otimes B}\otimes\Delta_{A\otimes B})(J^{\prime}_{A\otimes B})(J_{A})_{3,5}(J_{B})_{4,6}(1_{A}\otimes 1_{B}\otimes J^{\prime}_{A\otimes B})

\displaystyle=(\id_{A\otimes B}\otimes\Delta^{\prime}_{A\otimes B})(J^{\prime}_{A\otimes B})(1_{A}\otimes 1_{B}\otimes J^{\prime}_{A\otimes B}),

where we used (
2.17
) for
J_{A}
,
J_{B}

and
J_{A\otimes B}
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{A\otimes B}

\displaystyle\otimes\id_{A\otimes B})(J^{\prime}_{A\otimes B})=((\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\id_{A})(J_{A})\otimes(\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\id_{B})(J_{B}))\cdot

\displaystyle\mskip 300.0mu(\varepsilon_{A}\otimes\varepsilon_{B}\otimes\id_{A}\otimes\id_{B})(J_{A\otimes B}).

The first factor is contained in
Z(A)\otimes Z(B)\subset Z(A\otimes B)
while the second is contained in
Z(A\otimes B)
.
The assertion for
\id_{A\otimes B}\otimes\varepsilon_{A\otimes B}
is proved similarly. The converse follows by interchanging the role of
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
and using Remark
2.27
.
∎

Corollary 2.32
.

Let
A
and
B
be bialgebras with respective comultiplications
\Delta_{A}:A\to A\otimes A
and
\Delta_{B}:B\to B\otimes B
.
Suppose
that
F\in B\otimes A
is a relative Drinfeld twist for
(A\otimes B,\Delta_{A\otimes B})
and that
\Delta_{A}
(respectively,
\Delta_{B}
)
is obtained by twisting another comultiplication
\Delta^{\prime}_{A}:A\to A\otimes A
(respectively,
\Delta^{\prime}_{B}:B\to B\otimes B
)
by some Drinfeld twist
J_{A}\in A\otimes A
(respectively,
J_{B}\in B\otimes B
).
Then
(J_{A})_{13}(J_{B})_{24}F_{23}
is a Drinfeld twist for
((A\otimes B)^{\otimes 2},\Delta^{\prime}_{A\otimes B})
where
\Delta^{\prime}_{A\otimes B}=(\Delta^{\prime}_{A})_{1,3}\circ(\Delta^{\prime}_{B})_{2,4}
.

We conclude this section with a natural generalization
of the defining property of an R-matrix.

Proposition 2.33
.

Let
A
,
B
be bialgebras with respective comultiplications
\Delta_{A}
,
\Delta_{B}

and let
F\in B\widehat{\otimes}A
be a relative Drinfeld twist. Let
U

be a coalgebra with the comultiplication
\Delta_{U}

and let
\psi_{A}:U\to A
,
\psi_{B}:U\to B
be homomorphisms of coalgebras. Then
(\psi_{A}\otimes\psi_{B})\circ\Delta_{U}:U\to A\otimes B
is a homomorphism of coalgebras,
where the natural comultiplication on
A\otimes B
is twisted by
F_{2,3}
, if and only if

F\cdot((\psi_{B}\otimes\psi_{A})\circ\Delta_{U})(u))=(\tau\circ(\psi_{A}\otimes\psi_{B})\circ\Delta_{U}(u))\cdot F,\qquad u\in U.

Proof.

Abbreviate
\boldsymbol{\Delta}=(\psi_{A}\otimes\psi_{B})\circ\Delta_{U}
. Then
\boldsymbol{\Delta}:U\to A\otimes B
is
a homomorphism of coalgebras if and only if

(2.24)

(\Delta_{A\otimes B}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}(u)))\cdot F_{2,3}=F_{2,3}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\Delta}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Delta})(\Delta_{U}(u)),\qquad u\in U.

Since

\displaystyle\Delta_{A\otimes B}(\boldsymbol{\Delta}(u))

\displaystyle=\Delta_{A\otimes B}(\psi_{A}(u_{(1)})\otimes\psi_{B}(u_{(2)}))=\Delta_{A}(\psi_{A}(u_{(1)}))_{1,3}\cdot\Delta_{B}(\psi_{B}(u_{(2)}))_{2,4}

\displaystyle=(\psi_{A}\otimes\psi_{A})(u_{(1)}\otimes u_{(2)})_{1,3}\cdot(\psi_{B}\otimes\psi_{B})(u_{(3)}\otimes u_{(4)})_{2,4}

\displaystyle=\psi_{A}(u_{(1)})\otimes\psi_{B}(u_{(3)})\otimes\psi_{A}(u_{(2)})\otimes\psi_{B}(u_{(4)}),

while

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\Delta}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Delta})(\Delta_{U}(u))

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Delta}(u_{(1)})\otimes\boldsymbol{\Delta}(u_{(2)})=\psi_{A}(u_{(1)})\otimes\psi_{B}(u_{(2)})\otimes\psi_{A}(u_{(3)})\otimes\psi_{B}(u_{(4)}),

it follows that (
2.24
) is equivalent to

F\cdot(\psi_{B}(u_{(1)})\otimes\psi_{A}(u_{(2)}))=(\psi_{B}(u_{(2)})\otimes\psi_{A}(u_{(1)}))\cdot F,\qquad u\in U

which is the assertion.
∎

2.12.
Duality in the quantum case

In this section we go over the dual analogues
of various constructions discussed above. Since most
arguments are very similar to those in previous
quantum sections, we omit most of them for the sake of brevity.

We say that

\mathcal{R}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes B,\mathbb{k})
is a

co-quasi-triangular structure
on a bialgebra
B

if
\mathcal{R}
is
\ast
-invertible
and

(2.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(a_{(1)},a^{\prime}_{(1)})a_{(2)}a^{\prime}_{(2)}=\mathcal{R}(a_{(2)},a^{\prime}_{(2)})a^{\prime}_{(1)}a_{(1)},\qquad a,a^{\prime}\in B

(2.26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\circ(m_{B}\otimes\id_{B})=\mathcal{R}_{1,3}\ast\mathcal{R}_{2,3},\quad\mathcal{R}\circ(\id_{B}\otimes m_{B})=\mathcal{R}_{1,3}\ast\mathcal{R}_{1,2},

where
m_{B}:B\otimes B\to B
is the multiplication map. Henceforth we will often use the “bilinear form” notation for elements of
\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes B,\mathbb{k})
.
A particularly important case is when
\mathcal{R}

is
counital
, that is

(2.27)

\mathcal{R}(a,1)=\varepsilon(a)=\mathcal{R}(1,a),\qquad a\in B,

which holds, for example, for quantized coordinate algebras on
reductive groups.
The following is the co-quasi-triangular
analogue of Proposition
2.23
.

Proposition 2.34
.

Let
\{\mathcal{R}^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}\subset\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes B,\mathbb{k})
be a family
of co-quasi-triangular structures on
B
.
Then for all
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}

(2.28)

\mathcal{R}_{i,j}^{(c)}\ast\mathcal{R}_{i,k}^{(c^{\prime})}\ast\mathcal{R}_{j,k}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}=\mathcal{R}_{j,k}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}\ast\mathcal{R}_{i,k}^{(c^{\prime})}\ast\mathcal{R}_{i,j}^{(c)},\qquad c^{\prime}\in\{c,c^{\prime\prime}\}\subset C.

Proof.

As before, it suffices to prove (
2.28
) for
(i,j,k)=(1,2,3)
.
Let
a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\in B
.
Applying
\mathcal{R}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(-,a^{\prime\prime})

to the left hand side of (
2.25
)
with
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{(c)}
yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(c)}(

\displaystyle a_{(1)},a^{\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{R}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(a_{(2)}a^{\prime}_{(2)},a^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{R}^{(c)}(a_{(1)},a^{\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{R}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(a_{(2)},a^{\prime\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{R}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(a^{\prime}_{(2)},a^{\prime\prime}_{(2)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{1,2}^{(c)}(a_{(1)}\otimes a^{\prime}_{(1)}\otimes a^{\prime\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{R}_{1,3}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(a_{(2)}\otimes a^{\prime}_{(2)}\otimes a^{\prime\prime}_{(2)})\mathcal{R}_{2,3}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(a_{(3)}\otimes a^{\prime}_{(3)}\otimes a^{\prime\prime}_{(3)})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{R}_{1,2}^{(c)}\ast\mathcal{R}_{1,3}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}\ast\mathcal{R}_{2,3}^{(c^{\prime\prime})})(a\otimes a^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime\prime}),

where we used the first equality in (
2.26
).
On the other hand, applying
\mathcal{R}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(-,a^{\prime\prime})

to the right hand side of (
2.25
)
with the same convention
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(c)}(

\displaystyle a_{(2)},a^{\prime}_{(2)})\mathcal{R}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(a^{\prime}_{(1)}a_{(1)},a^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{R}^{(c)}(a_{(2)},a^{\prime}_{(2)})\mathcal{R}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(a^{\prime}_{(1)},a^{\prime\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{R}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(a_{(1)},a^{\prime\prime}_{(2)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{2,3}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(a_{(1)}\otimes a^{\prime}_{(1)}\otimes a^{\prime\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{R}_{1,3}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}(a_{(2)}\otimes a^{\prime}_{(2)}\otimes a^{\prime\prime}_{(2)})\mathcal{R}_{1,2}^{(c)}(a_{(3)}\otimes a^{\prime}_{(3)}\otimes a^{\prime\prime}_{(3)})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{R}_{2,3}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}\ast\mathcal{R}_{1,3}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}\ast\mathcal{R}_{1,2}^{(c)})(a\otimes a^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime\prime}),

which yields (
2.28
) with
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime\prime}
. The
case
c=c^{\prime}
is proven similarly
by applying
\mathcal{R}^{(c)}(a^{\prime\prime},-)
to both sides of (
2.25
) with
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}
and using
the second equality in (
2.26
).
∎

Like in the case of R-matrices, the basic example of such a family is provided by

Lemma 2.35
.

Let
B
be a bialgebra and let
\mathcal{R}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes B,\mathbb{k})
be a co-quasi-triangular
structure. Then
\mathcal{R}^{(-1)}:=\mathcal{R}^{\ast-1}\circ\tau
is
also a co-quasi-triangular structure.

Proof.

Using (
2.25
) with
a
and
a^{\prime}

interchanged and
Lemma
2.7
with
C=B\otimes B
,

\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{R}
,
V=B
,

f=m_{B}
and
g=m_{B}\circ\tau
we obtain for all
a,a^{\prime}\in B
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\ast-1}(a^{\prime}_{(2)},a_{(2)})a^{\prime}_{(1)}a_{(1)}=\mathcal{R}^{\ast-1}(a^{\prime}_{(1)},a_{(1)})a_{(2)}a^{\prime}_{(2)},

which is (
2.25
) for
\mathcal{R}^{(-1)}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{R}^{(-1)}_{1,3}\ast\mathcal{R}^{(-1)}_{2,3}=(\mathcal{R}^{\ast-1}_{1,2}\ast\mathcal{R}^{\ast-1}_{1,3})\circ\tau_{1,2}\circ\tau_{2,3}=(\mathcal{R}_{1,3}\ast\mathcal{R}_{1,2})^{\ast-1}\circ\tau_{1,2}\circ\tau_{2,3}
, and so to prove
that
\mathcal{R}^{(-1)}
satisfies the first
identity in (
2.26
),
it suffices
to prove that
\mathcal{R}^{(-1)}\circ(m_{B}\otimes\id_{B})\circ\tau_{2,3}\circ\tau_{1,2}=(\mathcal{R}_{1,2}\ast\mathcal{R}_{1,3})^{\ast-1}
, or,
equivalently, that
\mathcal{R}^{\ast-1}\circ(\id_{B}\otimes m_{B})=(\mathcal{R}_{1,3}\ast\mathcal{R}_{1,2})^{\ast-1}
. The latter
follows from (
2.26
) and (
2.4
) since
\id_{B}\otimes m_{B}:B^{\otimes 3}\to B^{\otimes 2}

is a homomorphism of coalgebras. The second identity in (
2.26
) for
\mathcal{R}^{(-1)}
is proven similarly.
∎

Note that any (restricted) dual
B^{\star}\subset B^{\smallsquare}
of
B
is also a bialgebra, the product being
\ast

while
\Delta(f)(b\otimes b^{\prime})=f(bb^{\prime})
,

f\in B^{\star}
,
b,b^{\prime}\in B
and
\varepsilon(f)=f(1)
.

Lemma 2.36
.

Let
B
be a finitely generated bialgebra, let

R,R^{\prime}\in B^{\widehat{\otimes}n}
and let
B^{\star}\subset B^{\smallsquare}
be a bialgebra. Define
f,f^{\prime}:B^{\star}{}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{k}
by, respectively,

f(a):=a(R)
,
f^{\prime}(a):=a(R^{\prime})
,
a\in B^{\star}{}^{\otimes n}
. Then

(f\ast f^{\prime})(a)=a(RR^{\prime})
for all
a\in B^{\star}{}^{\otimes n}
.
In particular, if
R
is invertible then
f
is
\ast
-invertible and
f^{\ast-1}(a)=a(R^{-1})
,
a\in B^{\star\otimes n}
.

Proof.

Let
a\in B^{\star}{}^{\otimes n}
and
write
\Delta_{B^{\star}{}^{\otimes n}}(a)=a_{(1)}\otimes a_{(2)}
in Sweedler notation.
Then
(f\ast f^{\prime})(a)=f(a_{(1)})f^{\prime}(a_{(2)})=a_{(1)}(R)a_{(2)}(R^{\prime})=a(RR^{\prime})
for all
a\in B^{\star}
. The
last assertion is immediate.
∎

The following is immediate from the definition,
Lemma
2.36

and Corollary
2.4
.

Lemma 2.37
.

Let
B
be a (topological) quasi-triangular bialgebra with an R-matrix
R
. Then for any (restricted) dual bialgebra
B^{\star}\subset B^{\smallsquare}
,

\mathcal{R}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B^{\star}\otimes B^{\star},\mathbb{k})

defined by
\mathcal{R}(f,f^{\prime})=(f\otimes f^{\prime})(R)
,

f,f^{\prime}\in B^{\star}
, is a co-quasi-triangular structure.

We say that a
\ast
-invertible

\mathcal{J}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes B,\mathbb{k})
is a
dual Drinfeld twist
if

(2.29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(a_{(1)},1)a_{(2)}=\mathcal{J}(a_{(2)},1)a_{(1)},\qquad\mathcal{J}(1,a_{(1)})a_{(2)}=\mathcal{J}(1,a_{(2)})a_{(1)},

(2.30)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}\circ(m_{B}\otimes\id_{B}))\ast\mathcal{J}_{1,2}=(\mathcal{J}\circ(\id_{B}\otimes m_{B}))\ast\mathcal{J}_{2,3}.

The following is immediate from the definition.

Lemma 2.38
.

Let
B
be a (topological) bialgebra and let
J\in B\widehat{\otimes}B
be a Drinfeld twist. Let
B^{\star}\subset B^{\smallsquare}
be a (restricted) dual of
B
.
Then

\mathcal{J}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B^{\star}\otimes B^{\star},\mathbb{k})
defined by
\mathcal{J}(f,f^{\prime})=(f\otimes f^{\prime})(J)
,

f,f^{\prime}\in B^{\star}
,
is a dual Drinfeld twist.

Define
\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes B,B)
by

a\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}b=\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})\mathcal{J}(a_{(3)},b_{(3)})a_{(2)}b_{(2)},\qquad a,b\in B

The following is well-known (see, e.g.
[
29
]
).

Lemma 2.39
.

Let
B
be a bialgebra and let
\mathcal{J}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B^{\otimes 2},\mathbb{k})
be a dual Drinfeld twist. Then
B
is a bialgebra with the multiplication
\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}

and with the comultiplication
\Delta:B\to B\otimes B
.

Proof.

We have, for all
a\in B
,

a\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}1=\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}(a_{(1)},1)\mathcal{J}(a_{(3)},1)a_{(2)}=\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}(a_{(1)},1)\mathcal{J}(a_{(2)},1)a_{(3)}=\varepsilon(a_{(1)})a_{(2)}=a.

Similarly,
1\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}a=a
.
Furthermore, we have, for all
a,b\in B

(\Delta\otimes\id_{B})\Delta(a\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}b)=\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})\mathcal{J}(a_{(5)},b_{(5)})a_{(2)}b_{(2)}\otimes a_{(3)}b_{(3)}\otimes a_{(4)}b_{(4)}.

Then,
for all
a,b,c\in\mathcal{J}
,

\displaystyle(a\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}b)\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}c

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}((a\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}b)_{(1)},c_{(1)})\mathcal{J}((a\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}b)_{(3)},c_{(3)})(a\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}b)_{(2)}c_{(2)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})\mathcal{J}(a_{(5)},b_{(5)})\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}(a_{(2)}b_{(2)},c_{(1)})\mathcal{J}(a_{(4)}b_{(4)},c_{(3)})a_{(3)}b_{(3)}c_{(2)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}(b_{(1)},c_{(1)})\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}(a_{(1)},b_{(2)}c_{(2)})\mathcal{J}(b_{(5)},c_{(5)})\mathcal{J}(a_{(3)},b_{(4)}c_{(4)})a_{(2)}b_{(3)}c_{(3)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}(a_{(1)},(b\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}c)_{(1)})\mathcal{J}(a_{(3)},(b\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}c)_{(3)})a_{(2)}(b\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}c)_{(2)}=a\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}(b\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}c).

Finally, for all
a,b\in B

\displaystyle\Delta(a)\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}\Delta(b)

\displaystyle=a_{(1)}\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}b_{(1)}\otimes a_{(2)}\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}b_{(2)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}(a_{(4)},b_{(4)})\mathcal{J}(a_{(3)},b_{(3)})\mathcal{J}(a_{(6)},b_{(6)})a_{(2)}b_{(2)}\otimes a_{(5)}b_{(5)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}^{\ast-1}(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})\mathcal{J}(a_{(4)},b_{(4)})a_{(2)}b_{(2)}\otimes a_{(3)}b_{(3)}=\Delta(a\bullet_{\mathcal{J}}b).\qed

Note the following analogue of Lemma
2.31
.

Lemma 2.40
.

Let
A
(respectively,
B
) be unital bialgebras and let
\mathcal{J}_{A}
,
\mathcal{J}_{B}
be Drinfeld twists on
A
(respectively,
B
).
Then
\mathcal{J}_{A\otimes B}:(A\otimes B)^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbb{k}
is a Drinfeld twist for
A\otimes B
if and only if
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{A\otimes B}:=(\mathcal{J}_{A})_{1,3}^{\ast-1}\ast(\mathcal{J}_{B})_{2,4}^{\ast-1}\ast\mathcal{J}_{A\otimes B}
is a Drinfeld twist for
A\otimes B

with the multiplication defined by
(a\otimes b)\cdot(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})=(a\bullet_{\mathcal{J}_{A}}a^{\prime})\otimes(b\bullet_{\mathcal{J}_{B}}b^{\prime})
,
a,a^{\prime}\in A
,
b,b^{\prime}\in B
.

The argument mimics that in the proof of Lemma
2.31
and is left to the reader as an exercise.

Let
A
,
B
be bialgebras. We say that
\mathcal{F}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes A,\mathbb{k})
is a
relative dual Drinfeld twist
if
\mathcal{F}_{2,3}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}((A\otimes B)^{\otimes 2},\mathbb{k})

is a dual Drinfeld twist.
The following is easily checked.

Lemma 2.41
.

Let
A
and
B
be bialgebras.
A
\ast
-invertible
\mathcal{F}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes A,\mathbb{k})

is a relative dual Drinfeld twist is and only if

\mathcal{F}(b_{(1)},1_{A})b_{(2)}=\mathcal{F}(b_{(2)},1_{A})b_{(1)}
for all
b\in B
,

\mathcal{F}(1_{B},a_{(1)})a_{(2)}=\mathcal{F}(1_{B},a_{(2)})a_{(1)}
for all
a\in A
and

(2.31)

\mathcal{F}_{13,4}\ast\mathcal{F}_{1,2}=\mathcal{F}_{1,24}\ast\mathcal{F}_{3,4},

where
\mathcal{F}_{13,4}(b\otimes a\otimes b^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime})=\mathcal{F}(bb^{\prime},a^{\prime})\varepsilon(a)
and
\mathcal{F}_{1,24}(b\otimes a\otimes b^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime})=\mathcal{F}(b,aa^{\prime})\varepsilon(b^{\prime})
,
a,a^{\prime}\in A
,
b,b^{\prime}\in B
.

Lemma 2.42
.

Let
A=B
be a bialgebra and let
\mathcal{R}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes B,\mathbb{k})
be a co-quasitriangular structure. Then
\mathcal{R}

is a relative dual Drinfeld twist.

Proof.

We only need to check that (
2.31
) holds.
Indeed, for any
a,a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime}\in B
we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}_{13,4}\ast

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1,2})(b\otimes a\otimes b^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}_{13,4}(b_{(1)}\otimes a_{(1)}\otimes b^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime})\mathcal{R}(b_{(2)}\otimes a_{(2)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}(b_{(1)}b^{\prime},a^{\prime})\mathcal{R}(b_{(2)},a)=\mathcal{R}(b_{(1)},a^{\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{R}(b^{\prime},a^{\prime}_{(2)})\mathcal{R}(b_{(2)},a)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}(b,aa^{\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{R}(b^{\prime},a^{\prime}_{(2)})=\mathcal{R}_{1,24}(b\otimes a\otimes b^{\prime}_{(1)}\otimes a^{\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{R}(b^{\prime}_{(2)},a^{\prime}_{(2)})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{R}_{1,24}\ast\mathcal{R}_{3,4})(b\otimes a\otimes b^{\prime}\otimes a^{\prime}).\qed

The following analogue
of Corollary
2.32
is immediate from Lemma
2.40
.

Corollary 2.43
.

Let
A
and
B
be bialgebras and suppose
that
\mathcal{F}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes A,\mathbb{k})
is a relative dual Drinfeld twist where multiplications
on
A
and
B
are twisted by
\mathcal{J}_{A}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(A\otimes A,\mathbb{k})
and
\mathcal{J}_{B}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes B,\mathbb{k})
, respectively.
Then
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{F}}:=(\mathcal{J}_{A})_{1,3}\ast(\mathcal{J}_{B})_{2,4}\ast\mathcal{F}_{2,3}
is a dual Drinfeld twist for
A\otimes B

with respect to the non-twisted multiplication and

(a\otimes b)\bullet_{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{F}}}(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})=(\bullet_{\mathcal{J}_{A}}\otimes\bullet_{\mathcal{J}_{B}})(\id_{A}\otimes\Psi_{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\id_{B})(a\otimes b\otimes a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime}),

where
\Psi_{\mathcal{F}}:B\otimes A\to A\otimes B
is defined by

\Psi_{\mathcal{F}}(b\otimes a)=\mathcal{F}^{\ast-1}(b_{(1)},a_{(1)})\mathcal{F}(b_{(3)},a_{(3)})a_{(2)}\otimes b_{(2)},\qquad a\in A,\,b\in B.

We conclude this section with a natural generalization of (
2.25
) and the counterpart of Proposition
2.33
.

Proposition 2.44
.

Let
A
,
B
be bialgebras and let
\mathcal{F}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes A,\mathbb{k})
be a relative dual Drinfeld twist. Let
U
be a unital algebra with
the multiplication
m_{U}:U\otimes U\to U
and
let
\varphi_{A}:A\to U
,
\varphi_{B}:B\to U
be homomorphisms of unital algebras.
Then
m_{U}\circ(\varphi_{A}\otimes\varphi_{B})
is
homomorphism of algebras

(A\otimes B,\bullet_{\mathcal{F}_{2,3}})\to U
if and only if

(2.32)

\mathcal{F}(b_{(1)},a_{(1)})\varphi_{B}(b_{(2)})\varphi_{A}(a_{(2)})=\mathcal{F}(b_{(2)},a_{(2)})\varphi_{A}(a_{(1)})\varphi_{B}(b_{(2)}),\qquad a\in A,\,b\in B.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbf{m}:=m_{U}\circ(\varphi_{A}\otimes\varphi_{B})
is a homomorphism of algebras
if and only if

\mathbf{m}((a^{\prime}\otimes b)\bullet(a\otimes b^{\prime}))=\varphi_{A}(a^{\prime})\varphi_{B}(b)\varphi_{A}(a)\varphi_{B}(b^{\prime}),\quad a,a^{\prime}\in A,\,b,b^{\prime}\in B.

Using Corollary
2.43

with trivial twists
\mathcal{J}_{A}
and
\mathcal{J}_{B}
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}((a^{\prime}\otimes b)\bullet(a\otimes b^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{\ast-1}(b_{(1)},a_{(1)})\mathcal{F}(b_{(3)},a_{(3)})\mathbf{m}(a^{\prime}a_{(2)}\otimes b_{(2)}b^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{\ast-1}(b_{(1)},a_{(1)})\mathcal{F}(b_{(3)},a_{(3)})\varphi_{A}(a^{\prime}a_{(2)})\varphi_{B}(b_{(2)}b^{\prime}).

Thus,
\mathbf{m}
is a homomorphism of algebras if and only if

\varphi_{A}(a^{\prime})\mathbf{m}(\Psi_{\mathcal{F}}(b\otimes a))\varphi(b^{\prime})=\varphi_{A}(a^{\prime})\varphi_{B}(b)\varphi_{A}(a)\varphi_{B}(b^{\prime})

for all
a,a^{\prime}\in A
,
b,b^{\prime}\in B
or, equivalently,
if and only if

\varphi_{B}(b)\varphi_{A}(a)=\mathcal{F}^{\ast-1}(b_{(1)},a_{(1)})\mathcal{F}(b_{(3)},a_{(3)})\varphi_{A}(a_{(2)})\varphi_{B}(b_{(2)}),\qquad a\in A,\,b\in B,

which in turn is equivalent to (
2.32
) by
Lemma
2.7
applied with
C=B\otimes A
,
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{F}
,

V=U
,

f=m_{U}\circ(\varphi_{B}\otimes\varphi_{A})
and
g=m_{U}\circ\tau\circ(\varphi_{B}\otimes\varphi_{A})
.
∎

3.
Combinatorial background

3.1.
Transitivity and permutations

Let
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
.
We say that
S\subset[n]\times[n]
is
transitive
if
(i,j),(j,k)\in S

implies that
(i,k)\in S
(see e.g.
[
4
]
*§4.2). We
can identify
[n]\times[n]
with a complete bioriented graph
\overset{\leftrightarrow\circlearrowleft}{K_{n}}
with vertex set
[n]

and with loops. In this language, a transitive subset corresponds
to a subgraph of
\overset{\leftrightarrow\circlearrowleft}{K_{n}}
which contains the oriented edge
i\to k

provided that it contains oriented edges
i\to j
and
j\to k
,
i,j,k\in[n]
.

Let
S\subset[n]\times[n]
be transitive and let
C
be any set. We say that

\mathbf{c}:S\to C

is
transitive
(cf.
[
4
]
*Definition 1.1 and
[
3
]
*Definition 1.2) if

\mathbf{c}(i,k)\in\{\mathbf{c}(i,j),\mathbf{c}(j,k)\}
for all
(i,j),(j,k)\in S
. Clearly, if
S^{\prime}\subset S
is transitive
and
\mathbf{c}:S\to C
is transitive, then so is
\mathbf{c}|_{S^{\prime}}
.

Given a permutation
w\in S_{n}
, denote
\Inv(w)=\{(i,j)\,:\,1\leq i<j\leq n,\,w(i)>w(j)\}
. In particular, we abbreviate

I_{n}=\Inv(w_{\circ})=\{(i,j)\,:\,1\leq i<j\leq n\}
where

w_{\circ}
is the longest element of
S_{n}
.
It is well-known
that
S\subset I_{n}
is equal to
\Inv(w)
for some
w\in S_{n}

if and only if both
S
and
I_{n}\setminus S
are transitive. Moreover, the map
w\mapsto\Inv(w)
,
w\in S_{n}
, is injective.

The following was established in
[
4
]
*Observation 4.5.

Lemma 3.1
.

For any
w\in S_{n}
,
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w):I_{n}\to\{1,-1\}

defined by

\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)(i,j)=\sign(w(j)-w(i))
,
1\leq i<j\leq n

(cf. (
1.7
)) is transitive. Moreover, the assignments

w\mapsto\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)
,
w\in S_{n}
define a bijection from
S_{n}

onto the set of all transitive maps
I_{n}\to\{1,-1\}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
w\in S_{n}
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}:[n]\to\{1,-1\}
and define
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w,\boldsymbol{\alpha}):[n]\times[n]\to\{1,-1\}
by

\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w,\boldsymbol{\alpha})(i,j)=\delta_{i,j}\alpha(i)+\sign(w(j)-w(i)),\qquad i,j\in[n]

(cf. (
1.3
)).
Then
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w,\boldsymbol{\alpha})
is transitive. Moreover,
all transitive maps
\mathbf{c}:[n]\times[n]\to\{1,-1\}
satisfying

\mathbf{c}(j,i)=-\mathbf{c}(i,j)
,
(i,j)\in I_{n}
, are obtained this way.
In particular, there are
n!2^{n}
such maps.

Proof.

Abbreviate
\epsilon_{ij}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w,\boldsymbol{\alpha})(i,j)
,

i,j\in[n]
. We need to prove that
\epsilon_{ik}\in\{\epsilon_{ij},\epsilon_{jk}\}
for all
i,j,k\in[n]
. Note that
\epsilon_{ij}=-\epsilon_{ji}

if
i\not=j
.

If
i=j=k
then there is nothing to prove. Suppose that
|\{i,j,k\}|=2
. If
i\not=k
then
j\in\{i,k\}
and the assertion is obvious.
If
i=k
then
i\not=j
and so
\{\epsilon_{ij},\epsilon_{jk}\}=\{\epsilon_{ij},\epsilon_{ji}\}=\{1,-1\}\ni\epsilon_{ik}
.

Assume now that
|\{i,j,k\}|=3
. Suppose first that
i<k
. If
i<j<k
then
\epsilon_{ik}\in\{\epsilon_{ij},\epsilon_{jk}\}
by Lemma
3.1
.
If
k<j
then
\epsilon_{ij}\in\{\epsilon_{ik},\epsilon_{kj}\}
by Lemma
3.1
. Thus either

\epsilon_{ik}=\epsilon_{ij}
or
\epsilon_{ik}=-\epsilon_{ij}=-\epsilon_{kj}=\epsilon_{jk}
.
If
j<i
then
\epsilon_{jk}\in\{\epsilon_{ji},\epsilon_{ik}\}
by Lemma
3.1
and so either
\epsilon_{ik}=\epsilon_{jk}

or
\epsilon_{ik}=-\epsilon_{jk}=-\epsilon_{ji}=\epsilon_{ij}
.
Finally, if
k<i
then
\epsilon_{ki}\in\{\epsilon_{kj},\epsilon_{ji}\}
by the above and so
\epsilon_{ik}=-\epsilon_{ki}\in\{-\epsilon_{ji},-\epsilon_{kj}\}=\{\epsilon_{ij},\epsilon_{jk}\}
.

To prove the converse, note that
\mathbf{c}:[n]\times[n]\to\{1,-1\}

satisfying
\mathbf{c}(j,i)=-\mathbf{c}(i,j)
,
(i,j)\in I_{n}
,
is uniquely determined by
(\mathbf{c}(i,i))_{i\in[n]}
and
by
\mathbf{c}|_{I_{n}}\to\{1,-1\}
which is transitive. It remains to apply Lemma
3.1
.
∎

3.2.
Admissible functors and polynomiality

Let
M
be a set and
denote by
\Part(M)
the set of all partitions of
M
into nonempty disjoint subsets. Given
m\in M
and
\mathcal{P}\in\Part(M)
, let
\mathcal{P}[m]
be the necessarily unique set in
\mathcal{P}

containing
m
.
Clearly, if
M
is finite then
\Part(M)=\bigsqcup\limits_{k\geq 1}\Part_{k}(M)
where
\Part_{k}(M)
is the set of all

{\mathcal{P}}\in\Part(M)
with exactly
k
parts.

Given sets
M
and
C
,
denote
\Map(M,C)
the set of all maps from
M
to
C
and let
\Inj(M,C)\subset\Map(M,C)

be the set of all injective maps from
M

to
C
.
Clearly,
each
f\in\Map(M,C)
defines a partition
{\mathcal{P}}_{f}=\{f^{-1}(c)\}_{c\in f(M)}\in\Part(M)
.

Denote
\Map_{k}(M,C):=\{\varepsilon\in\Map(M,C):|\varepsilon(M)|=k\}
for any sets
M
,
C
and
k\geq 1
. Clearly,
\Map_{k}(M,C)=\emptyset
if and only if
k>|C|
and if either
M
or
C
is finite, then
\Map(M,C)=\bigsqcup\limits_{k\geq 1}\Map_{k}(M,C)
. Note that
{\mathcal{P}}_{\varepsilon}\in\Part_{k}(M)
if and only
\varepsilon\in\Map_{k}(M,C)
.
Given
{\mathcal{E}}\subset\Map(M,C)
and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
we denote

{\mathcal{E}}_{k}:={\mathcal{E}}\cap\Map_{k}(M,C),\qquad\Part_{k}^{\mathcal{E}}(M):=\{{\mathcal{P}}_{\varepsilon}\in\Part_{k}(M):\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}\}.

It is immediate from definitions that
\Part_{k}^{\mathcal{E}}(M)=\Part_{k}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}(M)
and
\Part_{k}^{\mathcal{E}}(M)\subset\Part_{k}^{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}(M)
whenever
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}\subset{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{k}\subset\Map_{k}(M,C)
.

Lemma 3.3
.

\Part_{k}^{\mathcal{E}}(M)=\Part_{k}^{f\circ{\mathcal{E}}}(M)
for any
{\mathcal{E}}\subset\Map(M,C)
and any
f\in\Inj(C,C^{\prime})
, where we abbreviated
f\circ{\mathcal{E}}=\{f\circ\varepsilon:\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}\}
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to observe that if
f\in\Inj(C,C^{\prime})
then
{\mathcal{P}}_{\varepsilon}={\mathcal{P}}_{f\circ\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon\in\Map(M,C)
.
∎

Proposition 3.4
.

For any finite sets
M
and
C
,
{\mathcal{E}}\subset\Map(M,C)
, one has

(3.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|{\mathcal{E}}|=\sum_{k\geq 1}|\Part_{k}^{\mathcal{E}}(M)|\cdot(|C|)_{k},\end{split}

where
(x)_{k}:=x(x-1)\cdots(x-k+1)
.

Proof.

For any sets
M,C
and any
\mathcal{P}\in\Part(M)
and any
{\mathcal{E}}\subset\Map(M,C)
denote

(3.2)

{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}=\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}:{\mathcal{P}}_{\varepsilon}={\mathcal{P}}\}\ .

Clearly,
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}=\emptyset
for
{\mathcal{P}}\in\Part_{k}(M)
if and only if
\Part_{k}^{\mathcal{E}}(M)=\emptyset
.

Lemma 3.5
.

|{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}|=(|C|)_{k}
for any finite
M
and
C
,
{\mathcal{E}}\subset\Map(M,C)
and
\mathcal{P}\in\Part_{k}^{\mathcal{E}}(M)
,
k\geq 1
.

Proof.

Given
{\mathcal{P}}\in\Part_{k}(M)
, fix a surjective map
\pi:M\twoheadrightarrow[k]
such that
{\mathcal{P}}_{\pi}={\mathcal{P}}
.
Clearly,
\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}
if and only if
\varepsilon=\underline{\varepsilon}\circ\pi
for some
\underline{\varepsilon}\in\Inj([k],C)
. Thus, the assignments
\varepsilon\mapsto\underline{\varepsilon}
define a
bijection
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Inj([k],C)
.
Finally, note that
|\Inj([k],C)|=(|C|)_{k}
for any finite set
C
.
∎

Since
\mathcal{E}=\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{P}\in\Part(M)}{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}
, we have

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{E}}|

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{{\mathcal{P}}\in\Part(M)}|{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}|=\sum\limits_{k\geq 1}\sum\limits_{{\mathcal{P}}\in\Part_{k}^{\mathcal{E}}(P)}(|C|)_{k}=\sum\limits_{k\geq 1}|\Part_{k}^{\mathcal{E}}(M)|\cdot(|C|)_{k},

which is (
3.1
).
∎

We now describe a categorical framework for constructing
{\mathcal{E}}(C)\subset\Map(M,C)
for a given
M
and various
C
such that
\Part_{k}^{{\mathcal{E}}(C)}(M)
does not depend on
C
. If this is the case then
|{\mathcal{E}}(C)|
is a polynomial in
|C|
by Proposition
3.4
. More precisely,
|{\mathcal{E}}(C)|=\sum_{k\geq 1}a_{M,k}(|C|)_{k}
where
a_{M,k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}

and
a_{M,k}=0
for
k\gg 0
.

Given a category
{\mathcal{C}}
and
M\in\Ob({\mathcal{C}})
, we say that a family
\{F(C)\subset\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(M,C)\,:\,C\in\Ob({\mathcal{C}})\}

is an
M
-
admissible
functor
F:{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathbf{Set}
if

(3.3)

f\circ F(C)\subset F(C^{\prime})

for any
f\in\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(C,C^{\prime})
and any
C,C^{\prime}\in\Ob({\mathcal{C}})
.

Let
\mathbf{Set}_{0}
be a full subcategory of
\mathbf{Set}
containing all the
[c]
,
c\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and closed with respect to subsets.
Given a finite set
M
and an
M
-admissible functor
F:{\bf Set_{0}}\to\mathbf{Set}
we denote

{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{M}{k}}_{F}:=|\Part_{k}^{F([k])}(M)|
,
\quad k\geq 1
. Clearly,
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{M}{k}_{F}=0
if
k>|M|

and
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{M}{1}_{F}=1
. Define

p_{F}=\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k\geq 1}{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{M}{k}}_{F}\cdot(x)_{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}[x].

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
M
be a finite set and
F
be an
M
-admissible functor
\mathbf{Set}_{0}\to\mathbf{Set}
. Then

|F(C)|=p_{F}(|C|)

for any finite
C\in\Ob(\mathbf{Set}_{0})
.

Proof.

We need the following result.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
F:\mathbf{Set}_{0}\to\mathbf{Set}
be any
M
-admissible functor. Then

\Part^{F(C)}_{k}(M)=\Part_{k}^{F([k])}(M)

for all
C\in\Ob(\mathbf{Set}_{0})
such that
|C|\geq k
.

Proof.

We need the following

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
F
be an
M
-admissible functor. Then

F(C)_{k}=\bigcup\limits_{f\in\Inj([k],C)}f\circ F([k])
for all
C\in\Ob(\mathbf{Set}_{0})
,
k\geq 1
.

Proof.

Denote
F(C)^{\prime}_{k}:=\bigcup\limits_{f\in\Inj([k],C)}f\circ F([k])
. Clearly,
F(C)^{\prime}_{k}\subset F(C)_{k}
by (
3.3
) which guarantees that
f\circ F([k])\subset F(C)_{k}
for any
f\in\Inj([k],C)
.

To prove the opposite inclusion, given
\varepsilon\in F(C)_{k}
, choose
f\in\Inj([k],C)
such that
f([k])=\varepsilon(M)
and let
g
be a (necessarily surjective) map

C\twoheadrightarrow[k]
such that
g\circ f=\id_{[k]}
. By construction, both
f
and
g
are morphisms in
\mathbf{Set}_{0}
, such that
f\circ g\circ\varepsilon=\varepsilon
.

Denote
\underline{\varepsilon}:=g\circ\varepsilon
. Clearly,
\underline{\varepsilon}\in F([k])
by (
3.3
) and

f\circ\underline{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon
. That is,
\varepsilon\in f\circ F([k])
.
Therefore,
F(C)_{k}\subset F(C)^{\prime}_{k}
, which completes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

We have

\Part_{k}^{F(C)}(M)=\Part_{k}^{F(C)_{k}}(M)=\bigcup\limits_{f\in\Inj([k],C)}\{{\mathcal{P}}_{\varepsilon}\,:\,\varepsilon\in f\circ F([k])\}=\Part_{k}^{F([k])}(M).

This completes the proof of Proposition
3.7
.
∎

By Proposition
3.7
,
{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{M}{k}}_{F}=|\Part_{k}^{F(C)}(M)|
for any object
C\in\mathbf{Set}_{0}
.
Using this and (
3.1
) with
{\mathcal{E}}=F(C)
we obtain

\displaystyle|F(C)|=\sum_{k\geq 1}|\Part_{k}^{F(C)}|\,(|C|)_{k}=\sum_{k\geq 1}{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{M}{k}}_{F}\,(|C|)_{k}=p_{F}(|C|).

Theorem
3.6
is proved.
∎

Corollary 3.9
.

\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{M}{2}_{F}=\frac{1}{2}|F([2])|-1
under the assumptions of Theorem
3.6
.

Example 3.10
.

Let
M
be a finite set. The assignments
F(C):=\Map(M,C)
for all sets
C
define an
M
-admissible functor
F:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}
.
Then
|F(C)|=|C|^{|M|}
hence
p_{F}=x^{|M|}
. In particular,
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{[n]}{k}_{F}=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{n}{k}
is the number of partitions of
[n]
into
k
disjoint nonempty subsets, that is, the Stirling number of the second kind.
Thus, we can view the
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{M}{k}_{F}
as generalized Stirling numbers which justifies the notation.

3.3.
Polynomiality and transitivity

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a directed graph. A function
\mathbf{c}:E\to C
is called a
transitive coloring
of
\Gamma
(cf.
[
4
]
) if for any vertices
i,j,k\in V
such that
(i,j),(j,k),(i,k)\in E
,

\mathbf{c}(i,k)\in\{\mathbf{c}(i,j),\mathbf{c}(j,k)\}
.
For the directed graph
\vec{K}_{n}=([n],I_{n})
(an acyclic tournament),
a transitive coloring is precisely a transitive map

I_{n}\to C
.
For an unoriented graph, the corresponding notion is a
Gallai coloring
(cf.
[
4
]
). Given a graph
\Gamma
,
let
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Gamma}(C)
be the set of transitive (respectively, Gallai) colorings of
\Gamma
with values in
C
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Given a (directed) graph
\Gamma=(V,E)
, the assignments
C\mapsto{\mathcal{E}}_{\Gamma}(C)
for all sets
C
define an
E
-admissible functor
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Gamma}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a graph,
C
be a set and
\mathbf{c}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Gamma}(C)
. Let
f\in\Map(C,C^{\prime})
.

Suppose first that
\Gamma
is directed. Then for any
i,j,k\in V

such that the oriented edges
(i,j),(j,k),(i,k)\in E
,

\mathbf{c}(i,k)\in\{\mathbf{c}(i,j),\mathbf{c}(j,k)\}
whence

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{c}(i,k))\in\{f(\mathbf{c}(i,j)),f(\mathbf{c}(j,k))\},

That is,
f\circ\mathbf{c}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Gamma}(C^{\prime})
. Thus, (
3.3
) holds and
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Gamma}
is an
E
-admissible functor
\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

Similarly, if
V
is unoriented then
|\{\mathbf{c}(i,j),\mathbf{c}(j,k),\mathbf{c}(i,k)\}|\leq 2

for any vertices
i,j,k\in V

and edges
(i,j),(j,k),(i,k)\in V
, whence

\displaystyle|\{f(\mathbf{c}(i,j)),f(\mathbf{c}(j,k)),f(\mathbf{c}(i,k))\}|\leq 2.

Again,
f\circ\mathbf{c}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Gamma}(C^{\prime})
and so
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Gamma}
is an
E
-admissible functor
\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}
.
∎

Corollary 3.12

(cf.
[
3
]
*Proposition 2.13)
.

For any finite set
C
and any graph
\Gamma=(V,E)
,

|\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma}(C)|=\sum_{k\geq 1}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{\Gamma}{k}\,(|C|)_{k}

where
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\Gamma}{k}=|\Part_{k}^{\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma}([k])}(V)|\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
.

Let
\Gamma=\vec{K}_{n}
. Then

\Part^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Gamma}([r])}_{r}(I_{n})

is the set of all
\mathcal{P}\in\Part_{r}(I_{n})

such that for any
(i,k)\in I_{n}
,

\mathcal{P}[(i,k)]\cap\{(i,j),(j,k)\}\not=\emptyset
or, equivalently
\mathcal{P}[(i,k)]
coincides
with one of the
\mathcal{P}[(i,j)]
and
\mathcal{P}[(j,k)]
,
for all
1\leq i<j<k\leq n
. By
[
4
]
*Observation 2.15,
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\vec K_n}{k}=0

if
k\geq n
. By
[
4
]
*Theorem 1.4),
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\vec K_{n}}{n-1}
is the
(n-1)
th
Catalan number
\frac{1}{n}\binom{2(n-1)}{n-1}
, while
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\vec K_n}{2}=\frac{1}{2}n!-1
by Lemma
3.1
or by Corollary
3.9
.

Conjecture 3.13
.

\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\vec K_n}{n-2}=(n-2)\binom{2n-3}{n}+\binom{2(n-2)}{n}

for all
n\geq 3
.

The numbers
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\vec K_n}{k}
for small values of
n
are shown in Table
1
.

\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{\vec K_n}{k}
for
1\leq k<n\leq 9

In particular, for
|C|=3
(respectively,
|C|=4
), the number of transitive maps
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
is given by the sequences

3
,
15
,

,

,

,

,
627621,\dots

(respectively,
4
,

,

,
2704
,

,

,
4885336,\dots
).

Proposition 3.14
.

For
\Gamma=\overset{\leftrightarrow\circlearrowleft}{K_{n}}
, we have

\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{\Gamma}{k}=\begin{cases}2\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{n}{k},&k\geq 3,\\
\tfrac{1}{2}B_{n}-1,&k=2,\end{cases}

where

B_{n}:=\sum\limits_{1\leq k\leq n}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{n}{k}2^{k}k!

is the number of transitive relations
\mathscr{R}
on
[n]
such that
([n]\times[n])\setminus\mathscr{R}
is also transitive.

Proof.

Given
i,j\in[n]
and
\mathcal{P}\in\Part([n]\times[n])
,
abbreviate
\mathcal{P}(i,j):=\mathcal{P}[(i,j)]
.
Let
\mathscr{T}_{n}
be the set of all
\mathcal{P}\in\Part([n]\times[n])

such that
\mathcal{P}(i,k)\in\{\mathcal{P}(i,j),\mathcal{P}(j,k)\}
for all
i,j,k\in[n]
.
It is immediate from the definition that
\Part_{r}^{\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma}([r])}([n]\times[n])=\mathscr{T}_{n}\cap\Part_{r}([n]\times[n]):=\mathscr{T}_{n,r}
.

Given
\mathcal{P}\in\Part([n]\times[n])
, define a relation
\sim_{\mathcal{P}}

on
[n]
by
i\sim_{\mathcal{P}}j
if and only if
\mathcal{P}(i,j)=\mathcal{P}(j,i)
,

i,j\in[n]
. This relation is clearly reflexive and symmetric.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\mathcal{P}\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
. Then

(a)

\sim_{\mathcal{P}}
is an equivalence relation;

(b)

If
i\sim_{\mathcal{P}}i^{\prime}
,

j\sim_{\mathcal{P}}j^{\prime}
then
\mathcal{P}(i,j)=\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Note that if
\mathcal{P}\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
and
\mathcal{P}(i,j)=\mathcal{P}(j,i)

then
\mathcal{P}(i,i)=\mathcal{P}(i,j)=\mathcal{P}(j,i)=\mathcal{P}(j,j)
.
Suppose that
i\sim_{\mathcal{P}}j
,
j\sim_{\mathcal{P}}k
,
i,j,k\in[n]
. Then
\mathcal{P}(i,i)=\mathcal{P}(j,j)=\mathcal{P}(k,k)
,

\mathcal{P}(i,k)\in\{\mathcal{P}(i,j),\mathcal{P}(j,k)\}=\{\mathcal{P}(j,j)\}

and
\mathcal{P}(k,i)\in\{\mathcal{P}(k,j),\mathcal{P}(j,i)\}=\{\mathcal{P}(j,j)\}
.
Therefore
\mathcal{P}(i,k)=\mathcal{P}(k,i)
, that is
i\sim_{\mathcal{P}}k
.
To prove
(b)
,
suppose that
\mathcal{P}(i,j)\not=\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
. Since

\mathcal{P}(i,j)\in\{\mathcal{P}(i,i^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j)\}
while

\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\{\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},i),\mathcal{P}(i,j^{\prime})\}=\{\mathcal{P}(i,i^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(i,j^{\prime})\}
, it follows that
\mathcal{P}(i,j)=\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j)\not=\mathcal{P}(i,j^{\prime})=\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
. Yet,

\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j)\in\{\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(j^{\prime},j)\}
, whence
\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j)=\mathcal{P}(j,j^{\prime})
, while
\mathcal{P}(i,j^{\prime})\in\{\mathcal{P}(i,j),\mathcal{P}(j,j^{\prime})\}
whence
\mathcal{P}(i,j^{\prime})=\mathcal{P}(j,j^{\prime})
. Thus,
\mathcal{P}(i,j^{\prime})=\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j)
which is a contradiction.
∎

We need the following

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
\pi:[n]\to[r]

be surjective.

(a)

Suppose that
\mathcal{P}\in\Part([n]\times[n])

satisfies

\mathcal{P}(i,j)=\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
for all
i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}\in[n]
such that
\pi(i)=\pi(i^{\prime})
and
\pi(j)=\pi(j^{\prime})
.
Then
(\pi\times\pi)(\mathcal{P}):=\{(\pi\times\pi)(P)\,:\,P\in\mathcal{P}\}\in\Part([r]\times[r])
;

(b)

Suppose that
\mathcal{Q}\in\Part([r]\times[r])
.
Then
(\pi\times\pi)^{-1}(\mathcal{Q}):=\{(\pi\times\pi)^{-1}(Q)\,:\,Q\in\mathcal{Q}\}

is a partition of
[n]\times[n]
and
(\pi\times\pi)((\pi\times\pi)^{-1}(\mathcal{Q}))=\mathcal{Q}
.

Proof.

Since
\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P=[n]\times[n]
and
\pi

is surjective,
\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}(\pi\times\pi)(P)=[r]\times[r]
. Suppose that
(i,j)\in(\pi\times\pi)(P)\cap(\pi\times\pi)(P^{\prime})
,
i,j\in[r]
. Then
i=(\pi(i^{\prime}),\pi(j^{\prime}))=(\pi(i^{\prime\prime}),\pi(j^{\prime\prime}))
for some
i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime}\in[n]

and
P=\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})=P^{\prime}

whence
(\pi\times\pi)(P)=(\pi\times\pi)(P^{\prime})
.
Thus,
(\pi\times\pi)(\mathcal{P})\in\Part([r]\times[r])
.

Part
(b)
is immediate.
∎

Given
\mathcal{P}\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
, let
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{P})
be
the partition of
[n]
into equivalence classes for
\sim_{\mathcal{P}}
. Denote
\underline{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
the set of
all
\mathcal{P}\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
satisfying
\mathcal{P}(i,j)\not=\mathcal{P}(j,i)
for all
i\not=j\in[n]
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\mathcal{P}\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
and suppose that
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{P})\in\Part_{r}([n])
. Then

(\pi\times\pi)(\mathcal{P})\in\underline{\mathscr{T}}_{r}

for any surjective
\pi:[n]\to[r]
such that
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{P})=\{\pi^{-1}(i)\}_{i\in[r]}
.

Proof.

By
Lemmata
3.15
(b)
and
3.16
,

(\pi\times\pi)(\mathcal{P})\in\Part([r]\times[r])
. Let
i=\pi(i^{\prime}),j=\pi(j^{\prime}),k=\pi(k^{\prime})\in[r]
,
i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime}\in[n]
.
Then
\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\{\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),\mathcal{P}(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})\}
,
and so
(\pi\times\pi)(\mathcal{P})(i,k)\in\{(\pi\times\pi)(\mathcal{P})(i,j),(\pi\times\pi)(\mathcal{P})(j,k)\}
, that is
(\pi\times\pi)(\mathcal{P})\in\mathscr{T}_{r}
.
Finally, suppose that
(\pi\times\pi)(\mathcal{P})(i,j)=(\pi\times\pi)(\mathcal{P})(j,i)

for some
i\not=j\in[r]
. Then

i=\pi(i^{\prime})
,
j=\pi(j^{\prime})
,
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in[n]
with
i^{\prime}\not\sim_{\mathcal{P}}j^{\prime}

yet
(\pi\times\pi)(\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=(\pi\times\pi)(\mathcal{P}(j^{\prime},i^{\prime}))
, that
is,
(i,j)=(\pi(i^{\prime\prime}),\pi(j^{\prime\prime}))
for some
i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime}\in[n]
such that
\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{P}(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})\not=\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
. Since
\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{P}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})

by Lemma
3.15
(b)
, this is a contradiction.
∎

Let
\mathcal{J}\in\Part_{r}([n])
.
Define
\mathcal{J}_{k}=\mathcal{J}[m_{k}]
where
m_{k}=\min\big([n]\setminus\bigcup_{1\leq t<k}\mathcal{J}_{t}\big)
; thus,
m_{1}=1

and
\mathcal{J}_{1}=\mathcal{J}[1]
and so on.
Let
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}
be the unique surjective map
\pi:[n]\to[r]

satisfying
\pi^{-1}(t)=\mathcal{J}_{t}
,
t\in[r]
.
By Lemma
3.17

we obtain a well-defined map
\Pi_{n}:\mathscr{T}_{n}\to\bigsqcup_{1\leq r\leq n}\underline{\mathscr{T}}_{r}
,
\mathcal{P}\mapsto(\pi_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{P})}\times\pi_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{P})})(\mathcal{P})
,

\mathcal{P}\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
.

Lemma 3.18
.

The map
\Pi_{n}
is surjective and
|\Pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P})|=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{n}{r}

for any
\mathcal{P}\in\underline{\mathscr{T}}_{r}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}\in\underline{\mathscr{T}}_{r}
,

\mathcal{J}\in\Part_{r}([n])
and define
\mathcal{Q}:=\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{J})=(\pi_{\mathcal{J}}\times\pi_{\mathcal{J}})^{-1}(\mathcal{P})\in\Part([n]\times[n])
by Lemma
3.16
(b)
and
(\pi_{\mathcal{J}}\times\pi_{\mathcal{J}})(\mathcal{Q})=\mathcal{P}
.
We claim that
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
. Indeed,
let
i,j,k\in[n]
. Then

(\pi_{\mathcal{J}}\times\pi_{\mathcal{J}})(\mathcal{Q}(i,k))=\mathcal{P}(\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(i),\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(k))\in\{\mathcal{P}(\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(i),\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(j)),\mathcal{P}(\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(j),\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(k))\},

whence
\mathcal{Q}(i,k)\in\{\mathcal{Q}(i,j),\mathcal{Q}(j,k)\}
.

Next, we prove that
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Q})=\mathcal{J}
,
that is,
\mathcal{Q}(i,j)=\mathcal{Q}(j,i)
if and only if
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(i)=\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(j)
. Suppose first that
\mathcal{Q}(i,j)=\mathcal{Q}(j,i)
. Then
\mathcal{P}(\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(i),\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(j))=\mathcal{P}(\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(j),\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(i))
which forces
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(i)=\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(j)

since
\mathcal{P}\in\bigsqcup_{r\in[n]}\underline{\mathscr{T}}_{r}
.
The converse is obvious
since
\mathcal{Q}(i,j)=(\pi_{\mathcal{J}}\times\pi_{\mathcal{J}})^{-1}(\mathcal{P}(\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(i),\pi_{\mathcal{J}}(j))
,
i,j\in[n]
.

Thus, we proved that
\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{J}))=\mathcal{P}
, that is,
\Pi_{n}
is surjective and, moreover,
that
\Pi^{-1}_{n}(\mathcal{P})=\{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{J})\,:\,\mathcal{J}\in\Part_{r}([n])\}
. It remains to observe that,
since
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{J}))
,
the map
\Part_{r}([n])\to\Pi_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{P})
,
\mathcal{J}\mapsto\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{J})
,
\mathcal{J}\in\Part_{r}([n])

is also injective.
∎

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
\mathcal{P}\in\underline{\mathscr{T}}_{n}
.

(a)

If
\mathcal{P}(i,i)\not=\mathcal{P}(j,j)
for all
i\not=j\in[n]

then
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}_{n}\}
where
either
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\{(i,j)\,:\,j\in[n]\}
for all
i\in[n]

or
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\{(j,i)\,:\,j\in[n]\}
for all
i\in[n]
.

(b)

If
\mathcal{P}(i,i)=\mathcal{P}(j,j)
for some
i\not=j
then
\mathcal{P}\in\Part_{2}([n])
.

Proof.

To prove
(a)
,
note that for
\mathcal{P}\in\underline{\mathscr{T}}_{n}

and satisfying
\mathcal{P}(i,i)\not=\mathcal{P}(j,j)
for all
j\not=i\in[n]
, since

\{\mathcal{P}(i,i),\mathcal{P}(j,j)\}\subset\{\mathcal{P}(i,j),\mathcal{P}(j,i)\}

it follows that, without loss of generality,

\mathcal{P}(i,i)=\mathcal{P}(i,j)
and
\mathcal{P}(j,j)=\mathcal{P}(j,i)
.
If
n=2
we are done. If
n>2
, let
k\in[n]\setminus\{i,j\}

and suppose first that
\mathcal{P}(i,k)=\mathcal{P}(k,k)
, whence
\mathcal{P}(k,i)=\mathcal{P}(i,i)
. Then
\mathcal{P}(i,i)=\mathcal{P}(k,i)\in\{\mathcal{P}(k,j),\mathcal{P}(j,i)\}=\{\mathcal{P}(k,j),\mathcal{P}(j,j)\}\subset\{\mathcal{P}(j,j),\mathcal{P}(k,k)\}
which is a contradiction. Thus,

\mathcal{P}(i,k)=\mathcal{P}(i,i)
and then
\mathcal{P}(k,i)=\mathcal{P}(k,k)
. As
\mathcal{P}(k,k)=\mathcal{P}(k,i)\in\{\mathcal{P}(k,j),\mathcal{P}(j,i)\}=\{\mathcal{P}(k,j),\mathcal{P}(j,j)\}
and
\mathcal{P}(k,j)\in\{\mathcal{P}(j,j),\mathcal{P}(k,k)\}
this forces
\mathcal{P}(k,j)=\mathcal{P}(k,k)
and
\mathcal{P}(j,k)=\mathcal{P}(j,j)
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{P}(i,i)=\mathcal{P}(i,j)=\mathcal{P}(i,k)
,
\mathcal{P}(j,i)=\mathcal{P}(j,j)=\mathcal{P}(j,k)
and
\mathcal{P}(k,i)=\mathcal{P}(k,j)=\mathcal{P}(k,k)
.
This implies that
\mathcal{P}(r,s)=\mathcal{P}(r,r)
for all
r,s\in[n]
.

To prove part
(b)
, suppose that

\mathcal{P}(i,i)=\mathcal{P}(j,j)
for some
i\not=j
.
Since
\mathcal{P}(i,i)\in\{\mathcal{P}(i,j),\mathcal{P}(j,i)\}

and
\mathcal{P}(i,j)\not=\mathcal{P}(j,i)
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{P}(i,j)=\mathcal{P}(i,i)=\mathcal{P}(j,j)\not=\mathcal{P}(j,i)
.

Let
k\not=i
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{P}(i,k)\notin\{\mathcal{P}(i,j),\mathcal{P}(j,i)\}
. Then
\mathcal{P}(i,k)\in\{\mathcal{P}(i,j),\mathcal{P}(j,k)\}

forces
\mathcal{P}(j,k)=\mathcal{P}(i,k)
while
\mathcal{P}(i,j)=\mathcal{P}(i,i)\in\{\mathcal{P}(i,k),\mathcal{P}(k,i)\}
yields
\mathcal{P}(k,i)=\mathcal{P}(i,j)
.
But then
\mathcal{P}(j,i)\in\{\mathcal{P}(j,k),\mathcal{P}(k,i)\}=\{\mathcal{P}(i,j),\mathcal{P}(i,k)\}
, which is a contradiction.
If
\mathcal{P}(k,i)\notin\{\mathcal{P}(i,j),\mathcal{P}(j,i)\}
, then

\mathcal{P}(j,i)\in\{\mathcal{P}(j,k),\mathcal{P}(k,i)\}
forces
\mathcal{P}(j,k)=\mathcal{P}(j,i)
.
Since
\mathcal{P}(i,j)=\mathcal{P}(j,j)\in\{\mathcal{P}(j,k),\mathcal{P}(k,j)\}
we have
\mathcal{P}(k,j)=\mathcal{P}(i,j)
. Then
\mathcal{P}(k,i)\in\{\mathcal{P}(k,j),\mathcal{P}(j,i)\}=\{\mathcal{P}(i,j),\mathcal{P}(j,i)\}
which is a contradiction.

Therefore, for any
k\in[n]\setminus\{i,j\}
,
\mathcal{P}(i,k),\mathcal{P}(k,i)\in\{\mathcal{P}(i,j),\mathcal{P}(j,i)\}
.
But then
\mathcal{P}(k,l)\in\{\mathcal{P}(k,i),\mathcal{P}(i,l)\}\subset\{\mathcal{P}(i,j),\mathcal{P}(j,i)\}
for all
k,l\in[n]
. Thus,
\mathcal{P}\in\Part_{2}([n]\times[n])
.
∎

It follows from Corollary
3.9
and Lemmata
3.18
,
3.19
(a)
and
3.2
that
|\mathscr{T}_{n,r}|=2\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{n}{r}
,
while
\mathcal{E}_{\Gamma}([2])=\sum_{1\leq r\leq n}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{n}{r}2^{r}r!=B_{n}
. The assertion is now immediate.
∎

Corollary 3.20
.

Let
n\geq 2
. Then for any finite
C
,
|\mathcal{E}_{\overset{\leftrightarrow\circlearrowleft}{K_{n}}}(C)|=q_{n}(|C|)
where
q_{n}(x)=2x^{n}-x+(\frac{1}{2}B_{n}-2^{n}+1)x(x-1)
. In particular,
|\mathcal{E}_{\overset{\leftrightarrow\circlearrowleft}{K_{n}}}(\{1,-1\})|=q_{n}(2)=B_{n}
.

Proof.

Since
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{n}{2}=2^{n-1}-1
,
n\geq 1
,
by Corollary
3.12

and Proposition
3.14

\displaystyle q_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{\overset{\leftrightarrow\circlearrowleft}{K_n}}{k}(x)_{k}=x+\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}B_{n}-1\Big)x(x-1)+2\sum_{3\leq k\leq n}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{n}{k}(x)_{k}

\displaystyle=x+\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}B_{n}-1\Big)x(x-1)+2x^{n}-2x-2\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{n}{2}x(x-1)

\displaystyle=2x^{n}-x+\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}B_{n}-2^{n}+1\Big)x(x-1).\qed

3.4.
Extensions and restrictions

Given
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
, denote
\mathbf{c}^{-}=\mathbf{c}|_{I_{n-1}}
and define
\mathbf{c}^{+}:I_{n-1}\to C
by
\mathbf{c}^{+}(i,j)=\mathbf{c}(i+1,j+1)
,
(i,j)\in I_{n-1}
.
Furthermore, given
\boldsymbol{\alpha}:[n]\to C
, denote
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{-}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{[n-1]}
and define
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{+}:[n-1]\to C
by

\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{+}(i)=\boldsymbol{\alpha}(i+1)
,
i\in[n-1]
. When convenient,
we identify
\boldsymbol{\alpha}:[n]\to C
with
(\boldsymbol{\alpha}(1),\dots,\boldsymbol{\alpha}(n))\in C^{n}
.
Finally, for any
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}:[n]\to C
we
define
\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:I_{n+1}\to C
by
\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|_{I_{n}}=\mathbf{c}
and
\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(i,n+1)=\boldsymbol{\alpha}(i)
,
i\in[n]
.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}:[n]\to C
.

(a)

\mathbf{c}=(\mathbf{c}^{-})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathbf{c}}}
where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathbf{c}}(i)=\mathbf{c}(i,n)
,

i\in[n-1]
.

(b)

\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is transitive if and only if

\mathbf{c}
is transitive and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}(i)\in\{\mathbf{c}(i,j),\boldsymbol{\alpha}(j)\}
for all
1\leq i<j\leq n
.

(c)

If
\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is transitive, then so are
(\mathbf{c}^{\pm})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\pm}}:I_{n}\to C
.

Proof.

Part
(a)
is immediate from definitions.
To prove part
(b)
, note that
if
\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is transitive then so is
\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|_{I_{n}}
. Furthermore, for all
1\leq i<j\leq n

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}(i)=\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(i,n+1)\in\{\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(i,j),\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(j,n+1)\}=\{\mathbf{c}(i,j),\boldsymbol{\alpha}(j)\}.

Conversely, since
\mathbf{c}
is transitive,
\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(i,k)=\mathbf{c}(i,k)\in\{\mathbf{c}(i,j),\mathbf{c}(j,k)\}=\{\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(i,j),\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(j,k)\}
for
all
1\leq i<j<k\leq n
, while

\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(i,n+1)=\boldsymbol{\alpha}(i)\in\{\mathbf{c}(i,j),\boldsymbol{\alpha}(j)\}=\{\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(i,j),\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(j,n+1)\},\qquad 1\leq i<j\leq n.

To prove part
(c)
, note that if
\mathbf{c}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is transitive, then
\mathbf{c}
is transitive by part
(b)
, hence
\mathbf{c}^{-}
is also transitive, while for all
1\leq i<j<k\leq n-1
we have

\mathbf{c}^{+}(i,k)=\mathbf{c}(i+1,k+1)\in\{\mathbf{c}(i+1,j+1),\mathbf{c}(j+1,k+1)\}=\{\mathbf{c}^{+}(i,j),\mathbf{c}^{+}(j,k)\},

whence
\mathbf{c}^{+}
is transitive.
Furthermore, for all
1\leq i<j\leq n-1
,

\boldsymbol{\alpha}(i)\in\{\mathbf{c}(i,j),\boldsymbol{\alpha}(j)\}=\{\mathbf{c}^{-}(i,j),\boldsymbol{\alpha}(j)\}
while
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{+}(i)=\boldsymbol{\alpha}(i+1)\in\{\mathbf{c}(i+1,j+1),\boldsymbol{\alpha}(j+1)\}=\{\mathbf{c}^{+}(i,j),\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{+}(j)\}
.
Then
(\mathbf{c}^{-})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{-}}
and
(\mathbf{c}^{+})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{+}}
are transitive by part
(b)
.
∎

4.
Main results: the classical case

4.1.
C
-quasi-triangular Lie algebra

Given a set
C
and
n\geq 2
, let
{\mathfrak{qtr}}_{n}(C)
be the Lie algebra with generators
\mathsf{r}_{i,j}^{(c)}
,

i\not=j\in[n]
,
c\in C
subject to relations

(4.1)

\displaystyle[\mathsf{r}_{i,j}^{(c)},\mathsf{r}_{k,l}^{(c^{\prime})}]=0,

\displaystyle i\not=j\not=k\not=l\in[n],

\displaystyle c,c^{\prime}\in C,

(4.2)

\displaystyle[\mathsf{r}_{i,j}^{(c)},\mathsf{r}_{i,k}^{(c^{\prime})}]+[\mathsf{r}_{i,j}^{(c)},\mathsf{r}_{j,k}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}]+[\mathsf{r}_{i,k}^{(c^{\prime})},\mathsf{r}_{j,k}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}]=0,

\displaystyle i\not=j\not=k\in[n],

\displaystyle c^{\prime}\in\{c,c^{\prime\prime}\}\subset C.

We call
{\mathfrak{qtr}}_{n}(C)
a
C
-
quasi-triangular Lie algebra
since
it generalizes the quasi-triangular Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{qtr}}_{n}
defined in
[
5
]
which
is
{\mathfrak{qtr}}_{n}(C)
with
|C|=1
. Clearly,
S_{n}
acts
on
{\mathfrak{qtr}}_{n}(C)
by Lie algebra automorphisms via

\sigma(\mathsf{r}_{i,j}^{(c)})=\mathsf{r}_{\sigma(i),\sigma(j)}^{(c)}
,
i\not=j\in[n]
,
c\in C
,
\sigma\in S_{n}
.

Let
N\geq n
,
C^{\prime}\supset C
and fix a sequence
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\in[N]^{n}
, with
i_{k}\not=i_{l}
for all
1\leq k<l\leq n
. By (
4.1
) and (
4.2
), the assignments
\mathsf{r}_{k,l}^{(c)}\mapsto\mathsf{r}_{i_{k},i_{l}}^{(c)}
,
k,l\in[n]
,
c\in C
,
define a homomorphism of Lie algebras
\psi_{\mathbf{i}}:{\mathfrak{qtr}}_{n}(C)\to{\mathfrak{qtr}}_{N}(C^{\prime})
.
The following is an immediate consequence of Proposition
2.11
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
be a quasi-triangular Lie algebra with a family of classical
r-matrices
\boldsymbol{r}=\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The assignments
\mathsf{r}_{i,j}^{(c)}\mapsto r_{i,j}^{(c)}
,
i\not=j\in[n]
,
c\in C
,
define a homomorphism of Lie algebras
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(n)}:{\mathfrak{qtr}}_{n}(C)\to U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes n}
.

Given
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:I_{n}\to C
,
n\geq 2
define
{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\mathfrak{qtr}}_{2n}(C)

by

(4.3)

{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\mathsf{r}_{j,n+i}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,j))}.

The following is immediate.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
n\geq 3
. Then
for all
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:I_{n}\to C

(4.4)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{[1,2n-1]\setminus\{n\}}({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}\mathsf{r}_{n,i+n}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,n))}

(4.5)

\displaystyle=\psi_{[2,2n]\setminus\{n+1\}}({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{+}})+\sum_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathsf{r}_{i,n+1}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(1,i))}.

We now prove an identity which is the key ingredient in our proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Proposition 4.3
.

For any
m\geq 1
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:I_{m}\to C
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m})\in C^{m}
such that
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:I_{m+1}\to C
is transitive

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}\Big([\psi_{[m+2,2m+1]\cup[2m+3,3m+2]}({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{-}),\mathsf{r}_{m+1,m+1+i}^{(\alpha_{i})}+\mathsf{r}_{m+1,2m+2+i}^{(\alpha_{i})}]

\displaystyle\phantom{\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}}-[\psi_{[1,m]\cup[2m+3,3m+2]}({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{-}),\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,i}^{(\alpha_{i})}+\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,2m+2+i}^{(\alpha_{i})}]

\displaystyle\phantom{\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}}+[\psi_{[1,m]\cup[m+2,2m+1]}({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{-}),\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,i}^{(\alpha_{i})}+\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,m+1+i}^{(\alpha_{i})}]\Big)

(4.6)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\not=j\leq m}[\mathsf{r}_{m+1,i+m+1}^{(\alpha_{i})},\mathsf{r}_{m+1,j+2m+2}^{(\alpha_{j})}]+[\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,i+2m+2}^{(\alpha_{i})},\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}]+[\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,i}^{(\alpha_{i})}\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,j+m+1}^{(\alpha_{j})}]

in
{\mathfrak{qtr}}_{3m+3}(C)
,
where
{\mathfrak{j}}^{-}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}={\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-\sigma_{m}({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})
and

\sigma_{m}\in S_{2m}
is the Grassmann permutation, that is
\sigma_{m}(i)=i+m
if
i\in[n]
,
\sigma_{m}(i)=i-m
if
i\in[m+1,2m]
.

Proof.

We use induction on
m
. The induction base is trivial since both sides of the identity are equal to zero for
m=1
.

To prove the inductive step, abbreviate
\gamma_{i,j}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,j)
,
1\leq i<j\leq m
and denote
\mathscr{L}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
(respectively,
\mathscr{R}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
) the left (respectively, the right) hand side of (
4.6
), the latter being independent of
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
.
By (
5.4
) we have

{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{-}={\mathfrak{j}}^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq m-1}\mathsf{r}_{m,i+m}^{(\gamma_{i,m})}-\sum_{1\leq i\leq m-1}\mathsf{r}_{2m,i}^{(\gamma_{i,m})}

where we abbreviate
{\mathfrak{j}}^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\psi_{[1,m-1]\cup[m+1,2m-1]}(({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})^{-})
.
Since

\displaystyle\psi_{[m+2,2m+1]\cup[2m+3,3m+2]}(\mathsf{r}_{m,i+m}^{(\gamma_{i,m})}-\mathsf{r}_{2m,i}^{(\gamma_{i,m})})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{r}_{2m+1,2m+i+2}^{(\gamma_{i,m})}-\mathsf{r}_{3m+2,i+m+1}^{(\gamma_{i,m})},

\displaystyle\psi_{[1,m]\cup[2m+3,3m+2]}(\mathsf{r}_{m,i+m}^{(\gamma_{i,m})}-\mathsf{r}_{2m,i}^{(\gamma_{i,m})})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{r}_{m,2m+i+2}^{(\gamma_{i,m})}-\mathsf{r}_{3m+2,i}^{(\gamma_{i,m})},

\displaystyle\psi_{[1,m]\cup[m+2,2m+1]}(\mathsf{r}_{m,i+m}^{(\gamma_{i,m})}-\mathsf{r}_{2m,i}^{(\gamma_{i,m})})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{r}_{m,m+i+1}^{(\gamma_{i,m})}-\mathsf{r}_{2m+1,i}^{(\gamma_{i,m})}

we can write

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}\Big([\psi_{[m+2,2m+1]\cup[2m+3,3m+2]}({\mathfrak{j}}^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}),\mathsf{r}_{m+1,m+1+i}^{(\alpha_{i})}+\mathsf{r}_{m+1,2m+2+i}^{(\alpha_{i})}]

\displaystyle\phantom{\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}}-[\psi_{[1,m]\cup[2m+3,3m+2]}({\mathfrak{j}}^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}),\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,i}^{(\alpha_{i})}+\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,2m+2+i}^{(\alpha_{i})}]

\displaystyle\phantom{\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}}+[\psi_{[1,m]\cup[m+2,2m+1]}({\mathfrak{j}}^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}),\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,i}^{(\alpha_{i})}+\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,m+1+i}^{(\alpha_{i})}]\Big)+\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\alpha}),

where

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\alpha}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq t\leq m-1\\
1\leq s\leq m\end{subarray}}\Big(

\displaystyle[\mathsf{r}_{2m+1,2m+t+2}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}-\mathsf{r}_{3m+2,t+m+1}^{(\gamma_{t,m})},\mathsf{r}_{m+1,m+1+s}^{(\alpha_{s})}+\mathsf{r}_{m+1,2m+2+s}^{(\alpha_{s})}]

\displaystyle-[\mathsf{r}_{m,2m+t+2}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}-\mathsf{r}_{3m+2,t}^{(\gamma_{t,m})},\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,s}^{(\alpha_{s})}+\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,2m+2+s}^{(\alpha_{s})}]

\displaystyle+[\mathsf{r}_{m,m+t+1}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}-\mathsf{r}_{2m+1,t}^{(\gamma_{t,m})},\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,s}^{(\alpha_{s})}+\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,m+1+s}^{(\alpha_{s})}]\Big)

\displaystyle\mskip-100.0mu=\sum_{1\leq t\leq m-1}\Big(-[\mathsf{r}_{m+1,2m+t+2}^{(\alpha_{t})},\mathsf{r}_{2m+1,2m+t+2}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}]-[\mathsf{r}_{m+1,2m+1}^{(\alpha_{m})},\mathsf{r}_{2m+1,2m+t+2}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}]

\displaystyle\mskip-100.0mu\phantom{=\sum_{1\leq t\leq m-1}\Big(}+[\mathsf{r}_{m+1,m+t+1}^{(\alpha_{t})},\mathsf{r}_{3m+2,m+t+1}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}]+[\mathsf{r}_{m+1,3m+2}^{(\alpha_{m})},\mathsf{r}_{3m+2,m+t+1}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}]

\displaystyle\mskip-100.0mu\phantom{=\sum_{1\leq t\leq m-1}\Big(}+[\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,2m+t+2}^{(\alpha_{t})},\mathsf{r}_{m,2m+t+2}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}]+[\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,m}^{(\alpha_{m})},\mathsf{r}_{m,2m+t+2}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}]

\displaystyle\mskip-100.0mu\phantom{=\sum_{1\leq t\leq m-1}\Big(}-[\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,t}^{(\alpha_{t})},\mathsf{r}_{3m+2,t}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}]-[\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,3m+2}^{(\alpha_{m})},\mathsf{r}_{3m+2,t}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}]

\displaystyle\mskip-100.0mu\phantom{=\sum_{1\leq t\leq m-1}\Big(}-[\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,m+t+1}^{(\alpha_{t})},\mathsf{r}_{m,m+t+1}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}]-[\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,m}^{(\alpha_{m})},\mathsf{r}_{m,m+t+1}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}]

\displaystyle\mskip-100.0mu\phantom{=\sum_{1\leq t\leq m-1}\Big(}+[\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,t}^{(\alpha_{t})},\mathsf{r}_{2m+1,t}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}]+[\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,2m+1}^{(\alpha_{m})},\mathsf{r}_{2m+1,t}^{(\gamma_{t,m})}]\Big).

Since
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is transitive,

\alpha_{t}\in\{\gamma_{t,m},\alpha_{m}\}
,
1\leq t\leq m-1
by Lemma
3.21
(b)
. Using (
4.2
)
with
(c,c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime})=(\alpha_{m},\alpha_{t},\gamma_{t,m})
and, respectively,

(i,j,k)\in\{(m+1,2m+1,2m+t+2),(m+1,3m+2,m+t+1),(2m+2,m,2m+t+2),\\
(2m+2,3m+2,t),(3m+3,m,m+t+1),(3m+3,2m+1,t)\}

we obtain

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{m}

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\sum_{1\leq t\leq m-1}[\mathsf{r}_{m+1,2m+1}^{(\alpha_{m})},\mathsf{r}_{m+1,2m+t+2}^{(\alpha_{t})}]-[\mathsf{r}_{m+1,3m+2}^{(\alpha_{m})},\mathsf{r}_{m+1,m+t+1}^{(\alpha_{t})}]

\displaystyle-[\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,m}^{(\alpha_{m})},\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,2m+t+2}^{(\alpha_{t})}]+[\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,3m+2}^{(\alpha_{m})},\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,t}^{(\alpha_{t})}]

\displaystyle+[\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,m}^{(\alpha_{m})},\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,m+t+1}^{(\alpha_{t})}]-[\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,2m+1}^{(\alpha_{m})},\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,t}^{(\alpha_{t})}],

whence

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}

{}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\not=j\leq m-1}[\mathsf{r}_{m+1,i+m+1}^{(\alpha_{i})},\mathsf{r}_{m+1,j+2m+2}^{(\alpha_{j})}]+[\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,i+2m+2}^{(\alpha_{i})},\mathsf{r}_{2m+2,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}]+[\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,i}^{(\alpha_{i})},\mathsf{r}_{3m+3,j+m+1}^{(\alpha_{j})}]

\displaystyle=\psi_{[1,3m+3]\setminus\{m,2m+1,3m+2\}}\Big(\sum_{1\leq i\not=j\leq m-1}[\mathsf{r}_{m,i+m}^{(\alpha_{i})},\mathsf{r}_{m,j+2m}^{(\alpha_{j})}]+[\mathsf{r}_{2m,i+2m}^{(\alpha_{i})},\mathsf{r}_{2m,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}]+[\mathsf{r}_{3n,i}^{(\alpha_{i})},\mathsf{r}_{3n,j+m}^{(\alpha_{j})}]\Big)

(4.7)

\displaystyle=\psi_{[1,3m+3]\setminus\{m,2m+1,3m+2\}}(\mathscr{R}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{-})).

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\psi_{[m+2,2m+1]\cup[2m+3,3m+2]}({\mathfrak{j}}^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})

\displaystyle=\psi_{[m+2,2m]\cup[2m+3,3m+1]}(({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})^{-})

\displaystyle=\psi_{[1,3m+3]\setminus\{m,2m+1,3m+2\}}\circ\psi_{[m+1,2m-1]\cup[2m+1,3m-1]}(({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})^{-}),

\displaystyle\psi_{[1,m]\cup[2m+3,3m+2]}({\mathfrak{j}}^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})

\displaystyle=\psi_{[1,m-1]\cup[2m+3,3m+1]}(({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})^{-})

\displaystyle=\psi_{[1,3m+3]\setminus\{m,2m+1,3m+2\}}\circ\psi_{[1,m-1]\cup[2m+1,3m-1]}(({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})^{-}),

\displaystyle\psi_{[1,m]\cup[m+2,2m+1]}({\mathfrak{j}}^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})

\displaystyle=\psi_{[1,m-1]\cup[m+2,2m]}(({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})^{-})

\displaystyle=\psi_{[1,3m+3]\setminus\{m,2m+1,3m+2\}}\circ\psi_{[1,m-1]\cup[m+1,2m-1]}(({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})^{-}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}

{}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\psi_{[1,3m+3]\setminus\{m,2m+1,3m+2\}}\Big(\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}\Big([\psi_{[m+1,2m-1]\cup[2m+1,3m-1]}(({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})^{-}),\mathsf{r}_{m,m+i}^{(\alpha_{i})}+\mathsf{r}_{m,2m+i}^{(\alpha_{i})}]

\displaystyle\phantom{=\psi_{[1,3m+3]\setminus\{m,2m+1,3m+2\}}\Big(\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}}-[\psi_{[1,m-1]\cup[2m+1,3m-1]}(({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})^{-}),\mathsf{r}_{2m,i}^{(\alpha_{i})}+\mathsf{r}_{2m,2m+i}^{(\alpha_{i})}]

\displaystyle\phantom{=\psi_{[1,3m+3]\setminus\{m,2m+1,3m+2\}}\Big(\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}}+[\psi_{[1,m-1]\cup[m+1,2m-1]}(({\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})^{-}),\mathsf{r}_{3n,i}^{(\alpha_{i})}+\mathsf{r}_{3n,m+i}^{(\alpha_{i})}]\Big)\Big).

The terms corresponding to
i=m
