in these sums are equal zero by (
4.1
), whence

\mathscr{L}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\psi_{[1,3m+3]\setminus\{m,2m+1,3m+2\}}(\mathscr{L}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{-})).

It follows from (
4.7
) and the above that
\mathscr{L}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\mathscr{R}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})

if and only if

\psi_{[1,3m+3]\setminus\{m,2m+1,3m+2\}}(\mathscr{L}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{-})-\mathscr{R}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha^{-}}))=0.

Yet
(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{-}}
is transitive by Lemma
3.21
(c)
,
and so
\mathscr{L}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{-})-\mathscr{R}_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha^{-}})=0
by the induction hypothesis. This
completes the proof of the inductive step and hence of the Proposition.
∎

4.2.
A family of classical twists

We can now establish the main result of this section.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
be a Lie bialgebra with a family
\mathbf{r}=\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}
of classical r-matrices for
\delta
. Then for any
n\geq 2
and any
transitive
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
,
j_{\mathbf{c}}=j_{\mathbf{c}}(\mathbf{r}):=\Psi_{\mathbf{r}}^{(2n)}({\mathfrak{j}}_{\mathbf{c}})\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}\subset U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes n}\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes n}
is a classical Drinfeld
twist for
({\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}})
.

Proof.

The argument is by induction on
n
. The case
n=2
was established in Proposition
2.16
. To prove the inductive step, note
that
j_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}
is a classical Drinfeld twist for
({\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus(n-1)},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus(n-1)}})
by the induction hypothesis.
We need the following

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
{\mathfrak{a}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus(n-1)}
with
\delta_{{\mathfrak{a}}}

being the natural Lie bialgebra structure
\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus(n-1)}}
twisted by

j_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}(\mathbf{r})
and let
{\mathfrak{b}}={\mathfrak{g}}

with
\delta_{{\mathfrak{b}}}=\delta
. Then
\mathbf{f}=\sum\limits_{1\leq i\leq n-1}r_{1,i+1}^{(\mathbf{c}(i,n))}\in{\mathfrak{b}}\otimes{\mathfrak{a}}
is a relative Drinfeld twist for
({\mathfrak{a}}\oplus{\mathfrak{b}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{a}}\oplus{\mathfrak{b}}})
.

Proof.

Abbreviate

c_{i,k}=\mathbf{c}(i,k)
,
1\leq i<k\leq n
,

\mathbf{i}=[n(i-1)+1,ni-1]
,
i\in\{1,3,5\}
,

\mathbf{k}=\{n(k-1)\}
,
k\in\{2,4,6\}

and identify
U({\mathfrak{a}})
with
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes(n-1)}
as an associative algebra.
By Proposition
2.9
(d)
,

(\delta_{{\mathfrak{b}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{a}}})(r_{1,i+1}^{(c)})=[r_{2,i+2}^{(c)},r_{1,i+2}^{(c)}]
,

c\in C
,
1\leq i\leq n-1
, whence

\displaystyle(\id_{(U({\mathfrak{a}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{b}}))^{\otimes 3}}

\displaystyle-\tau_{\mathbf{4},\mathbf{6}}\tau_{\mathbf{3},\mathbf{5}}+\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{4}}\tau_{\mathbf{4},\mathbf{6}}\tau_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{5}})((\delta_{{\mathfrak{b}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{a}}})(\mathbf{f})_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{4},\mathbf{5}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}[r_{2n,2n+i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{n,2n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}]-[r_{3n,n+i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{n,n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}]+[r_{3n,i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{2n,i}^{(c_{i,n)}}].

Furthermore, we have in
(U({\mathfrak{a}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{b}}))^{\otimes 3}

\displaystyle[\mathbf{f}

{}_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}},\mathbf{f}_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{5}}]+[\mathbf{f}_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{5}},\mathbf{f}_{\mathbf{4},\mathbf{5}}]+[\mathbf{f}_{\mathbf{4},\mathbf{5}},\mathbf{f}_{\mathbf{4},\mathbf{1}}]+[\mathbf{f}_{\mathbf{4},\mathbf{1}},\mathbf{f}_{\mathbf{6},\mathbf{1}}]+[\mathbf{f}_{\mathbf{6},\mathbf{1}},\mathbf{f}_{\mathbf{6},\mathbf{3}}]+[\mathbf{f}_{\mathbf{6},\mathbf{3}},\mathbf{f}_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i,k\leq n-1}[r_{n,i+n}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{n,k+2n}^{(c_{k,n})}]+[r_{n,k+2n}^{(c_{k,n})},r_{2n,i+2n}^{(c_{i,n})}]+[r_{2n,i+2n}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{2n,k}^{(c_{k,n})}]+[r_{2n,k}^{(c_{k,n})},r_{3n,i}^{(c_{i,n})}]

\displaystyle\phantom{=\sum_{1\leq i,k\leq n-1}}+[r_{3n,i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{3n,k+n}^{(c_{k,n})}]+[r_{3n,k+n}^{(c_{k,n})},r_{n,i+n}^{(c_{i,n})}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i,k\leq n-1}[r_{n,i+n}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{n,k+2n}^{(c_{k,n})}]+[r_{2n,i+2n}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{2n,k}^{(c_{k,n})}]+[r_{3n,i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{3n,k+n}^{(c_{k,n})}]

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}[r_{n,i+2n}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{2n,i+2n}^{(c_{i,n})}]+[r_{2n,i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{3n,i}^{(c_{i,n})}]+[r_{3n,i+n}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{n,i+n}^{(c_{i,n})}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i,k\leq n-1}[r_{n,i+n}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{n,k+2n}^{(c_{k,n})}]+[r_{2n,i+2n}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{2n,k}^{(c_{k,n})}]+[r_{3n,i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{3n,k+n}^{(c_{k,n})}]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-(\id_{(U({\mathfrak{a}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{b}}))^{\otimes 3}}-\tau_{\mathbf{4},\mathbf{6}}\tau_{\mathbf{3},\mathbf{5}}+\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{4}}\tau_{\mathbf{4},\mathbf{6}}\tau_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{5}})((\delta_{{\mathfrak{b}}}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{a}}})(\mathbf{f})_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{4},\mathbf{5}}).

Thus, by Proposition
2.15
, it remains to prove that

\displaystyle(\id_{(U({\mathfrak{a}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{b}}))^{\otimes 3}}

\displaystyle-\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{4}}\tau_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{3}}+\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{6}}\tau_{\mathbf{3},\mathbf{5}}\tau_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{3}})((\id_{{\mathfrak{b}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{a}}})(\mathbf{f})_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3},\mathbf{5}})

(4.8)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i,k\leq n-1}[r_{n,i+n}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{n,k+2n}^{(c_{k,n})}]+[r_{2n,i+2n}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{2n,k}^{(c_{k,n})}]+[r_{3n,i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{3n,k+n}^{(c_{k,n})}].

By Proposition
2.9
(d)
and the definition of
\delta_{{\mathfrak{a}}}
we have in
U({\mathfrak{b}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{a}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{a}})

\displaystyle(\id_{{\mathfrak{b}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{a}}})(r_{1,i+1}^{(c)})=[r_{1,i+1}^{(c)},r_{1,n+i}^{(c)}]+[1\otimes(j_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})^{-},r_{1,i+1}^{(c)}+r_{1,n+i}^{(c)}],

whence

(\id_{{\mathfrak{b}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{a}}})(\mathbf{f})_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3},\mathbf{5}}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}[r_{n,n+i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{n,2n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}]+[(j_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})^{-}_{[n+1,2n-1]\cup[2n+1,3n-1]},r_{n,n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}+r_{n,2n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}].

Therefore,

\displaystyle(

\displaystyle\id_{(U({\mathfrak{a}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{b}}))^{\otimes 3}}-\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{4}}\tau_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{3}}+\tau_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{6}}\tau_{\mathbf{3},\mathbf{5}}\tau_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{3}})((\id_{{\mathfrak{b}}}\otimes\delta_{{\mathfrak{a}}})(\mathbf{f})_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3},\mathbf{5}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}[r_{n,n+i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{n,2n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}]-[r_{2n,i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{2n,2n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}]+[r_{3n,i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{3n,n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}\Big([(j_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})^{-}_{[n+1,2n-1]\cup[2n+1,3n-1]},r_{n,n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}+r_{n,2n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}]

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\quad-[(j_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})^{-}_{[1,n-1]\cup[2n+1,3n-1]},r_{2n,i}^{(c_{i,n})}+r_{2n,2n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}]+[(j_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})^{-}_{[1,n-1]\cup[n+1,2n-1]},r_{3n,i}^{(c_{i,n})}+r_{3n,n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}]\Big),

and so (
4.8
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}\Big([(j_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})^{-}_{[n+1,2n-1]\cup[2n+1,3n-1]},r_{n,n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}+r_{n,2n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}]

\displaystyle\phantom{\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}}-[(j_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})^{-}_{[1,n-1]\cup[2n+1,3n-1]},r_{2n,i}^{(c_{i,n})}+r_{2n,2n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}]+[(j_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})^{-}_{[1,n-1]\cup[n+1,2n-1]},r_{3n,i}^{(c_{i,n})}+r_{3n,n+i}^{(c_{i,n})}]\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\not=k\leq n-1}[r_{n,i+n}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{n,k+2n}^{(c_{k,n})}]+[r_{2n,i+2n}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{2n,k}^{(c_{k,n})}]+[r_{3n,i}^{(c_{i,n})},r_{3n,k+n}^{(c_{k,n})}].

Since
\mathbf{c}=(\mathbf{c}^{-})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathbf{c}}}
by Lemma
3.21
(a)
and hence is transitive,
it remains to use Proposition
4.3
with
m=n-1
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\mathbf{c}^{-}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathbf{c}}

and apply the homomorphism
\Psi^{(3n)}_{\mathbf{r}}
.
∎

By Lemma
4.2
,
j_{\mathbf{c}}=(j_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})_{[1,2n-1]\setminus\{n\}}+\mathbf{f}_{[n,2n-1]}=(j_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{3}}+\mathbf{f}_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}}
, where
\mathbf{1}=[n-1]
,
\mathbf{2}=\{n\}
,
\mathbf{3}=[n+1,2n-1]
.
Then by Proposition
4.5
and Corollary
2.20
,
j_{\mathbf{c}}
is a classical Drinfeld twist for
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
with its standard cobracket
\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}}
.
∎

Corollary 4.6
.

For any transitive
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
,
\sum\limits_{2\leq i\leq n}r_{i-1,n}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,i))}
is a relative Drinfeld twist
for
({\mathfrak{a}}\oplus{\mathfrak{b}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{a}}\oplus{\mathfrak{b}}})
where
({\mathfrak{a}},\delta_{{\mathfrak{a}}})=({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
and
{\mathfrak{b}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus(n-1)}
with
\delta_{{\mathfrak{b}}}
obtained by twisting
\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus(n-1)}}
by
j_{\mathbf{c}^{+}}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Lemma
4.2
, Proposition
2.18

and Theorem
4.4
.
∎

4.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.3

We now have all necessary ingredients to the main
result announced in the Introduction in the classical case.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Let
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
be transitive
and let
\boldsymbol{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\in C^{n}
.
Then
\mathbf{r}^{(\diag\boldsymbol{d})}=\sum_{i\in[n]}r_{i,i+n}^{(d_{i})}

is a classical r-matrix for
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}

with its standard cobracket
\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}}
.
Since
j_{\mathbf{c}}=j_{\mathbf{c}}(\mathbf{r})
is a classical Drinfeld twist
by Theorem
4.4
,
it follows from Proposition
2.14

(b)
that
\mathbf{r}^{(\diag\boldsymbol{d})}+j_{\mathbf{c}}^{-}
is a classical r-matrix for
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
. It remains to observe that

\mathbf{r}^{(\diag\boldsymbol{d})}+j_{\mathbf{c}}^{-}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}r_{i,i+n}^{(d_{i})}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}r_{j,i+n}^{(\mathbf{c}(i,j))}-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}r_{j+n,i}^{(\mathbf{c}(i,j))}=\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{d}).\qed

Remark 4.7
.

Let
r
be an r-matrix and let
\mathbf{r}=\{r^{(1)},r^{(-1)}\}=\{r,-\tau(r)\}
.
Since
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w):I_{n}\to\{1,-1\}
is transitive
by Lemma
3.1
,

\mathbf{r}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w),\boldsymbol{d})
,

\boldsymbol{d}\in\{1,-1\}^{n}
is an r-matrix for
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
by Lemma
2.12
and Theorem
1.3
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w),\boldsymbol{d})

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}r_{i,i+n}^{(d_{i})}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(r_{j,i+n}^{(\sign(w(j)-w(i)))}-r_{j+n,i}^{(\sign(w(j)-w(i)))})

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}r_{i,i+n}^{(d_{i})}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}r_{j,i+n}^{(\sign(w(j)-w(i)))}+r_{i,j+n}^{(\sign(w(i)-w(j)))}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\in[n]}r_{i,j+n}^{(d_{i}\delta_{i,j}+\sign(w(j)-w(i)))}=\mathbf{r}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w,\boldsymbol{d}))},

which proves Theorem
1.1
.

Remark 4.8
.

Since
w
acts on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
by permutation of factors and
(w\otimes w)(\mathbf{r}^{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(\id,\boldsymbol{d}))})=\mathbf{r}^{(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w,w(\boldsymbol{d})))}
,
it follows that all r-matrices described in Theorem
1.1
are equivalent
to
\mathbf{r}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(\id),\boldsymbol{d})

for some
\boldsymbol{d}\in\{1,-1\}^{n}
,
and the corresponding bialgebra
structures on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
are isomorphic.

4.4.
Diagonal embedding

Given a Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, the diagonal map
\Delta^{(n)}:{\mathfrak{g}}\to{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
,

x\mapsto(x,\dots,x)
,
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
, is obviously a homomorphism of Lie algebras.
However, if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a Lie bialgebra with the cobracket
\delta:{\mathfrak{g}}\to{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
, then
\Delta^{(n)}

is not a homomorphism of Lie bialgebras
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)\to({\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n},\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}})
.

Remark 4.9
.

The notation
\Delta^{(n)}
is justified by the fact that, on the level of universal enveloping algebras, the diagonal embedding
{\mathfrak{g}}\hookrightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}

corresponds to the iterated comultiplication
\Delta^{(n)}:U({\mathfrak{g}})\to U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes n}
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
be a quasi-triangular Lie bialgebra with a family
\mathbf{r}=\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}
of classical r-matrices corresponding to
\delta
.
Then for
n\geq 2
and
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
transitive,
\Delta^{(n)}
is a homomorphism of Lie bialgebras

({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)\to({\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n},\tilde{\delta}_{\mathbf{c}})
where
\tilde{\delta}_{\mathbf{c}}:=\widetilde{\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}}}_{j_{\mathbf{c}}(\mathbf{r})}

is the standard cobracket
\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
twisted by
j_{\mathbf{c}}(\mathbf{r})
.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in C^{n}
and let
\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

be as in Theorem
1.3
.
Let
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
. Then
\Delta^{(n)}(x)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}x_{i}
in
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes n}

where
x_{i}=1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes x\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-i)}
and so

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}_{\mathbf{c}}

\displaystyle(\Delta^{(n)}(x))=[\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{\alpha}),\Delta_{U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes n}}(\Delta^{(n)}(x))]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in[n]}\Big(\sum_{i\in[n]}[r_{i,n+i}^{(\alpha_{i})},x_{k}+x_{n+k}]+\sum_{(i,j)\in I_{n}}[r_{j,n+i}^{(\mathbf{c}(i,j))},x_{k}+x_{n+k}]+[-r_{n+j,i}^{(\mathbf{c}(i,j))},x_{k}+x_{n+k}]\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]}[r_{i,n+i}^{(\alpha_{i})},x_{i}+x_{n+i}]+\sum_{(i,j)\in I_{n}}[r_{j,n+i}^{(\mathbf{c}(i,j))},x_{j}+x_{n+i}]+[-r_{n+j,i}^{(\mathbf{c}(i,j))},x_{i}+x_{n+j}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]}[r^{(\alpha_{i})},\Delta(x)]_{i,n+i}+\sum_{(i,j)\in I_{n}}[r^{(\mathbf{c}(i,j))},\Delta(x)]_{j,n+i}+[-\tau(r^{(\mathbf{c}(i,j))}),\Delta(x)]_{i,n+j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\delta(x)_{i,j+n}=(\Delta^{(n)}\otimes\Delta^{(n)})(\delta(x)),

since
[r^{(c)},\Delta(x)]=[-\tau(r^{(c)}),\Delta(x)]=\delta(x)
for all
c\in C
,

x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
.
∎

We now establish the necessary and sufficient condition
on our family
\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}

for
\mathbf{r}^{(\mathbf{c})}
to be an r-matrix for
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
, assuming that it satisfies CYBE.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\mathbf{r}=\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
be such that
r^{(c)}+r_{2,1}^{(c)}
,
c\in C
is
{\mathfrak{g}}
-invariant. Then for any
\mathbf{c}:[n]\times[n]\to C
,
\mathbf{r}^{(\mathbf{c})}+\mathbf{r}^{(\mathbf{c})}{}^{op}
is
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
-invariant if and only if

r^{(c_{i,k})}+\tau(r^{(c_{k,i})})\in{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})
for all
(i,k)\in I_{n}
, where
{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})

is the center of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\mathbf{f}\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
is
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\oplus n}
-invariant
if and only if
[\mathbf{f},x_{k}+x_{k+n}]=0
for all
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
and
k\in[n]
, where, as before,
x_{r}=1^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes x\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-r)}
,
r\in[n]
.
We have

\displaystyle[\mathbf{r}^{(\mathbf{c})}

\displaystyle+\mathbf{r}^{(\mathbf{c})}{}^{op},x_{k}+x_{k+n}]=\sum_{i,j\in[n]}[r_{i,j+n}^{(c_{j,i})}+r_{i+n,j}^{(c_{j,i})},x_{k}+x_{k+n}]

\displaystyle=[r_{k,k+n}^{(c_{k,k})}+r_{k+n,k}^{(c_{k,k})},x_{k}+x_{k+n}]+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{k\}}([r_{k,i+n}^{(c_{i,k})}+r_{i+n,k}^{(c_{k,i})},x_{k}]+[r_{i,k+n}^{(c_{k,i})}+r_{k+n,i}^{(c_{i,k})},x_{k+n}])

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{k\}}[r_{k,i+n}^{(c_{i,k})}+r_{i+n,k}^{(c_{k,i})},x_{k}]+\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{k\}}[r_{k+n,i}^{(c_{i,k})}+r_{i,k+n}^{(c_{k,i})},x_{k+n}]

since the first term is
[r^{(c_{k,k})}+\tau(r^{(c_{k,k})}),x\otimes 1+1\otimes x]_{k,k+n}=0
as
r^{(c)}+\tau(r^{(c)})
is
{\mathfrak{g}}
-invariant for all
c\in C
. Since the second sum
is obtained by applying
op
to the first,
it follows that

[\mathbf{r}^{(\mathbf{c})}+\mathbf{r}^{(\mathbf{c})}{}^{op},x_{k}+x_{k+n}]=0

if and only if

\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus\{k\}}[r_{k,i+n}^{(c_{i,k})}+r_{i+n,k}^{(c_{k,i})},x_{k}]=0,

which in turn is equivalent to

\sum_{1\leq i\leq k-1}[(\tau(r^{(c_{i,k})})+r^{(c_{k,i})})_{i,k},x_{k}]+\sum_{k+1\leq i\leq n}[(r^{(c_{i,k})}+\tau(r^{(c_{k,i})}))_{k,i},x_{k}]=0

as an element of
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes n}
. Since
x

is arbitrary, this is equivalent to

\sum_{1\leq i\leq k-1}(\tau(r^{(c_{i,k})})+r^{(c_{k,i})})_{i,k}+\sum_{k+1\leq i\leq n}(r^{(c_{i,k})}+\tau(r^{(c_{k,i})}))_{k,i}\in U_{1}({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U_{1}({\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes(n-k)},

or, finally, to
r^{(c_{i,k})}+\tau(r^{(c_{k,i})})\in({\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}}))\cap({\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})
,
i\not=k\in[n]
.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

The condition that
r^{(c)}+r_{2,1}^{(c)}
is an invariant
for each
c\in C
is reminiscent of
[
19
]
. However,
the authors do not consider CYBE involving more than one member of the family
\{r^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}
.

4.5.
Poisson algebras and proof of Theorem
1.6

Let
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
be transitive. By Proposition
2.21
,
\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}

acquires a Poisson algebra structure via

\{f,f^{\prime}\}_{\mathbf{c}}:=\mu(\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{d})\bowtie(f\otimes f^{\prime})),\qquad f,f^{\prime}\in\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}

for any
\boldsymbol{d}\in C^{n}
, where
\mu:\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}
is the multiplication map. It should be noted that this bracket is independent of
\boldsymbol{d}
, which explains the notation. More explicitly,
\mathbb{k}[G]
is
generated, as an algebra, by
f^{(k)}:=1^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes f\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-k)}
,
f\in\mathbb{k}[G]
,

k\in[n]
. Thus, the Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathbf{c}}
is uniquely determined
by
\{f^{\prime}{}^{(k^{\prime})},f^{(k)}\}_{\mathbf{c}}
where
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathbb{k}[G]
and
k\leq k^{\prime}\in[n]
.
Furthermore, by the definition (
1.6
) of
\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{c},\boldsymbol{d})
and Proposition
2.21
,

(4.9)

\{f^{\prime}{}^{(k^{\prime})},f^{(k)}\}_{\mathbf{c}}=\begin{cases}(r^{(\mathbf{c}(k,k^{\prime}))}\bowtie(f^{\prime}\otimes f))_{k,k^{\prime}},&k<k^{\prime},\\
\{f^{\prime},f\}^{(k)},&k=k^{\prime},\end{cases}

for all
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathbb{k}[G]
,
k\leq k^{\prime}\in[n]
, where
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is the Poisson bracket on
\mathbb{k}[G]

corresponding to
r^{(c)}
for
any

c\in C
.

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

It suffices to prove the assertion for
generators
f^{(k)}
,
f\in\mathbb{k}[G]
,
k\in[n]
. The iterated multiplication map

\mu^{(n)}:\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{k}[G]
is then given by

f^{(k)}\mapsto f
,
f\in\mathbb{k}[G]
. Also, since the Poisson bracket is skew-symmetric, it suffices to prove that
\mu^{(n)}(\{f^{\prime}{}^{(k^{\prime})},f^{(k)}\}_{\mathbf{c}})=\{f^{\prime},f\}

for
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathbb{k}[G]
and
k\leq k^{\prime}\in[n]
. This is obvious for
k=k^{\prime}
, while for
k<k^{\prime}
,

\mu^{(n)}(\{f^{\prime}{}^{(k^{\prime})},f^{(k)}\})=\mu(r^{(\mathbf{c}(k,k^{\prime}))}\bowtie(f^{\prime}\otimes f))=\{f^{\prime},f\}
for all
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathbb{k}[G]
.
∎

5.
Main results: the quantum case

5.1.
C
-quasi-triangular monoid

Let
C
be a set and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
.
The
C
-
quasi-triangular monoid

\mathsf{QTr}^{+}_{n}(C)
is generated by
the
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}^{(c)}
,
1\leq i\not=j\leq n
,
c\in C
subjects to relations

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{i,j}^{(c)}\mathsf{R}_{k,l}^{(c^{\prime})}=\mathsf{R}_{k,l}^{(c^{\prime})}\mathsf{R}_{i,j}^{(c)},

\displaystyle i\not=j\not=k\not=l\in[n],

\displaystyle c,c^{\prime}\in C

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{i,j}^{(c)}\mathsf{R}_{i,k}^{(c^{\prime})}\mathsf{R}_{j,k}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}=\mathsf{R}_{j,k}^{(c^{\prime\prime})}\mathsf{R}_{i,k}^{(c^{\prime})}\mathsf{R}_{i,j}^{(c)},

\displaystyle i\not=j\not=k\in[n],

\displaystyle c^{\prime}\in\{c,c^{\prime\prime}\}\subset C.

For
|C|=1
, it
has the same defining relations as the quasi-triangular group
\mathsf{QTr}_{n}

introduced in
[
5
]
.

Let
N\geq n
, let
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\in[N]^{n}
with
i_{k}\not=i_{l}
,
1\leq k<l\leq n
and let
C^{\prime}\supset C
. Then the assignments
\mathsf{R}_{k,l}^{(c)}\mapsto\mathsf{R}_{i_{k},i_{l}}^{(c)}
,
1\leq k<l\leq n
,
c\in C

define a homomorphism of monoids
\phi_{\mathbf{i}}:\mathsf{QTr}^{+}_{n}(C)\to\mathsf{QTr}^{+}_{N}(C^{\prime})
.

Let
H
be a bialgebra and suppose that
\boldsymbol{R}=\{R^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}
is a family of R-matrices for
H
with respect to the same comultiplication. It follows from Proposition
2.23
that
the assignments
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}^{(c)}\mapsto R_{i,j}^{(c)}
,
i\not=j\in[n]
,
c\in C
, define a homomorphism of monoids

\Phi^{(n)}_{\boldsymbol{R}}:\mathsf{QTr}^{+}_{n}(C)\to H^{\otimes n}
for any
n\geq 2
.

Mirroring (
4.3
) we define, for any
n\geq 2
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:I_{n}\to C
,

(5.3)

\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}\,\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq i-1}\mathsf{R}_{i,n+j}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(j,i))}\in\mathsf{QTr}^{+}_{2n}(C).

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
n\geq 3
. Then
for all
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:I_{n}\to C

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\phi_{[1,2n-1]\setminus\{n\}}(\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq n-1}\mathsf{R}_{n,i+n}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,n))}

(5.5)

\displaystyle=\phi_{[2,2n]\setminus\{n+1\}}(\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{+}})\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathsf{R}_{i,n+1}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(1,i))}.

Proof.

Abbreviate
\gamma_{ij}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,j)
,
1\leq i<j\leq n
.
We have

\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n-1}\,\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq i-1}\mathsf{R}^{(\gamma_{j,i})}_{i,n+j}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\mathsf{R}_{n,j+n}^{(\gamma_{j,n})}\Big).

Since

\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}}=\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n-1}\,\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq i-1}\mathsf{R}_{i,n-1+j}^{(\gamma_{ji})},

the first identity follows. To prove the second, write

\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}\Big(\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq i-1}\mathsf{R}^{(\gamma_{j,i})}_{i,n+j}\Big)\mathsf{R}_{i,n+1}^{(\gamma_{1,i})}\Big).

By (
5.1
),
\mathsf{R}_{i,n+1}^{(c)}
commutes with the
\mathsf{R}_{k,n+j}^{(c^{\prime})}
,

i<k\leq n-1
,
2\leq j\leq k-1
,
c,c^{\prime}\in C
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{3\leq i\leq n}\,\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq i-1}\mathsf{R}^{(\gamma_{j,i})}_{i,n+j}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathsf{R}_{i,n+1}^{(\gamma_{1,i})}\Big)=\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n-1}\,\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq i-1}\mathsf{R}^{(\gamma_{j+1,i+1})}_{i+1,n+j+1}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathsf{R}_{i,n+1}^{(\gamma_{1,i})}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n-1}\,\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq i-1}\mathsf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{+}(i,j))}_{i+1,n+j+1}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathsf{R}_{i,n+1}^{(\gamma_{1,i})}\Big)=\phi_{[2,2n]\setminus\{n+1\}}(\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{+}})\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathsf{R}_{i,n+1}^{(\gamma_{1,i})}\Big).\qed

The following can be viewed as a quantum analogue of Proposition
4.3

and plays a crucial role in our proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
m\geq 2
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:I_{m}\to C
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m})\in C^{m}

and suppose that
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:I_{m+1}\to C
is transitive. Then in
\mathsf{QTr}^{+}_{2m-1}(C)

(5.6)

\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)=\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{m})}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\mathsf{R}_{1,m+1}^{(\alpha_{1})}\,\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}.

Proof.

We use induction on
m
.
For
m=2
,
\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}=\mathsf{R}_{2,3}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(1,2))}
and so (
5.6
)
reads

(5.7)

\mathsf{R}_{2,3}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(1,2))}\mathsf{R}_{1,3}^{(\alpha_{1})}\mathsf{R}_{1,2}^{(\alpha_{2})}=\mathsf{R}_{1,2}^{(\alpha_{2})}\mathsf{R}_{1,3}^{(\alpha_{1})}\mathsf{R}_{2,3}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(1,2))}.

Since
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is transitive,
\alpha_{1}\in\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}(1,2),\alpha_{2}\}
by Lemma
3.21
(b)
and so (
5.7
) follows from (
5.2
).

To prove the inductive step, we need the following

Lemma 5.3
.

For all
1\leq k\leq m
,

\displaystyle\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq m-1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{k\leq i\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{k\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{m})}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\times

\displaystyle\qquad\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq k-1}\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big).

Proof.

The argument is by induction on
k
, the case
k=1
being trivial.

For the inductive step, suppose that
1\leq k<m
.
Since by (
5.1
)
\mathsf{R}_{m,k+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(k,m))}

commutes with the
\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}
,
k+1\leq j\leq m-1
, we obtain by the induction hypothesis,

\displaystyle\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq m-1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{k+1\leq i\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{k+1\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\mathsf{R}_{m,m+k}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(k,m))}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+k}^{(\alpha_{k})}\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{m})}\times

\displaystyle\qquad\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq k-1}\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big).

Since
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is transitive,
\alpha_{k}\in\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}(k,m),\alpha_{m}\}
by Lemma
3.21
(b)
. Therefore, by (
5.2
)

\displaystyle\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq m-1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{k+1\leq i\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{k+1\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{m})}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+k}^{(\alpha_{k})}\mathsf{R}_{m,m+k}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(k,m))}\times

\displaystyle\qquad\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq k-1}\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{k+1\leq i\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{k+1\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{m})}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq k}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\times

\displaystyle\qquad\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq k}\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big),

which completes the proof of the inductive step.
∎

Applying the Lemma with
k=m
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq m-1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{m})}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big).

Therefore, by Lemma
5.1
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{J}

{}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)

\displaystyle=\phi_{[1,2m-1]\setminus\{m\}}(\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{m})}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{m})}\mathsf{R}_{1,2m-1}^{(\alpha_{m-1})}\phi_{[1,2m-1]\setminus\{m\}}(\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq m-2}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{m})}\mathsf{R}_{1,2m-1}^{(\alpha_{m-1})}\phi_{[1,2m-1]\setminus\{m\}}\Big(\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq m-2}\!\!\mathsf{R}_{1,m-1+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-1}\!\!\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq m-1}\!\!\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big).

By Lemma
3.21
(c)
,

(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{-}}:I_{m}\to C

is transitive.
Thus, the induction hypothesis applies to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}:I_{m-1}\to C
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{-}:[m-1]\to C
, and we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq m-2}\mathsf{R}_{1,m-1+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)=\mathsf{R}_{1,m-1}^{(\alpha_{m-1})}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-2}\mathsf{R}_{1,m-1+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{1})}\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathsf{J}

{}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{m})}\mathsf{R}_{1,2m-1}^{(\alpha_{m-1})}\phi_{[1,2m-1]\setminus\{m\}}\Big(\mathsf{R}_{1,m-1}^{(\alpha_{m-1})}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-2}\!\!\mathsf{R}_{1,m-1+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{1})}\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq m-1}\!\!\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{m})}\mathsf{R}_{1,2m-1}^{(\alpha_{m-1})}\mathsf{R}_{1,m-1}^{(\alpha_{m-1})}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-2}\!\!\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\mathsf{R}_{1,m+1}^{(\alpha_{1})}\phi_{[1,2m-1]\setminus\{m\}}(\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{-}})\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{m,i+m}^{(\boldsymbol{\gamma}(i,m))}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}_{1,m}^{(\alpha_{m})}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq m-1}\mathsf{R}_{1,m+j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\mathsf{R}_{1,j}^{(\alpha_{j})}\Big)\mathsf{R}_{1,m+1}^{(\alpha_{1})}\mathsf{J}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},

which completes the proof of the inductive step.
∎

5.2.
A family of Drinfeld twists

We will now prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

Note that
J_{\mathbf{c}}=J_{\mathbf{c}}(\mathbf{R})

defined in Theorem
1.9
equals

\Phi^{(2n)}_{\mathbf{R}}(\mathsf{J}_{\mathbf{c}})
.
Abbreviate
c_{j,i}=\mathbf{c}(j,i)
,
1\leq j<i\leq n
. We use the induction on
n
, the case
n=1
being trivial. For
n=2
, we have
J_{\mathbf{c}}=R_{23}^{(c_{12})}
, and the assertion follows from Proposition
2.29
.

Suppose that
n\geq 3
and let
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
be transitive. Then
\mathbf{c}^{-}

is transitive and so,
by the induction hypothesis,
J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}
is a Drinfeld twist for
H^{\otimes(n-1)}
with its standard comultiplication.
The key ingredient in our proof is

Proposition 5.4
.

Suppose that
n\geq 3
. Let
A=H^{\otimes(n-1)}
with the comultiplication

\Delta_{A}:A\to A\otimes A
defined by
\Delta_{A}(x)=J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}{}^{-1}\Delta_{H^{\otimes(n-1)}}(x)J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}
,
x\in H^{\otimes(n-1)}
, and let
B=H
with
\Delta_{B}=\Delta
. Then

F=\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}\limits_{1\leq j\leq n-1}R_{1,j+1}^{(c_{j,n})}\in B\otimes A

is a relative Drinfeld twist for
(A\otimes B,\Delta_{A\otimes B})
.

Proof.

Since
(\varepsilon_{B}\otimes\id_{A})(F)
and
(\id_{B}\otimes\varepsilon_{A})(F)
are manifestly central in respective algebras,
by Proposition
2.28
we only need to prove that

(5.8)

[(\Delta_{B}\otimes\id_{A})(F)]_{(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{3},\mathbf{4})}(F\otimes 1_{B}\otimes 1_{A})=[(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta_{A})(F)]_{(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{2},\mathbf{4})}(1_{B}\otimes 1_{A}\otimes F).

We have

\displaystyle[(\Delta_{B}\otimes\id_{A})(F)]_{(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{3},\mathbf{4})}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}[(\Delta\otimes\id_{H^{\otimes(n-1)}})(R_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})})]_{\{1\}\cup[n+1,2n]}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}(R_{1,n+j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}R_{n+1,n+j}^{(c_{j-1,n})})=\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{1,n+j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{n+1,n+j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)

(5.9)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{1,n+j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)(1_{B}\otimes 1_{A}\otimes F).

Since
F
is invertible, it is therefore sufficient to prove that

(5.10)

\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{1,n+j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)=[(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta_{A})(F)]_{(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{2},\mathbf{4})}.

Since both sides of this expression are contained in
B\otimes A\otimes 1_{B}\otimes A
, (
5.10
) is equivalent to

\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{1,n+j-1}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)=(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta_{A})(F)

in
B\otimes A\otimes A
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle(\id_{B}

\displaystyle\otimes\Delta_{A})(F)=(1_{B}\otimes J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}{}^{-1})\cdot(\id_{B}\otimes\Delta_{H^{\otimes(n-1)}})(F)\cdot(1_{B}\otimes J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})

\displaystyle=(1_{B}\otimes J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}{}^{-1})\cdot(\Delta_{2,n+1}\cdots\Delta_{n,2n-1})\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\cdot(1_{B}\otimes J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})

\displaystyle=(1_{B}\otimes J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}^{-1})\cdot\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{1,n+j-1}^{(c_{j-1,n})}R_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\cdot(1_{B}\otimes J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})

in
B\otimes A\otimes A
.
Thus, (
5.8
) is equivalent to

(1_{H}\otimes J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{1,n+j-1}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)=\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{1,n+j-1}^{(c_{j-1,n})}R_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)(1_{H}\otimes J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})

inside
H^{\otimes n}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
. Note that,
since
J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}\in 1_{H}\otimes(H^{\otimes n-2})^{\otimes 2}\otimes 1_{H}\subset(H^{\otimes(n-1)})^{\otimes 2}
, it follows that
1_{H}\otimes J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}

commutes with
R_{1,2}^{(c_{1n})}
and
with
R_{1,2n-1}^{(c_{n-1,n})}
. Therefore,
(
5.8
) is equivalent to

(1_{H}\otimes J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n-1}R_{1,n+j-1}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{3\leq j\leq n}R_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\\
=R_{1,n}^{(c_{n-1,n})}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{3\leq j\leq n-1}R_{1,n+j-1}^{(c_{j-1,n})}R_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)R_{1,n+1}^{(c_{1,n})}(1_{H}\otimes J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}).

Finally, observe that both sides of the above
equation are contained in
H\otimes 1_{H}\otimes H^{\otimes 2n-3}\otimes 1_{H}
. Therefore, (
5.8
) is equivalent to

(5.11)

\displaystyle J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq n-2}\!\!R_{1,n+j-1}^{(c_{j,n})}\Big)

\displaystyle\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n-1}\!\!R_{1,j}^{(c_{j,n})}\Big)=R_{1,n-1}^{(c_{n-1,n})}\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n-2}\!\!R_{1,n+j-1}^{(c_{j,n})}R_{1,j}^{(c_{j,n})}\Big)R_{1,n}^{(c_{1,n})}J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}

in
(H^{\otimes(n-1)})^{\otimes 2}
. By Lemma
3.21
(a)
,
(\mathbf{c}^{-})^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathbf{c}}}=\mathbf{c}
is transitive. Then (
5.11
) follows
from Proposition
5.2
with
m=n-1
,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\mathbf{c}

and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathbf{c}}

by applying
the homomorphism
\Phi^{(2n)}_{\mathbf{R}}
.
∎

By (
5.4
),
J_{\mathbf{c}}=(J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})_{[1,2n-1]\setminus\{n\}}(1_{A}\otimes F\otimes 1_{B})=(J_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{3}}F_{\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3}}

where
\mathbf{1}=[n-1]
,
\mathbf{2}=\{n\}
,
\mathbf{3}=\{n+1,2n-1\}
. Then
by
Proposition
5.4
and Corollary
2.32
,

J_{\mathbf{c}}

is a Drinfeld twist for
H^{\otimes n}
with its standard comultiplication. This proves the first
assertion of Theorem
1.9
. Since

\prod_{i\in[n]}R_{i,i+n}^{(d_{i})}
is an R-matrix
for
\Delta_{H^{\otimes n}}
for any
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\in C
,
the second
assertion follows from the first and Proposition
2.26
(b)
.
∎

Corollary 5.5
.

For any transitive
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
,
\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}\limits_{2\leq i\leq n}R_{i-1,n}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,i))}
is a relative Drinfeld twist
for
A\otimes B
where
A=H
and
B=H^{\otimes(n-1)}
with
\Delta_{B}
obtained by twisting
\Delta_{H^{\otimes(n-1)}}
by
J_{\mathbf{c}^{+}}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Lemmata
5.1
and
2.31
and Theorem
1.9
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Given a permutation
w\in S_{n}
and an R-matrix
R
for
H
,
J_{w}(R)
defined in Theorem
1.7

coincides with
J_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)}(\mathbf{R})
where

\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w):I_{n}\to\{1,-1\}
is defined as in Lemma
3.1
and
\mathbf{R}=\{R^{(1)},R^{(-1)}\}=\{R,R_{2,1}^{-1}\}
. It remains to apply Theorem
1.9
with
\mathbf{c}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)
.
∎

We now prove a special case of Conjecture
1.8
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
H
be a quasi-triangular bialgebra with an R-matrix
R
,
and let
\mathbf{R}=\{R^{(1)},R^{(-1)}\}=\{R,R_{2,1}^{-1}\}
.
Suppose that
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{i,j})_{i,j\in[n]}
,

a_{i,j}\in\{1,-1\}
,
i,j\in[n]
is
transitive and satisfies
a_{j,i}=-a_{i,j}
,
(i,j)\in I_{n}
. Then
\mathbf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}
solves QYBE.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.2
,
there exists
w\in S_{n}
such that
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w,\boldsymbol{d})
where

\boldsymbol{d}=(a_{i,i})_{i\in[n]}
.
We claim that

\mathbf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}=\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq n}\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq n}R_{i,n+j}^{(a_{j,i})}=\mathbf{R}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w),\boldsymbol{d}).

In particular,
\mathbf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}

is an R-matrix by Theorem
1.7
(b)
and
hence satisfies QYBE.

To prove the claim, we use induction on
n
, the case
n=1
being trivial. For the inductive step, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}R_{1,n+j}^{(a_{j,1})}\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}R_{i,n+j}^{(a_{j,i})}\Big)\Big)R_{1,n+1}^{(a_{1,1})}\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}R_{i,n+1}^{(a_{1,i})}

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}R_{1,n+i}^{(-a_{1,i})}\Big)(\mathbf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{a}^{+})})_{[2,2n]\setminus\{n+1\}}R_{1,n+1}^{(a_{1,1})}\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}R_{i,n+1}^{(a_{1,i})},

where, as before,
\boldsymbol{a}^{+}:[n-1]\times[n-1]\to\{1,-1\}
is defined by
\boldsymbol{a}^{+}(i,j)=\boldsymbol{a}(i+1,j+1)
. By the induction hypothesis,

\mathbf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{a}^{+})}=\mathbf{R}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)^{+},\boldsymbol{d}^{+})=(J_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)^{+}}^{op})^{-1}\prod_{i\in[n-1]}R_{i,n-1+i}^{(a_{i+1,i+1})}J_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)}.

Since
R^{(-\epsilon)}=R_{2,1}^{(\epsilon)}{}^{-1}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}

\displaystyle=\Big(\Big((J_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)^{+}})_{[2,2n]\setminus\{n+1\}}\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}R_{i,n+1}^{(a_{1,i})}\Big)^{op}\Big)^{-1}\prod_{i\in[n]}R_{i,i+n}^{(a_{i,i)}}\Big((J_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)^{+}})_{[2,2n]\setminus\{n+1\}}\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq i\leq n}R_{i,n+1}^{(a_{1,i})}\Big)

\displaystyle=(J_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)}^{op})^{-1}\Big(\prod_{i\in[n]}R_{i,i+n}^{(a_{i,i)}}\Big)J_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)}=\mathbf{R}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w),\boldsymbol{d}),

where we used (
5.5
).
∎

5.3.
Diagonal homomorphism of bialgebras

We now establish a quantum analogue of Theorem
4.10
.

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
H
be a quasi-triangular bialgebra with a family of R-matrices

\mathbf{R}=\{R^{(c)}\}_{c\in C}
and let
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
be transitive.
Then the iterated comultiplication
\Delta^{(n)}:=\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}\limits_{0\leq t\leq n-2}(\Delta\otimes\id_{H}^{\otimes t})=\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}\limits_{0\leq t\leq n-2}(\id_{H}^{\otimes t}\otimes\Delta)
is
a homomorphism of bialgebras
H\to H^{\otimes n}
where
the standard comultiplication
\Delta_{H^{\otimes n}}
is twisted by
J_{\mathbf{c}}(\mathbf{R})
.

Proof.

The argument is by induction on
n
, the case
n=1
being
trivial. For the inductive step, let

A=H
and let
B=H^{\otimes(n-1)}
with the
comultiplication twisted by
J_{\mathbf{c}^{+}}(\mathbf{R})
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\Delta^{(n-1)}:H\to B

is a homomorphism of bialgebras, and

F=\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}\limits_{2\leq i\leq n}R_{i-1,n}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,i))}
is a relative Drinfeld twist for
A\otimes B

by Corollary
5.5
.
Since
\Delta^{(n)}=(\id_{H}\otimes\Delta^{(n-1)})\circ\Delta
,
by Proposition
2.33

with
U=H
,
\psi_{A}=\id_{H}
and
\psi_{B}=\Delta^{(n-1)}

it suffices to prove that

F\cdot\Delta^{(n)}(h)=(\tau_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{2}}\circ\Delta^{(n)})(h)\cdot F,\qquad h\in H

where we abbreviate
\mathbf{1}=\{1\}
,
\mathbf{2}=[2,n]
, or in Sweedler notation

(5.12)

\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}\limits_{1\leq i\leq n-1}R_{i,n}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,i+1))}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\otimes}}_{1\leq t\leq n}h_{(t)}\Big)=\Big(\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\otimes}}_{2\leq t\leq n}h_{(t)}\Big)\otimes h_{(1)}\Big)\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}\limits_{1\leq i\leq n-1}R_{i,n}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,i+1))}\Big).

We need the following

Lemma 5.8
.

For all
h\in H
,
c_{1},\dots,c_{n-1}\in C

and
1\leq k\leq n

\displaystyle\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}\limits_{1\leq i\leq n-1}R_{i,n}^{(c_{i})}\Big)\cdot\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\otimes}}_{1\leq t\leq n}h_{(t)}\Big)=\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq i\leq k-1}R_{i,n}^{(c_{i})}\Big)\cdot\Big(\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\otimes}}_{t\in[n]\setminus\{k\}}\!\!h_{(t)}\Big)\otimes h_{(k)}\Big)\cdot\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{k\leq i\leq n-1}\!\!R_{i,n}^{(c_{i})}\Big).

Proof.

The argument is by descending induction on
k
, the case
k=n
being trivial. For the inductive step,
it suffices to observe that, since
R^{(c)}\cdot\Delta(h^{\prime})=\tau\circ\Delta(h^{\prime})\cdot R^{(c)}
for all
h^{\prime}\in H
,
c\in C
,

\displaystyle R_{k,n}^{(c_{k})}\cdot\Big(\Big(

\displaystyle\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\otimes}}_{t\in[n]\setminus\{k+1\}}\!\!h_{(t)}\Big)\otimes h_{(k+1)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\otimes}}_{t\in[k-1]}h_{(t)}\Big)\otimes 1\otimes\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\otimes}}_{t\in[k+2,n]}h_{(t)}\Big)\otimes 1\Big)\cdot(R^{(c_{k})}\cdot(h_{(k)}\otimes h_{(k+1)}))_{k,n}

\displaystyle=\Big(\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\otimes}}_{t\in[k-1]}h_{(t)}\Big)\otimes 1\otimes\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\otimes}}_{t\in[k+2,n]}h_{(t)}\Big)\otimes 1\Big)\cdot((h_{(k+1)}\otimes h_{(k)})\cdot R^{(c_{k})})_{k,n}

\displaystyle=\Big(\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\otimes}}_{t\in[n]\setminus\{k\}}h_{(t)}\Big)\otimes h_{(k)}\Big)\cdot R_{k,n}^{(c_{k})}.\qed

Applying the Lemma with
k=1
and
c_{i}=\mathbf{c}(1,i+1)
,
i\in[n-1]

yields (
5.12
) and completes the
proof of the inductive step and hence of Theorem
5.7
.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

It should be noted that, while Lemma
5.8
does not require the transitivity of
\mathbf{c}
, we need it to ensure that
J_{\mathbf{c}}
is a Drinfeld twist and,
therefore,
\Delta_{H^{\otimes n}}
twisted by
J_{\mathbf{c}}
remains coassociative.

5.4.
The dual picture

Retain the notation from §
2.12
.
Let
H
be a bialgebra and let
\underline{\mathcal{R}}=\{\mathcal{R}^{(c)}\}\subset\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(H\otimes H,\mathbb{k})
be a family of co-quasi-triangular structures on
H
. As in §
5.1
, by Proposition
2.34

the assignments
\mathsf{R}_{i,j}^{(c)}\mapsto\mathcal{R}_{i,j}^{(c)}
,
i,j\in[n]
,
c\in C
, define a homomorphism of monoids

\Phi^{(n)}_{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}:\mathsf{QTr}^{+}_{n}(C)\to\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(H^{\otimes n},\mathbb{k})
.
Let

(5.13)

\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}}=\Phi^{(2n)}_{\underline{\mathcal{R}}}(\mathsf{J}_{\mathbf{c}})=\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{1\leq j\leq i-1}\mathcal{R}_{i,j+n}^{(\mathbf{c}(j,i))}.

Theorem 5.10
.

If
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
is transitive then
{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbf{c}}
is a dual Drinfeld twist
for
H^{\otimes n}\otimes H^{\otimes n}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Theorem
1.9
and uses induction on
n
, the induction base being Lemma
2.42
. To prove the
inductive step, note that
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}

is a dual Drinfeld twist for
H^{\otimes(n-1)}

by the induction hypothesis.
We prove that

\mathcal{F}=\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}\limits_{1\leq i\leq n-1}\mathcal{R}_{1,i+1}^{(c_{i,n})}

is a relative dual Drinfeld twist for
A\otimes B
where
B=H
and
A=H^{\otimes(n-1)}

with the multiplication
\bullet_{\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}}
and, as before,
c_{i,j}=\mathbf{c}(i,j)
,
(i,j)\in I_{n}
.
Indeed, note that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{13},\mathbf{4}}=(\mathcal{F}\circ m_{B}\otimes\id_{A})_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{3},\mathbf{4}}
as an element of
\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}((B\otimes A)^{\otimes 2},\mathbb{k})
which we identify with
\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(H^{\otimes 2n},\mathbb{k})
; we abbreviate
\mathbf{1}=\{1\}
,

\mathbf{2}=[2,n]
,
\mathbf{3}=\{n\}
,
\mathbf{4}=[n+1,2n]
. Then, similarly to (
5.9
)

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{13},\mathbf{4}}=\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{2\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}_{1,n+j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\ast\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{3},\mathbf{4}}.

Since
\mathcal{F}
is invertible, it is therefore sufficient to prove that

\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{2\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}_{1,n+j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\ast\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{2\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)=\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{24}}

(compare with (
5.10
)) or, equivalently that

(5.14)

\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{2\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}_{1,n+j-1}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\ast\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{2\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)=\mathcal{F}\circ(\id_{B}\otimes m_{A})

in
\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(B\otimes A\otimes A,\mathbb{k})
.
Now, since

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\circ(\id_{B}\otimes m_{A})(b\otimes a\otimes a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(b,a\bullet_{\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}}a^{\prime})=\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}{}^{-1}(a_{(1)},a^{\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{F}(b,a_{(2)}a^{\prime}_{(2)})\mathcal{J}_{c^{-}}(a_{(3)},a^{\prime}_{(3)})

\displaystyle=(\varepsilon_{B}\otimes\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}{}^{-1})\ast\mathcal{F}\circ(\id_{B}\otimes m_{H^{\otimes n}})\ast(\varepsilon_{B}\otimes\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})(b\otimes a\otimes a^{\prime}),

for all
a,a^{\prime}\in A
,
b\in B
,
the identity (
5.14
) is equivalent to

(\varepsilon_{H}\otimes\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}^{-}})\ast\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{2\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}_{1,n+j-1}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\ast\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{2\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)=\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{2\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}_{1,n+j-1}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\ast\mathcal{R}_{1,j}^{(c_{j-1,n})}\Big)\ast(\varepsilon_{H}\otimes\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}^{-}}),

which reduces to the same identity (
5.11
)
with products replaced by convolution products and elements of
H^{\widehat{\otimes}k}
replaced by their dual counterparts. Thus, (
5.14
) follows from Proposition
5.2
.
To complete the proof of the inductive step,
it remains to apply Lemma
5.1

together with Corollary
2.43
.
∎

Similarly to Corollary
5.5
,
we have

Corollary 5.11
.

For any transitive
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
,
\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}\limits_{2\leq i\leq n}\mathcal{R}_{i-1,n}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,i))}
is a relative dual Drinfeld twist
for
A\otimes B
where
A=H
and
B=H^{\otimes(n-1)}
with the multiplication twisted by
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}^{+}}
.

Abbreviate
\Psi^{(c)}:=\Psi_{\mathcal{R}^{(c)}}\in\End_{\mathbb{k}}(H\otimes H)

in the notation of Corollary
2.43
, that is

(5.15)

\Psi^{(c)}(h\otimes h^{\prime})=(\mathcal{R}^{(c)})^{\ast-1}(h_{(1)},h^{\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{R}^{(c)}(h_{(3)},h^{\prime}_{(3)})h^{\prime}_{(2)}\otimes h_{(2)},\qquad h,h^{\prime}\in H.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>1}
, let
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
be
transitive and abbreviate
\bullet_{\mathbf{c}}:=\bullet_{\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}}}
. Then
for all
\mathbf{h},\mathbf{h}^{\prime}\in H^{\otimes n}

(5.16)

\mathbf{h}\bullet_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{h}^{\prime}=m_{H}{}^{\otimes n}\circ\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}\limits_{1\leq i\leq n-1}\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}\limits_{i+1\leq j\leq n}\id_{H}^{\otimes(i+j-2)}\otimes\Psi^{(\mathbf{c}(i,j))}\otimes\id_{H}^{\otimes(2n-i-j)}\Big)(\mathbf{h}\otimes\mathbf{h}^{\prime}).

Explicitly, if
\mathbf{h}=a^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n}
,

\mathbf{h}^{\prime}=b^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b^{n}
,
a^{i},b^{i}\in H
,

i\in[n]
then

(5.17)

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}\bullet_{\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{h}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq j<i\leq n}(\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(j,i))})^{\ast-1}(a^{i}_{(j)},b^{j}_{(n+1-i)})\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(j,i))}(a^{i}_{(2i-j)},b^{j}_{(n+i+1-2j)})\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\otimes}}_{1\leq i\leq n}a^{i}_{(i)}b^{i}_{(n+1-i)}.

In particular, if
all the
\mathcal{R}^{(c)}
,
c\in C
are
counital in the sense of (
2.27
)
then

\displaystyle(1^{\otimes(l-1)}\otimes h\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-l)})\bullet_{\mathbf{c}}(1^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes h^{\prime}\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-k)})=\begin{cases}(hh^{\prime})_{k},&k=l,\\
(h\otimes h^{\prime})_{l,k},&k>l,\\
(\Psi^{(\mathbf{c}(k,l))}(h\otimes h^{\prime}))_{k,l},&k<l,\end{cases}

for all
h,h^{\prime}\in H
,
k,l\in[n]
.

Proof.

We prove (
5.16
) by induction on
n
. The case
n=2
is
immediate from Lemma
2.42
and Corollary
2.43
with
A=B=H
,
\mathcal{J}_{A}=\mathcal{J}_{B}=\varepsilon\otimes\varepsilon
.
For the inductive step, let

a,a^{\prime}\in A=H
,
\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\in B=H^{\otimes(n-1)}
, the multiplication in
B
being
\bullet_{\mathbf{c}^{+}}
.
Using Corollaries
2.43
and
5.11
and the
induction hypothesis, we obtain

\displaystyle(a^{\prime}

\displaystyle\otimes\mathbf{b})\bullet_{\mathbf{c}}(a\otimes\mathbf{b}^{\prime})=(m_{H}\otimes\bullet_{\mathbf{c}^{+}})(\id_{A}\otimes\Psi_{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\id_{B})(a^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{b}\otimes a\otimes\mathbf{b}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=m_{H}{}^{\otimes n}\circ\Big(\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}}\limits_{2\leq i\leq n-1}\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}\limits_{i+1\leq j\leq n}\id_{H}^{\otimes(i+j-2)}\otimes\Psi^{(\mathbf{c}(i,j))}\otimes\id_{H}^{\otimes(2n-i-j)}\Big)(a^{\prime}\otimes\Psi_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathbf{b}\otimes a)\otimes\mathbf{b}^{\prime}),

where
\mathcal{F}=\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\mathcal{R}_{j,n}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}
. Furthermore,
if
\mathbf{b}=h^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes h^{n-1}
,

h^{j}\in H
,
j\in[n-1]
then

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mathcal{F}}(

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}\otimes a)=\mathcal{F}^{\ast-1}(\mathbf{b}_{(1)},a_{(1)})\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{b}_{(3)},a_{(3)})a_{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{b}_{(2)}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\overleftarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{1\leq j\leq n-1}(\mathcal{R}_{j,n}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))})^{\ast-1}(\mathbf{b}_{(1)},a_{(1)})\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}^{\ast}}_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\mathcal{R}_{j,n}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}(\mathbf{b}_{(3)},a_{(3)})a_{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{b}_{(2)}

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq j\leq n-1}(\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))})^{\ast-1}(h^{j}_{(1)},a_{(n-j)})\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}(h^{j}_{(3)},a_{(n+j)})a_{(n)}\otimes h^{1}_{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes h^{n-1}_{(2)}

(5.18)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{2\leq j\leq n}(\id_{H}^{\otimes(j-2)}\otimes\Psi^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j))}\otimes\id_{H}^{\otimes(n-j)})\Big)(\mathbf{b}\otimes a).

The inductive step and hence (
5.16
) are now immediate. To prove the second assertion,
note that for
\mathbf{h}=a^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n}
,

\mathbf{h}^{\prime}=b^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b^{n}
,
a^{i},b^{i}\in H
,

i\in[n]
it follows from (
5.13
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}}(\mathbf{h},\mathbf{h}^{\prime})=\varepsilon(a^{1})\varepsilon(b^{n})\prod_{1\leq j<i\leq n}\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(j,i))}(a^{i}_{(i-j)},b^{j}_{(i-j)}),

(5.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}}^{\ast-1}(\mathbf{h},\mathbf{h}^{\prime})=\varepsilon(a^{1})\varepsilon(b^{n})\prod_{1\leq j<i\leq n}(\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(j,i))})^{\ast-1}(a^{i}_{(j)},b^{j}_{(n+1-i)}),

which immediately yields (
5.17
).
Finally, if all the
\mathcal{R}^{(c)}
,
c\in C

are counital then
for
\mathbf{h}=1^{\otimes(l-1)}\otimes h\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-l)}
,
\mathbf{h}^{\prime}=1^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes h^{\prime}\otimes 1^{\otimes(n-k)}
,
h,h^{\prime}\in H
,
k,l\in[n]
, (
5.19
) then yields

\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{c}}^{\ast\pm 1}(\mathbf{h},\mathbf{h}^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\varepsilon(h)\varepsilon(h^{\prime}),&k\geq l,\\
(\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(k,l))})^{\ast\pm 1}(h,h^{\prime}),&k<l.\end{cases}

The last assertion is now immediate.
∎

Thus, if all the
\mathcal{R}^{(c)}
,
c\in C

are counital,
(H^{\otimes n},\bullet_{\mathbf{c}})

is generated by the
h^{(k)}
,
h\in H
,
k\in[n]

subject to relations

\displaystyle a^{(k)}\bullet_{\mathbf{c}}b^{(k)}=(ab)^{(k)},\qquad k\in[n],

\displaystyle a^{(l)}\bullet_{\mathbf{c}}b^{(k)}=(\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(k,l))})^{\ast-1}(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(k,l))}(a_{(3)},b_{(3)})b_{(2)}{}^{(k)}\bullet_{\mathbf{c}}a_{(2)}{}^{(l)},\,\,k<l\in[n],\,a,b\in H.

Proposition 5.13
.

Suppose that all the
\mathcal{R}^{(c)}
,
c\in C
are counital.
Let
\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}:I_{n}\to C
be transitive
and let
\sigma\in S_{n}
. Then the assignments

h^{(i)}\mapsto h^{(\sigma(i))}
,
i\in[n]
,
h\in H

define
an isomorphism of bialgebras
(H^{\otimes n},\bullet_{\mathbf{c}})\to(H^{\otimes n},\bullet_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime}})

if and only if, for all
h,h^{\prime}\in H
,
k\not=l\in[n]

\mathcal{Z}_{k,l;\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\sigma}(h_{(1)}\otimes h^{\prime}_{(1)})h_{(2)}\otimes h^{\prime}_{(2)}=\mathcal{Z}_{k,l;\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\sigma}(h_{(2)},h^{\prime}_{(2)})h_{(1)}\otimes h^{\prime}_{(1)}

where
\mathcal{Z}_{k,l;\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\sigma}=(\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(k,l))})^{\Upsilon(l-k)}\ast(\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}^{\prime}(\sigma(k),\sigma(l)))})^{\ast-\Upsilon(\sigma(l)-\sigma(k))}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(H\otimes H,\mathbb{k})
.

Proof.

Let
k\not=l\in[n]
,
h,h^{\prime}\in H
and let
k^{\prime}=\sigma(k)
,
l^{\prime}=\sigma(l)
.
We have

\sigma(h^{(l)}\bullet_{\mathbf{c}}h^{\prime}{}^{(k)})=\begin{cases}(\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(k,l))})^{\ast-1}(h_{(1)},h^{\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(k,l))}(h_{(3)},h^{\prime}_{(3)})(h_{(2)}\otimes h^{\prime}_{(2)})_{l^{\prime},k^{\prime}},&k<l,\\
(h\otimes h^{\prime})_{l^{\prime},k^{\prime}},&k>l.\end{cases}

On the other hand,

h^{(l^{\prime})}\bullet_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}h^{\prime}{}^{(k^{\prime})}=\begin{cases}(\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}^{\prime}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime}))})^{\ast-1}(h_{(1)},h^{\prime}_{(1)})\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}^{\prime}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime}))}(h_{(3)},h^{\prime}_{(3)})(h_{(2)}\otimes h^{\prime}_{(2)})_{l^{\prime},k^{\prime}},&k^{\prime}<l^{\prime},\\
(h\otimes h^{\prime})_{l^{\prime},k^{\prime}},&k^{\prime}>l^{\prime}.\end{cases}

The assertion follows by applying Lemma
2.7
.
∎

In particular, for
n=2
, given any co-quasi-triangular structure
\mathcal{R}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(H^{\otimes 2},\mathbb{k})
,
we obtain two possible multiplications

\bullet_{\pm}:H^{\otimes 2}\otimes H^{\otimes 2}\to H^{\otimes 2}
on
H^{\otimes 2}
,
corresponding to
\mathcal{R}^{(\pm 1)}

where
\mathcal{R}^{(1)}=\mathcal{R}

and
\mathcal{R}^{(-1)}=\mathcal{R}^{\ast-1}\circ\tau

(cf. Lemma
2.35
).
Then
\tau:H\otimes H\to H\otimes H

is an isomorphism of algebras
(H^{\otimes 2},\bullet_{+})\to(H^{\otimes 2},\bullet_{-})
if and only if

(5.20)

\mathcal{R}(h_{(1)},h^{\prime}_{(1)})h_{(2)}\otimes h^{\prime}_{(2)}=\mathcal{R}(h_{(2)},h^{\prime}_{(2)})h_{(1)}\otimes h^{\prime}_{(1)},\qquad h,h^{\prime}\in H,

which holds automatically if
H
is cocommutative.

We complete this chapter with the counterpart of Theorem
5.7
.

Theorem 5.14
.

For any transitive
\mathbf{c}:I_{n}\to C
, the iterated multiplication map
m_{H}^{(n)}:H^{\otimes n}\to H

is a homomorphism of bialgebras
(H^{\otimes n},\bullet_{\mathbf{c}})\to H
.

Proof.

Since
m_{H}^{(n)}
is a homomorphism of coalgebras,
we only need to prove that it is a homomorphism of algebras.
The argument by induction on
n
, the case
n=1
being trivial. For the inductive step, let
A=H

and let
B=(H^{\otimes(n-1)},\bullet_{\mathbf{c}^{+}})
.
Since

m^{(n-1)}_{H}:(H^{\otimes(n-1)},\bullet_{\mathbf{c}^{+}})\to H

is a homomorphism of algebras by the induction hypothesis
and
m^{(n)}_{H}=m_{H}\circ(\id_{H}\otimes m_{H}^{(n-1)})
,
by Corollary
5.11
and Proposition
2.44
used with
C=H
,
\varphi_{A}=\id_{H}
,
\varphi_{B}=m_{H}^{(n-1)}

and
\mathcal{F}
as in the proof of Proposition
5.12
,
to complete the inductive step it remains to show
that, for all
a\in H
,
\mathbf{b}\in H^{\otimes(n-1)}
,

(5.21)

\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{b}_{(2)},a_{(2)})a_{(1)}m_{H}^{(n-1)}(\mathbf{b}_{(1)})=\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{b}_{(1)},a_{(1)})m_{H}^{(n-1)}(\mathbf{b}_{(2)})a_{(2)}.

Clearly, it suffices to prove (
5.21
)
for
\mathbf{b}=h^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes h^{n-1}
,

h^{j}\in H
,
1\leq j\leq n-1
.
We need the following

Lemma 5.15
.

For all
a,h^{1},\dots,h^{n-1}\in H
, and
1\leq k\leq n

\mathcal{F}(h^{1}_{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes h^{n-1}_{(2)},a_{(2)})a_{(1)}\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq n-1}h^{j}_{(1)}\Big)\\
=\prod_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\!\!\!\!\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}(h^{j}_{(1)},a_{(j)})\prod_{k\leq j\leq n-1}\!\!\!\!\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}(h^{j}_{(2)},a_{(j+1)})\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\!\!\!\!h^{j}_{(2)}\Big)a_{(k)}\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{k\leq j\leq n-1}\!\!\!\!h^{j}_{(1)}\Big)

Proof.

The argument is by induction on
k
, the case
k=1
being immediate from the definition of
\mathcal{F}
. To prove
the inductive step, it suffices to observe that,
by (
2.25
),

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\!\!\!\!\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}(h^{j}_{(1)},a_{(j)})\prod_{k\leq j\leq n-1}\!\!\!\!\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}(h^{j}_{(2)},a_{(j+1)})\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\!\!\!\!h^{j}_{(2)}\Big)a_{(k)}\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{k\leq j\leq n-1}\!\!\!\!h^{j}_{(1)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\!\!\!\!\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}(h^{j}_{(1)},a_{(j)})\prod_{k+1\leq j\leq n-1}\!\!\!\!\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}(h^{j}_{(2)},a_{(j+1)})\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\!\!\!\!h^{j}_{(2)}\Big)\times

\displaystyle\mskip 200.0mu\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,k+1))}(h^{k}_{(2)},a_{(k+1)})a_{(k)}h^{k}_{(1)}\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{k+1\leq j\leq n-1}\!\!\!\!h^{j}_{(1)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\!\!\!\!\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}(h^{j}_{(1)},a_{(j)})\prod_{k+1\leq j\leq n-1}\!\!\!\!\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}(h^{j}_{(2)},a_{(j+1)})\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\!\!\!\!h^{j}_{(2)}\Big)\times

\displaystyle\mskip 200.0mu\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,k+1))}(h^{k}_{(1)},a_{(k)})h^{k}_{(2)}a_{(k+1)}\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{k+1\leq j\leq n-1}\!\!\!\!h^{j}_{(1)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq j\leq k}\!\!\!\!\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}(h^{j}_{(1)},a_{(j)})\prod_{k+1\leq j\leq n-1}\!\!\!\!\mathcal{R}^{(\mathbf{c}(1,j+1))}(h^{j}_{(2)},a_{(j+1)})\times

\displaystyle\mskip 200.0mu\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{1\leq j\leq k-1}\!\!\!\!h^{j}_{(2)}\Big)a_{(k+1)}\Big(\mathop{\overrightarrow{\prod}}_{k+1\leq j\leq n-1}\!\!\!\!h^{j}_{(1)}\Big).\qed

It remains to observe that, for
k=n
, the
right hand side of the identity in the Lemma is
precisely the right hand side of (
5.21
) with
\mathbf{b}=h^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes h^{n-1}
,

h^{i}\in H
,
1\leq i\leq n-1
.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

If all the
\mathcal{R}^{(c)}
are counital, the argument simplifies dramatically since in that case it suffices to prove the assertion for generators
h^{(k)}
,
h\in H
,
k\in[n]
(cf. §
4.5
).

6.
Examples

6.1.
Twisted tensor powers of quantum matrices

We begin with the classical picture.
Retain the notation of §
4.5

and abbreviate
\mathcal{A}_{m}=\mathbb{k}[\Mat_{m}]
. Let
E_{a,b}
,
a,b\in[m]
be
the standard basis of
\Mat_{m}
or of
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{gl}}_{m}

and define
x_{i,j}\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
,
i,j\in[m]
, by
x_{i,j}(E_{a,b})=\delta_{i,a}\delta_{j,b}
,
a,b\in[m]
. Then
\mathcal{A}_{m}
identifies with
\mathbb{k}[x_{i,j}\,:\,i,j\in[m]]

and is naturally a sub-bialgebra of
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\smallsquare}\subset U({\mathfrak{g}})^{*}
in the notation
of §
2.2
.
The algebra
\mathbb{k}[GL_{m}]
is the localization of
\mathcal{A}_{m}
by the determinant
\det
.
Then standard
right and left actions of
{\mathfrak{g}}
on
\mathcal{A}_{m}

are given by, respectively, by

x_{i,j}\triangleleft E_{a,b}=\delta_{a,i}x_{b,j},\quad E_{a,b}\triangleright x_{i,j}=\delta_{b,j}x_{i,a},\qquad a,b,i,j\in[m].

Let

r=\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}E_{i,i}\otimes E_{i,i}+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq m}E_{i,j}\otimes E_{j,i}

be the standard r-matrix for
{\mathfrak{g}}
(see e.g.
[
11
]
*§2.2).

Lemma 6.1
.

For all
i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}\in[m]
,
\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}

\displaystyle r^{(\epsilon)}\bowtie x_{i,j}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}=(\sign(i^{\prime}-i)+\epsilon)x_{i^{\prime},j}\otimes x_{i,j^{\prime}}-(\sign(j-j^{\prime})+\epsilon)x_{i,j^{\prime}}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in[m]}(E_{aa}\otimes E_{aa})

\displaystyle\bowtie(x_{i,j}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})=\sum_{a\in[m]}(\delta_{a,i}\delta_{a,i^{\prime}}x_{a,j}\otimes x_{a,j^{\prime}}-\delta_{a,j}\delta_{a,j^{\prime}}x_{i,a}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},a})

\displaystyle=\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}x_{i,j}\otimes x_{i,j^{\prime}}-\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}x_{i,j}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j}=\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}x_{i^{\prime},j}\otimes x_{i,j^{\prime}}-\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}x_{i,j^{\prime}}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j},

while

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq m}(E_{ab}\otimes E_{ba})\bowtie(x_{i,j}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq m}(\delta_{a,i}\delta_{b,i^{\prime}}x_{b,j}\otimes x_{a,j^{\prime}}-\delta_{b,j}\delta_{a,j^{\prime}}x_{i,a}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},b})

\displaystyle=\Upsilon(i^{\prime}-i)x_{i^{\prime},j}\otimes x_{i,j^{\prime}}-\Upsilon(j-j^{\prime})x_{i,j^{\prime}}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j}.

Therefore,

r^{(\epsilon)}\bowtie(x_{i,j}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})=\epsilon(\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}+2\Upsilon(\epsilon(i^{\prime}-i))x_{i^{\prime},j}\otimes x_{i,j^{\prime}}-(\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}+2\Upsilon(\epsilon(j-j^{\prime})))x_{i,j^{\prime}}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j}).

By (
2.1
),
\delta_{k,l}+2\Upsilon(\epsilon(l-k))=1+\sign(\epsilon(l-k))=1+\epsilon\sign(l-k)

for all
k,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}
, and
the assertion follows.
∎

Using (
4.9
) and the Lemma, we immediately obtain the standard Poisson bracket
on
\mathcal{A}_{m}

(6.1)

\{x_{i,j},x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\}=(\sign(i^{\prime}-i)+\sign(j^{\prime}-j))x_{i,j^{\prime}}x_{i^{\prime},j}

and a Poisson bracket on
\mathcal{A}_{m}^{\otimes n}

\displaystyle\{x_{i,j}^{(k)},x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(\id)}

\displaystyle=(\sign(i^{\prime}-i)+\sign(k-k^{\prime}))x_{i^{\prime},j}^{(k)}x_{i,j^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}

(6.2)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+(\sign(j^{\prime}-j)-\sign(k-k^{\prime}))x_{i,j^{\prime}}^{(k)}x_{i^{\prime},j}^{(k^{\prime})}

for all
i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}\in[m]
,
k,k^{\prime}\in[n]
.
Evidently, the assignments
x_{i,j}^{(k)}\mapsto x_{i,j}
,
i,j\in[m]
,
k\in[n]
define a homomorphism
of Poisson algebras, as stipulated in Theorem
1.6
.

Remark 6.2
.

We can define Poisson brackets using the classical
Drinfeld twist corresponding to
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)
for any permutation
w\in S_{n}
. However, the natural action of
S_{n}

on
\mathcal{A}_{m}^{\otimes n}
by permutations of factors
yields an isomorphism between that Poisson algebra
and the one we just described, since the corresponding
Lie bialgebras are isomorphic (see Remark
4.8
).

Remark 6.3
.

It is easy to check, using (
2.13
), that

E_{a,b}\triangleright\det=\det\triangleleft E_{a,b}=\delta_{a,b}\det
,
a,b\in[m]
whence
r^{(\epsilon)}\bowtie\det\otimes x_{i,j}=0
,
\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}
,
i,j\in[m]
and so
\det
is Poisson-central
in
\mathcal{A}_{m}
and
\det^{(k)}
,
k\in[n]
are Poisson-central
in
\mathcal{A}_{m}^{\otimes n}
. Thus, our Poisson bracket remains the same on
\mathbb{k}[G]^{\otimes n}
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{k}^{\times}
and assume that
q
is not a root of unity. The quantum analogue
\mathcal{A}_{q,m}
of
\mathcal{A}_{m}
is
generated by the
x_{i,j}
,
i,j\in[m]
subject to relations

(6.3)

q^{\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}}x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}x_{i,j}-q^{\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}}x_{i,j}x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}=(q-q^{-1})(\Upsilon(j-j^{\prime})-\Upsilon(i^{\prime}-i))x_{i,j^{\prime}}x_{i^{\prime},j},

for all
i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}\in[m]
.
For instance, if
m=2
we obtain the familiar
relations of the quantum coordinate ring
of
2\times 2
matrices

\displaystyle x_{1,1}x_{1,2}=qx_{1,2}x_{1,1},

\displaystyle x_{1,1}x_{2,1}=qx_{2,1}x_{1,1},

\displaystyle x_{1,2}x_{2,2}=qx_{2,2}x_{1,2},

\displaystyle x_{2,1}x_{2,2}=qx_{2,2}x_{2,1},

\displaystyle x_{1,2}x_{2,1}=x_{2,1}x_{1,2},

\displaystyle x_{1,1}x_{2,2}-x_{2,2}x_{1,1}=(q-q^{-1})x_{1,2}x_{2,1}

(see e.g.
[
21
]
or
[
11
]
*§7.1 with
q=e^{-h}
).
The
q\to 1
limit
of this algebra is
\mathcal{A}_{m}

with the Poisson bracket given by (
6.1
). The coalgebra structure of
\mathcal{A}_{q,m}
is given by

\Delta(x_{i,j})=\sum_{k\in[m]}x_{i,k}\otimes x_{k,j}
,

i,j\in[m]
, while its counital co-quasi-triangular structure

\mathcal{R}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{k}}(\mathcal{A}_{q,m}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{q,m},\mathbb{k})
is defined by

\mathcal{R}(x_{k,l},x_{k^{\prime},l^{\prime}})=q^{\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}}\delta_{k,l}\delta_{k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}+(q-q^{-1})\Upsilon(k-k^{\prime})\delta_{k,l^{\prime}}\delta_{k^{\prime},l},\qquad k,k^{\prime},l,l^{\prime}\in[m].

Let
\mathcal{R}^{(1)}=\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}^{(-1)}=\mathcal{R}^{\ast-1}\circ\tau
(cf. Lemma
2.35
). In particular,

(\mathcal{R}^{(\epsilon)})^{\ast-1}=\mathcal{R}^{(-\epsilon)}\circ\tau
,
\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}
.
It is easy to check
that, for all
k,k^{\prime},l,l^{\prime}\in[m]
,
\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}
,

\mathcal{R}^{(\epsilon)}(x_{k,l},x_{k^{\prime},l^{\prime}})=q_{\epsilon}^{\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}}\delta_{k,l}\delta_{k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}+(q_{\epsilon}-q_{\epsilon}^{-1})\Upsilon(\epsilon(k-k^{\prime}))\delta_{k,l^{\prime}}\delta_{k^{\prime},l},

where
q_{\epsilon}=q^{\epsilon}
.
By (
5.15
), for all

i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}\in[m]
,
\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}
,

\displaystyle\Psi^{(\epsilon)}(x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\otimes x_{i,j})

\displaystyle=q_{\epsilon}^{\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}-\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}}x_{i,j}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}-q_{\epsilon}^{\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}}(q_{\epsilon}-q_{\epsilon}^{-1})\Upsilon(\epsilon(i^{\prime}-i))x_{i^{\prime},j}\otimes x_{i,j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+q_{\epsilon}^{-\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}}(q_{\epsilon}-q_{\epsilon}^{-1})\Upsilon(\epsilon(j-j^{\prime}))x_{i,j^{\prime}}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-(q-q^{-1})^{2}\Upsilon(\epsilon(i^{\prime}-i))\Upsilon(\epsilon(j-j^{\prime}))x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\otimes x_{i,j}.

Given
w\in S_{n}
, let
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w):I_{n}\to\{1,-1\}
be as in Lemma
3.1
and
abbreviate
\bullet_{w}=\bullet_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)}
.
Let, like in §
4.5
and §
5.4
,
x^{(k)}=1^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes x\otimes 1^{(n-k)}
,
x\in\mathcal{A}_{q,m}
,
k\in[n]
.
By Proposition
5.12
,

x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\bullet_{w}x_{i,j}^{(k)}=x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\cdot x_{i,j}^{(k)}
if
k^{\prime}\leq k
, while
for
k^{\prime}>k

\displaystyle x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\bullet_{w}x_{i,j}^{(k)}=q_{k,k^{\prime}}^{\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}-\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}}x_{i,j}^{(k)}\cdot x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}-q_{k,k^{\prime}}^{\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}}(q_{k,k^{\prime}}-q_{k,k^{\prime}}^{-1})\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{k,k^{\prime}}(i^{\prime}-i))x_{i^{\prime},j}^{(k)}\cdot x_{i,j^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}

\displaystyle+q_{k,k^{\prime}}^{-\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}}(q_{k,k^{\prime}}-q_{k,k^{\prime}}^{-1})\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{k,k^{\prime}}(j-j^{\prime}))x_{i,j^{\prime}}^{(k)}\cdot x_{i^{\prime},j}^{(k^{\prime})}

\displaystyle-(q-q^{-1})^{2}\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{k,k^{\prime}}(i^{\prime}-i))\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{k,k^{\prime}}(j-j^{\prime}))x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{(k)}\cdot x_{i,j}^{(k^{\prime})},

where
\cdot
stands for the usual multiplication in
\mathcal{A}_{q,m}{}^{\otimes n}
and
q_{k,k^{\prime}}=q_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{k,k^{\prime}}}
,
(k,k^{\prime})\in I_{n}
.
Thus,

\mathcal{A}_{q,m}^{\otimes n,w}:=\mathcal{A}_{q,m}{}^{\otimes n}
as a coalgebra, is generated, as an algebra, by the
x_{i,j}^{(k)}
,
i,j\in[m]
,
k\in[n]
,
such that for each
k\in[n]
, the subalgebra
generated by the
x_{i,j}^{(k)}
,
i,j\in[m]

is isomorphic to
\mathcal{A}_{q,m}
and

\displaystyle q_{k,k^{\prime}}^{\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}}x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\bullet_{w}x_{i,j}^{(k)}=q_{k,k^{\prime}}^{\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}}x_{i,j}^{(k)}\bullet_{w}x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}-q_{k,k^{\prime}}^{\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}}(q_{k,k^{\prime}}-q_{k,k^{\prime}}^{-1})\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{k,k^{\prime}}(i^{\prime}-i))x_{i^{\prime},j}^{(k)}\bullet_{w}x_{i,j^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}

\displaystyle+(q_{k,k^{\prime}}-q_{k,k^{\prime}}^{-1})\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{k,k^{\prime}}(j-j^{\prime}))x_{i,j^{\prime}}^{(k)}\bullet_{w}x_{i^{\prime},j}^{(k^{\prime})}

\displaystyle-(q-q^{-1})^{2}\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{k,k^{\prime}}(i^{\prime}-i))\Upsilon(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}(w)_{k,k^{\prime}}(j-j^{\prime}))x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{(k)}\bullet_{w}x_{i,j}^{(k^{\prime})},\quad(k,k^{\prime})\in I_{n},

for all
i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}\in[m]
. Unlike in the classical case
described §
6.1
, it is quite an exercise to verify directly that the assignments
x_{i,j}^{(k)}\mapsto x_{i,j}
,
i,j\in[m]
,
k\in[n]
define the homomorphism
of bialgebras
\mathcal{A}_{q,m}^{\otimes n,w}\to\mathcal{A}_{q,m}
provided by Theorem
5.14
.
The Poisson bracket (
6.2
) on
\mathcal{A}_{m}^{\otimes n}

is obtained
as a “dequantization” of
\mathcal{A}_{q,m}^{\otimes n,\id}
. Note also, that
since
\mathcal{A}_{q,m}
has a Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt (PBW) basis, so does
\mathcal{A}_{q,m}^{\otimes n,w}

for any
n\geq 2
and
w\in S_{n}
.

Needless to say, the
q=1
limit of
\mathcal{A}_{q,m}^{\otimes n,w}
is also a Poisson algebra which, as discussed in Remark
6.2
, is isomorphic to
\mathcal{A}_{m}^{\otimes n}
as a Poisson algebra via the natural action of
w
by permutation of factors. However, this no longer yields an isomorphism of algebras
\mathcal{A}_{q,m}^{\otimes n,\id}\to\mathcal{A}_{q,m}^{\otimes n,w}
. Indeed, let
n=2
. By (
5.20
), if

\tau
was an isomorphism of algebras

\mathcal{A}_{q,m}^{\otimes 2,\id}\to\mathcal{A}_{q,m}^{\otimes 2,(1,2)}
,

x^{(k)}\mapsto x^{(3-k)}
,
k\in\{1,2\}
,
x\in\mathcal{A}_{q,m}
, then we would have, for all

i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}\in[m]

\sum_{s,s^{\prime}\in[m]}\mathcal{R}(x_{i,s},x_{i^{\prime},s^{\prime}})x_{s,j}\otimes x_{s^{\prime},j^{\prime}}=\sum_{s,s^{\prime}\in[m]}\mathcal{R}(x_{s,j},x_{s^{\prime},j^{\prime}})x_{i,s}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},s^{\prime}},

which is equivalent to

(q^{\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}}-q^{\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}})x_{i,j}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}=(q-q^{-1})(\Upsilon(j^{\prime}-j)x_{i,j^{\prime}}\otimes x_{i^{\prime},j}-\Upsilon(i-i^{\prime})x_{i^{\prime},j}\otimes x_{i,j^{\prime}}).

Let
i>j\in[m]
and let
i^{\prime}=j
,
j^{\prime}=i
. Then
the above yields

(q-q^{-1})(x_{i,i}\otimes x_{j,j}-x_{j,j}\otimes x_{i,i})=0,

which is a contradiction for
q\not=\pm 1
.

6.2.
Families of classical r-matrices for Takiff
Lie algebras

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a Lie algebra and let
f:{\mathfrak{g}}\to V
be a surjective
homomorphism of
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules. Let
{\mathfrak{t}}={\mathfrak{t}}({\mathfrak{g}},V)=V\rtimes{\mathfrak{g}}
, which is equal to
V\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}
as a vector space with the Lie bracket
defined by
[(v,x),(v^{\prime},x^{\prime})]_{{\mathfrak{t}}}=(xv^{\prime}-x^{\prime}v,[x,x^{\prime}]_{{\mathfrak{g}}})
. The Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{t}}
is a generalization
of the Takiff Lie algebra, which corresponds to the case when
f
is an isomorphism and in turn is isomorphic to the truncated current algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\mathbb{k}[t]/(t^{2})
.
It follows from the definition that
{\mathfrak{g}}
(respectively,
V
) identifies with a subalgebra (respectively, an abelian ideal) of
{\mathfrak{t}}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
be a Lie bialgebra and let
f:{\mathfrak{g}}\to V
be a surjective homomorphism of
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules.
Then
{\mathfrak{t}}=V\rtimes{\mathfrak{g}}
is a Lie bialgebra with

\widehat{\delta}:{\mathfrak{t}}\to{\mathfrak{t}}\otimes{\mathfrak{t}}
defined by

\widehat{\delta}(x)=(f\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes f)\delta(x)
,
\widehat{\delta}(f(x))=(f\otimes f)\delta(x)
,

x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Proof.

Write
\delta(z)=z_{1}\otimes z_{2}
,
z\in{\mathfrak{g}}
in Sweedler-like notation. We have,
for all
x,y\in{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta}([x,y]_{{\mathfrak{t}}})

\displaystyle=(f\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes f)(\delta([x,y]_{{\mathfrak{g}}})=(f\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes f)([\delta(x),\Delta(y)]-[\delta(y),\Delta(x)])

\displaystyle=(f\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes f)([x_{1},y]\otimes x_{2}+x_{1}\otimes[x_{2},y]-[y_{1},x]\otimes y_{2}-y_{1}\otimes[y_{2},x])

\displaystyle=f([x_{1},y])\otimes x_{2}+f(x_{1})\otimes[x_{2},y]-f([y_{1},x])\otimes y_{2}-f(y_{1})\otimes[y_{2},x]

\displaystyle\phantom{-}+[x_{1},y]\otimes f(x_{2})+x_{1}\otimes f([x_{2},y])-[y_{1},x]\otimes f(y_{2})-y_{1}\otimes f([y_{2},x])

\displaystyle=[f(x_{1}),y]\otimes x_{2}+f(x_{1})\otimes[x_{2},y]-[f(y_{1}),x]\otimes y_{2}-f(y_{1})\otimes[y_{2},x]

\displaystyle\phantom{-}+[x_{1},y]\otimes f(x_{2})+x_{1}\otimes[f(x_{2}),y])-[y_{1},x]\otimes f(y_{2})-y_{1}\otimes[f(y_{2}),x])

\displaystyle=[\widehat{\delta}(x),\Delta(y)]-[\widehat{\delta}(y),\Delta(x)],

while

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta}([

\displaystyle x,f(y)]_{{\mathfrak{t}}})=\widehat{\delta}(f([x,y]_{{\mathfrak{g}}}))=(f\otimes f)\delta([x,y]_{{\mathfrak{g}}})=(f\otimes f)([\delta(x),\Delta(y)]-[\delta(y),\Delta(x)])

\displaystyle=f([x_{1},y])\otimes f(x_{2})+f(x_{1})\otimes f([x_{2},y])-f([y_{1},x])\otimes f(y_{2})-f(y_{1})\otimes f([y_{2},x])

\displaystyle=[x_{1},f(y)]\otimes f(x_{2})+f(x_{1})\otimes[x_{2},f(y)]-[f(y_{1}),x]\otimes f(y_{2})-f(y_{1})\otimes[f(y_{2}),x]

\displaystyle=[\widehat{\delta}(x),\Delta(f(y))]-[f(x_{1}),f(y)]\otimes x_{2}-x_{1}\otimes[f(x_{2}),f(y)]-[\widehat{\delta}(f(y)),\Delta(x)]

\displaystyle=[\widehat{\delta}(x),\Delta(f(y))]-[\widehat{\delta}(f(y)),\Delta(x)].

Finally,
\widehat{\delta}([f(x),f(y)]_{{\mathfrak{t}}})=0=[\widehat{\delta}(f(x)),\Delta(f(y))]-[\widehat{\delta}(f(y)),\Delta(f(x))]
for all
x,y\in{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Thus,
\widehat{\delta}
satisfies

(

⁢
L

B
1

)
. The condition
(

⁢
L

B
2

)
is evident. To verify
(

⁢
L

B
3

)
, note that for all
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle(\widehat{\delta}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{t}}})\widehat{\delta}(x)

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\delta}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{t}}})(f(x_{1})\otimes x_{2}+x_{1}\otimes f(x_{2}))

\displaystyle=(f\otimes f\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+f\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes f+\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes f\otimes f)(\delta(x_{1})\otimes x_{2})

\displaystyle=(f\otimes f\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+f\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes f+\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes f\otimes f)\circ(\delta\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})\circ\delta(x),

while

\displaystyle(\widehat{\delta}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{t}}})\widehat{\delta}(f(x))

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\delta}\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{t}}})(f(x_{1})\otimes f(x_{2}))=(f\otimes f\otimes f)\circ(\delta\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}})\circ\delta(x).

It is now immediate that
\widehat{\delta}

satisfies
(

⁢
L

B
3

)
.
∎

Given
r\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
, denote

\widehat{r}:=(f\otimes\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+\id_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes f)(r)

regarded as an element of
{\mathfrak{t}}\otimes{\mathfrak{t}}
.
We will now construct an infinite family of non-equivalent r-matrices for
{\mathfrak{t}}
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a quasi-triangular Lie bialgebra with an r-matrix
r
.
Then

(a)

{\mathfrak{t}}
is a quasi-triangular Lie bialgebra with an r-matrix
\widehat{r}
and
\widehat{\delta_{r}}=\delta_{\widehat{r}}
;
